=== [7:49] 밖에 하나도 안 보이지? ===
meaning: You can't see anything outside, right?
tone: Informal, conversational, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 밖에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 밖 + 에
meaning: outside
notes: '밖' means 'outside' and '에' is a location particle meaning 'at' or 'in'. When '밖에' is used with a negative verb, it takes on the meaning of 'nothing but' or 'only'. However, here it simply means 'at the outside'.
--- word ---
term: 하나도
type: Adverbial Phrase
breakdown: 하나 + 도
meaning: not at all
notes: '하나' means 'one' and '도' means 'even' or 'also'. When combined with a negative predicate (like '안 보여요'), the phrase '하나도' means 'not even one bit' or 'not at all'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: This is the most common way to make a verb negative in spoken Korean. It is placed directly before the verb. This is called the 'short-form negation'.
--- word ---
term: 보이지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 보이다 + -지?
meaning: can't see, right?
notes: The verb is '보이다' (to be seen, to be visible). The '-지?' ending is used in informal speech (반말, banmal) to confirm something the speaker believes is true, similar to adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 하나도 안 + Verb/Adjective
meaning: Used to strongly negate something, meaning 'not at all' or 'not one bit'.
example: 숙제를 하나도 안 했어요. (I didn't do my homework at all.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -지(요)?
meaning: A question ending used to seek confirmation or agreement for something the speaker already assumes to be true.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (The weather is nice today, right?)
level: Beginner

meaning: a lot, immensely
notes: A very common colloquial adverb meaning 'very', 'really', or 'a lot'. It's more informal than alternatives like '아주' or '매우'. The sentence trails off after this word, which is common in spoken language.

notes: The speaker seems to have created a portmanteau of 안개 (angae, fog) and 암막 (ammak, blackout curtain) to describe the thick fog. -이야 (iya) is the informal, non-polite declarative ending (반말, banmal) for nouns ending in a consonant. The polite form would be -이에요 (ieyo).

=== [8:43] 자 하여튼 ===

meaning: Well

breakdown: 
meaning: anyway
notes: Used to change the subject or get back to the main point after a digression. It's often used interchangeably with 아무튼 (amuteun) in casual conversation.

=== [8:44] 자 심심한데 ===
meaning: Well, I'm bored, so...
tone: Informal, setting up a situation, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Well
notes: A common filler word used to start a sentence or transition to a new thought, like 'well' or 'okay'.
--- word ---
term: 심심한데
type: adjective + ending
breakdown: 심심하다 + -ㄴ데
meaning: I'm bored, so...
notes: The -(으)ㄴ데 ending is attached to an adjective stem to provide background context for the following clause. It can imply a soft contrast, reason, or simply set the scene. Here, it explains why she is starting the live stream.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective + -(으)ㄴ데
meaning: Provides background information or sets up a situation for the next clause. Can mean '..., so...', '..., but...', or '...and...'.
example: 날씨가 좋은데 공원에 갈까? (The weather is nice, so shall we go to the park?)

=== [8:48] 이거 다 안개야 ===
meaning: This is all fog.

--- word ---
term: 이거
type: pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: this
notes: 이거 (igeo) is the colloquial, spoken form of 이것 (igeot), meaning 'this thing'. It's used very frequently in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: all
notes: An adverb that means 'all' or 'completely'. It can be placed before verbs, adjectives, or nouns to emphasize completeness.

breakdown: 안개 + -야
meaning: it's fog

=== [8:52] 너무 웃기지? ===
meaning: So funny, right?
tone: Informal, amused, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so
notes: An adverb used for emphasis. While it traditionally meant 'too' (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean, it's very commonly used to mean 'very' or 'so,' similar to 아주 (aju) or 정말 (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 웃기지?
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 웃기다 + -지?
meaning: it's funny, right?
notes: 웃기다 (utgida) means 'to be funny/amusing.' The ending -지? (ji?) is used to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener, similar to adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English. It's a common feature of informal speech (반말, banmal).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -지(요)?
meaning: Used to confirm information the speaker believes is true or to seek the listener's agreement.
example: 오늘 덥지? (It's hot today, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [8:53] 아까까진 그래도 거리라도 보였는데 ===
meaning: A little while ago, you could at least see the street, but...
tone: Informal, contrasting, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 아까까진
type: noun + particle + particle
breakdown: 아까 + -까지 + -는
meaning: until a little while ago
notes: A contraction of 아까까지는 (akkakkajineun). 아까 (akka) means 'a short while ago.' -까지 (kkaji) means 'until.' The topic particle -는 (neun) emphasizes the time frame as a point of contrast with the present.
--- word ---
term: 그래도
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: still
notes: Means 'still', 'nevertheless', or 'even so'. It's used to introduce a statement that contrasts with what might be expected from the previous context.
--- word ---
term: 거리라도
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 거리 + -(이)라도
meaning: at least the street
notes: The particle -(이)라도 (irado) is attached to a noun to mean 'at least' or 'even just,' suggesting it's not the best option but is better than nothing. For example, '물이라도 마셔' (Drink at least some water).
--- word ---
term: 보였는데
type: verb + ending
breakdown: 보이다 + -었- + -는데
meaning: it was visible, but...
notes: 보이다 (boida) is the passive form of 보다 (boda, to see), meaning 'to be seen'. The -었- (eot) indicates past tense. The -는데 (neunde) ending provides background information that contrasts with the current situation (now, nothing is visible). The sentence trails off, implying the unspoken contrast.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)라도
meaning: Indicates choosing a less-than-ideal but still acceptable option. Means 'at least' or 'even if it's just...'.
example: 밥이 없으면 라면이라도 끓여 먹자. (If there's no rice, let's at least cook and eat some ramen.)
level: Intermediate

=== [8:59] 너무 안 보인다 ===
meaning: You really can't see anything.
tone: Informal, emphatic, descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so
notes: An adverb used for emphasis. In modern spoken Korean, it's frequently used to mean 'very' or 'really,' strengthening the word that follows.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: This is a short-form negator placed directly before a verb or adjective to make it negative. It's generally used in spoken Korean. The long-form negation would be 보이지 않는다 (boiji anneunda).
--- word ---
term: 보인다
type: verb
breakdown: 보이다 + -ㄴ다
meaning: is visible
notes: 보이다 (boida) is the passive form of 보다 (boda, to see). The -(ㄴ)다 (nda) ending is a plain form declarative ending, often used in casual speech (반말, banmal) when talking to oneself or friends. It gives a slightly narrative or objective feel.

=== [9:02] 오늘 라이브 심상치 않지? ===
meaning: Today's live is something special, isn't it?
tone: Informal, engaging with the audience.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: Can function as both a noun ('Today is my birthday') and an adverb ('I met a friend today'). Here it modifies '라이브' (live), acting like an adjective.
--- word ---
term: 라이브
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: live broadcast
notes: A Konglish (Korean-style English) word from 'live'. It's commonly used to refer to live streams or live performances.
--- word ---
term: 심상치
type: adjective stem
breakdown: 심상하다 + ~지 않다
meaning: unusual, not ordinary
notes: The adjective '심상하다' (simsanghada) means 'to be ordinary/usual'. It is almost exclusively used in its negative form, '심상치 않다' (simsangchi anta), to mean 'unusual', 'extraordinary', or 'out of the ordinary'. The '~치' is a common contraction of '~하지'.
--- word ---
term: 않지?
type: verb ending
breakdown: 않다 + ~지?
meaning: isn't it?
notes: The '~지?' ending is used in casual speech to confirm something the speaker assumes the listener already knows or agrees with. It's similar to adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English and creates a friendly, inclusive tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A + ~지 않다
meaning: to not be (adjective)
example: 이 옷은 비싸지 않아요. (This clothing is not expensive.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Sentence + ~지?
meaning: ..., right? / ..., isn't it?
example: 날씨 좋지? (The weather's nice, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [9:05] 자 그러면 심심하니까 ===
meaning: Well then, since I'm bored...
tone: Informal, transitional, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: well, now, okay
notes: A common filler word used to get attention, transition to a new topic, or begin an action. It's similar to saying 'Alright then...' or 'Okay...' in English.
--- word ---
term: 그러면
type: adverb
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if so, then
notes: Connects a previous statement to a logical next step or consequence. In casual conversation, it's very often shortened to '그럼'.
--- word ---
term: 심심하니까
type: adjective
breakdown: 심심하다 + ~(으)니까
meaning: because I'm bored
notes: The '~ (으)니까' ending indicates the reason for the following clause. It's often used when the following clause is a suggestion, command, or statement of the speaker's will. It can carry a more subjective nuance than the similar '~아서/어서' ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + ~(으)니까
meaning: because / since (so...)
example: 시간이 없으니까 빨리 가자. (Since we don't have time, let's go quickly.)
level: Beginner

=== [9:09] 일단 부분 한번 해볼까? ===
meaning: First, shall we try doing a part?
tone: Informal, suggestive, thinking aloud.
--- word ---
term: 일단
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: first of all, for now
notes: Indicates the first step in a series of actions or a temporary plan. It has a nuance of 'for the time being' or 'before anything else'.
--- word ---
term: 부분
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: part, section
notes: The usage here is slightly ambiguous. She might be referring to starting a 'segment' of her live stream or a 'part' of her snack haul.
--- word ---
term: 한번
type: adverb
breakdown: 한 (one) + 번 (counter)
meaning: once, one time
notes: When used with a verb, '한번' often softens the meaning to 'to give it a try' or 'to do something (just to see)'. It makes the action sound less committal or serious.
--- word ---
term: 해볼까?
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~아/어 보다 + ~(으)ㄹ까?
meaning: shall we try doing?
notes: A combination of two grammar patterns: '~아/어 보다' (to try doing) and '~(으)ㄹ까?' (shall we...?). This form is used to gently suggest an action and ask for the listener's agreement.

pattern: V + ~아/어 보다
meaning: to try doing something
example: 이 김치를 한번 먹어 보세요. (Try this kimchi.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ까(요)?
meaning: Shall I/we...?
example: 우리 영화 볼까? (Shall we watch a movie?)
level: Beginner

meaning: Let's... (informal)

meaning: honestly, frankly

=== [9:27] 선물은 뭐 하도 많이 해가지고 ===
meaning: As for gifts, well, I've given so many that...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 선물은
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 선물 + ~은
meaning: as for gifts
notes: '선물' means 'gift'. The topic particle '~은/는' is used here to single out 'gifts' as the topic, often implying a contrast with something else (in this case, buying things for herself).
--- word ---
term: 뭐
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: well, you know
notes: A filler word indicating a slight pause or a casual, conversational tone.
--- word ---
term: 하도
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so much, too much
notes: An adverb that emphasizes the excessive degree of an action or state. It's almost always used with a clause of reason (like ~아서/어서) to mean 'so... that...'. It often carries a slightly negative or overwhelming nuance.
--- word ---
term: 많이
type: adverb
breakdown: 많다 + ~이
meaning: a lot
notes: The adverbial form of the adjective '많다' (to be many/much).
--- word ---
term: 해가지고
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: I did, and so...
notes: '~아/어 가지고' is a common spoken grammar pattern that means 'because' or 'after doing... and then'. Here, it indicates the reason: 'because I have given so many gifts...'. It's often used as a colloquial alternative to '~아서/어서'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: because / and then (spoken form)
example: 피곤해가지고 일찍 잤어요. (I was tired, so I slept early.)
level: Intermediate

=== [9:32] 이건 어찌 보면 내가 맛보고 싶어서 산 거 같아 ===
meaning: This one, I think I bought it because I wanted to taste it.
tone: Informal, reflective, explaining her motive.
--- word ---
term: 이건
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 이것 + ~은
meaning: this one (as for this)
notes: A common contraction of '이것은'. '이것' means 'this thing', and the topic particle '~은' sets it apart as the subject of her thought.
--- word ---
term: 어찌
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: in a way, somehow
notes: Often used in the set phrase '어찌 보면', which literally means 'if you look at it somehow' and translates to 'in a way' or 'from a certain perspective'.
--- word ---
term: 보면
type: verb
breakdown: 보다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if you look
notes: Part of the phrase '어찌 보면'. '보다' means 'to see/look' and '~(으)면' is the conditional 'if'.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + ~가
meaning: I (subject)
notes: '나' is the informal pronoun for 'I'. When combined with the subject particle '~가', it becomes '내가'. The formal equivalent is '제가'.
--- word ---
term: 맛보고
type: verb
breakdown: 맛보다 + ~고
meaning: taste and...
notes: '맛보다' is a compound verb from '맛' (taste) + '보다' (to try), meaning 'to taste/sample'. The '~고' connects it to the next part of the phrase, '싶어서'.
--- word ---
term: 싶어서
type: verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 + ~아/어서
meaning: because I wanted to
notes: Combines the desire pattern '~고 싶다' (to want to do) with the reason ending '~아/어서' (because). So, '맛보고 싶어서' means 'because I wanted to taste'.
--- word ---
term: 산
type: verb (modifier)
breakdown: 사다 + ~(으)ㄴ
meaning: (that I) bought
notes: The past-tense modifier form of the verb '사다' (to buy). It modifies the following noun, '거' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun (bound)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing
notes: '거' is the colloquial, spoken form of '것' (thing). It's a dependent noun that requires a modifier before it, as in '산 거' (the thing I bought).
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective
breakdown: 같다 + ~아
meaning: it seems / I think
notes: The informal conjugation of '같다'. The pattern '~(으)ㄴ 것 같다' is a very common way to express an opinion, guess, or impression softly, equivalent to 'I think...' or 'It seems like...'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~고 싶다
meaning: to want to (do something)
example: 한국에 가고 싶어요. (I want to go to Korea.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: It seems/looks like...; I think...
example: 저 사람은 학생인 것 같아. (That person seems to be a student.)
level: Intermediate

=== [9:40] 내가 좋아하는 호카이도 스튜 ===
meaning: The Hokkaido stew that I like.
tone: Informal, presentational. Showing an item she bought.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: This is the informal way to say 'I'. When the informal pronoun 나 (na, 'I') is combined with the subject particle -가 (-ga), it becomes 내가 (naega). The formal equivalent is 제가 (jega), from 저 + 가.
--- word ---
term: 좋아하는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 좋아하다 (to like) + -는 (present tense adnominal ending)
meaning: that (I) like
notes: The -는 ending turns a verb into a descriptive word that can modify a noun. It creates a relative clause in the present tense. For example, '내가 좋아하는 음식' means 'the food that I like'.
--- word ---
term: 호카이도
type: Proper noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Hokkaido
notes: A loanword for Hokkaido, a region in Japan famous for its dairy and food products.
--- word ---
term: 스튜
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: stew
notes: A loanword from the English word 'stew'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 + Noun
meaning: Creates a present-tense relative clause to describe a noun. It means 'the noun that (subject) is verb-ing'.
example: 지금 읽는 책 (the book I'm reading now)
level: Beginner

=== [9:47] 나중에 집에서 만들어 먹으려고 ===
meaning: (I bought this) to make and eat at home later.
tone: Informal, explanatory. The sentence trails off, which is common in speech.
--- word ---
term: 나중에
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: later
notes: Refers to a general time in the future. It's often used for plans that don't have a specific, immediate schedule. Compare with 이따가 (ittaga), which means 'a little later' on the same day.
--- word ---
term: 집에서
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 집 (home) + -에서 (location particle)
meaning: at home
notes: The particle -에서 marks the location where an action takes place. Use -에 to mark a destination (집에 가다 - to go home) or a static location (집에 있다 - to be at home).
--- word ---
term: 만들어
type: Verb
breakdown: 만들다 (to make) + -어 (connective ending)
meaning: make and...
notes: The -아/어 ending connects two clauses sequentially. Here, it connects 'making' and 'eating'. The full form is 만들어서, but the -서 is often dropped in speech. It implies the first action enables the second.
--- word ---
term: 먹으려고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 먹다 (to eat) + -(으)려고
meaning: in order to eat
notes: This ending expresses the speaker's intention or purpose for doing an action. The sentence is incomplete; the implied full sentence is '먹으려고 샀어' (I bought it to eat).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -(으)려고
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or plan. It translates to 'in order to (verb)' or 'planning to (verb)'.
example: 영화를 보려고 극장에 갔어요. (I went to the theater to watch a movie.)
level: Intermediate

=== [9:54] 어떻게 다 가져가 ===

tone: Informal, rhetorical, slightly overwhelmed.
--- word ---
term: 어떻게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: how
notes: A question word meaning 'how'. It can also be used as an exclamation of frustration or despair, similar to 'What should I do?!' or 'How could this happen?!'. Here, it's a rhetorical question expressing worry.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: all, everything
notes: A versatile adverb that can mean 'all' or 'completely'. It can modify verbs (다 먹었다 - I ate it all) or nouns (사람들 다 왔어 - All the people came).
--- word ---
term: 가져가
type: Verb
breakdown: 가져가다 (to take something away)
meaning: take (it)
notes: A compound verb from 가지다 (to have/possess) + 가다 (to go). In casual speech (반말), the dictionary form of a verb can be used as a statement or question.

=== [10:05] 브라질 청이가 있군 치아무 ===
meaning: Oh, there's a Brazilian Twit (fan). 'Te amo'.

term: 브라질

breakdown:

breakdown: 있다 (to exist) + -군
meaning: Oh, there is!
notes: The -군(요) ending expresses mild surprise or realization upon learning something new. The -요 is dropped for informal speech (반말). It's like saying 'Oh, so that's how it is!' or 'I see that...'
--- word ---
term: 치아무
type: Romanization
breakdown: 
meaning: 'Te amo'
notes: This is not a Korean word, but the speaker's Korean-style pronunciation of 'Te amo' (I love you in Portuguese/Spanish), likely in response to the fan's comment.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -군(요)
meaning: An exclamatory ending used to express a new realization or mild surprise about a fact or situation.
example: 비가 오는군요. (Oh, I see it's raining.)
level: Intermediate

=== [10:11] 자 일단 아까 택시에서 오는 길에 ===

--- word ---
term: 자
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Okay, so...
notes: A common filler word used to start a new topic, get someone's attention, or transition. Similar to 'Alright,' 'Well,' or 'Now...' in English.
--- word ---
term: 일단
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: first of all, for now
notes: Used to indicate the first thing that will be said or done. It sets a priority or starting point.
--- word ---
term: 아까
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a little while ago
notes: Refers to the recent past, usually within the same day. It's distinct from 어제 (eoje, yesterday) and 방금 (banggeum, just now/a moment ago).
--- word ---
term: 택시에서
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 택시 (taxi) + -에서 (location particle)
meaning: in the taxi
notes: The particle -에서 marks the location where an action occurs.
--- word ---
term: 오는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + -는 (present tense adnominal ending)
meaning: coming
notes: Modifies the noun 길 (gil, way), creating the phrase 'the way of coming'.
--- word ---
term: 길에
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 길 (way, road) + -에 (time/location particle)
meaning: on the way
notes: When combined with a verb like in '오는 길에', it means 'while on the way coming'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -는 길에
meaning: Indicates that something happened while in the process of going somewhere. It means 'on the way (to/from)'.
example: 집에 오는 길에 빵을 샀어요. (I bought bread on my way home.)
level: Intermediate

=== [10:17] 이거 뭔지 알지? ===

term: 이거

breakdown: 이것 (colloquial form)

--- word ---
term: 뭔지
type: Pronoun + verb ending (contracted)

meaning: what it is
notes: A contraction of 무엇인지. The -(으)ㄴ/는지 grammar pattern is used to embed a question ('What is it?') into a larger sentence ('Do you know...?').
--- word ---
term: 알지
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 알다 (to know) + -지(요)?
meaning: you know, right?
notes: The -지(요)? ending is a tag question used to confirm information the speaker assumes the listener already knows. In informal speech (반말), the -요 is dropped. This phrase is a very common verbal tic to create a sense of shared knowledge.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun/Question Word + -(이)ㄴ지 알다/모르다
meaning: Used to form an embedded question, meaning 'to know/not know what/who/where (etc.) something is'.

=== [10:40] 그림이 너무 맛있게 생긴 거야 ===
meaning: The picture just looks so delicious.
tone: Informal, descriptive, enthusiastic.
--- word ---
term: 그림이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 그림 + 이
meaning: the picture (subject)
notes: '그림' (geurim) means 'picture' or 'drawing'. The particle '이' (i) is the subject marker, attached to nouns ending in a consonant to show that 'the picture' is performing the action or is the subject of the description.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: Traditionally, '너무' (neomu) meant 'too much' in a negative sense. However, in modern colloquial Korean, it is widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in positive contexts as well, like in this sentence.
--- word ---
term: 맛있게
type: adverb
breakdown: 맛있다 + -게
meaning: deliciously
notes: The '-게' (-ge) suffix is attached to an adjective or verb stem to turn it into an adverb, similar to adding '-ly' in English. Here, '맛있다' (mashitda, 'to be delicious') becomes '맛있게' (mashitge, 'deliciously'), describing *how* it looks.
--- word ---
term: 생긴
type: verb
breakdown: 생기다 + -ㄴ
meaning: that looks
notes: '생기다' (saenggida) means 'to be formed' or 'to look (a certain way)'. The adjectival ending '-ㄴ' (-n) modifies the following noun, '거' (geo), so '맛있게 생긴' means 'that looks delicious'.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: noun + copula
breakdown: 것 + 이야
meaning: it is the thing that...
notes: '거' (geo) is a contraction of '것' (geot, 'thing'). The '-는 거야' (-neun geoya) sentence ending is very common in casual speech for stating a fact or observation with a slightly explanatory or emphatic tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective + -게 생기다
meaning: A pattern used to describe the appearance of someone or something. It literally means 'to be formed/appear in a ... way'.
example: 그 케이크는 예쁘게 생겼어. (That cake looks pretty.)
level: Beginner

=== [10:50] 아 라떼 먹지도 않는데 ===
meaning: Ah, I don't even drink lattes.
tone: Informal, self-reproachful. Realizing a pointless purchase.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common exclamation used to express a wide range of emotions like surprise, realization, or frustration. Here, it signals a sudden, slightly regretful thought.
--- word ---
term: 라떼
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Latte
notes: A loanword from English for 'latte'. Many coffee and cafe-related terms in Korean are loanwords (e.g., 아메리카노 - Americano, 카페 - cafe).
--- word ---
term: 먹지도
type: Verb + Particle
breakdown: 먹다 + ~지 + ~도
meaning: even drink
notes: The particle `~도` adds the meaning of 'even' or 'also'. It is attached to a verb stem via the connector `~지` to create an emphatic negative statement when followed by a negative verb like 않다.
--- word ---
term: 않는데
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 않다 + ~는데
meaning: don't, but...
notes: A combination of the negative auxiliary verb `않다` (to not do) and the connective ending `~는데`. `~는데` is used to provide background information or context, and often leaves the sentence trailing off, implying a contrast or a following thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-지도 않다
meaning: Used to express 'to not even do V'. It's a way to form an emphatic negation, stressing that not even the most basic action was performed.

--- word ---
term: 두
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: two
notes: The native Korean number used before a counter noun. For 'two items', you say '두 개' (du gae), not '이 개' (i gae) which uses the Sino-Korean number.
--- word ---
term: 개를
type: Counter Noun + Particle
breakdown: 개 + ~를
meaning: items (object)
notes: `개` (gae) is the most common, general-purpose counter for inanimate objects. `~를` is the object particle, marking 'two items' as the direct object of the verb 'bought'.

term: 샀네
type: Verb
breakdown: 사다 + ~았~ + ~네
meaning: I bought (I realize)
notes: The `~네` ending is used in informal speech to express a new realization, mild surprise, or an observation made in the moment. It often feels like the speaker is thinking out loud.

=== [10:59] 그럴까? ===

=== [11:55] 팩이 이렇게 흘러내리나보네 ===
meaning: I guess the face mask is dripping down like this.
tone: Informal, observational, making a guess.
--- word ---
term: 팩이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 팩 + 이
meaning: The face mask (subject)
notes: 팩 (paek) is a loanword from the English 'pack,' commonly used to mean a sheet face mask. 이 (i) is the subject-marking particle used after nouns that end in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this
notes: 이렇게 (ireoke) is a contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage). It describes an action or state that is happening in the way the speaker is demonstrating or currently seeing. It's part of the set: 이렇게 (like this), 그렇게 (like that), 저렇게 (like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 흘러내리나보네
type: Verb
breakdown: 흘러내리다 + -나 보다 + -네
meaning: I guess it's dripping down
notes: A combination of two endings. -나 보다 expresses a guess based on observation ('it seems/looks like'). -네 is an informal sentence ender expressing mild surprise or realization upon observing something directly. Together, it shows the speaker noticing the mask dripping and making a guess about it in the moment.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -나 보다
meaning: Expresses a guess or assumption based on observation ('it seems like...'). Used with action verbs. For descriptive verbs (adjectives), -(으)ㄴ가 보다 is used.
example: 밖에 비가 오나 봐요. (It seems like it's raining outside.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -네(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to express mild surprise, realization, or admiration upon directly experiencing or observing something.
example: 이 케이크 정말 맛있네! (Wow, this cake is really delicious!)
level: Beginner

--- grammar ---
pattern: (Noun) + -(이)래
meaning: A colloquial contraction of -(이)라고 해, used for informal reported speech. It means 'they say it is (Noun)' or 'it's called (Noun).'
example: 저 사람이 내 친구래. (They say that person is my friend.)
level: Intermediate

=== [12:52] 브라질 공연 끝나고 나니까 ===
meaning: After the Brazil concert ended...
tone: Narrative, setting up a story.
--- word ---
term: 브라질
type: Noun (Proper)
breakdown: 
meaning: Brazil
notes: A loanword from English.
--- word ---
term: 공연
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Performance, concert
notes: Refers to a public performance like a concert, play, or musical.
--- word ---
term: 끝나고
type: Verb
breakdown: 끝나다 + -고
meaning: ended and then...
notes: 끝나다 (kkeunnada) means 'to end' or 'to be over.' The -고 (-go) connector links sequential actions. 'A-고 B' means 'A happens, and then B happens.'
--- word ---
term: 나니까
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 나다 + -니까
meaning: when it happened...
notes: This is part of the grammar pattern V-고 나니까. The -니까 (-nikka) ending indicates a reason or the discovery of a new situation after the preceding action is completed.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 나니까
meaning: Expresses that after completing an action (V), the speaker realized or discovered something new. It connects the completion of an action to a resulting situation or feeling.
example: 운동하고 나니까 기분이 상쾌해졌어요. (After I exercised, I felt refreshed.)
level: Intermediate

=== [12:59] 입맛이 터졌더라구 ===
meaning: My appetite just exploded.
tone: Expressive, informal, anecdotal.
--- word ---
term: 입맛이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 입맛 + 이
meaning: Appetite (subject)
notes: 입맛 (immat) literally means 'mouth taste' but is used to mean 'appetite.' A common phrase is '입맛이 없다' (immasi eopda), meaning 'to have no appetite.'
--- word ---
term: 터졌더라구
type: Verb
breakdown: 터지다 + -었- + -더라고
meaning: it had exploded
notes: 터지다 (teojida) means 'to burst' or 'explode.' It's used idiomatically here to mean her appetite suddenly appeared in a big way. The ending -더라고 (-deorago), often written colloquially as -더라구 (-deoragu), is used to state a fact the speaker personally observed or experienced in the past, adding a sense of recollection.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -더라고(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to express a past observation or experience that the speaker is recalling. It conveys that the speaker directly saw, heard, or felt something.
example: 어제 그 식당에 가니까 사람이 많더라고요. (When I went to that restaurant yesterday, I saw that it was crowded.)
level: Intermediate

=== [13:01] 계속 먹고 있어 ===
meaning: I keep eating.
tone: Casual, describing a current, ongoing action.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Continuously, still
notes: 계속 (gyesok) is an adverb that means an action or state is ongoing without stopping.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb stem
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: eating
notes: This is the verb stem 먹다 (meokda), 'to eat,' connected to the -고 있다 (-go itda) present progressive pattern.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Auxiliary verb
breakdown: 있다 + -어
meaning: am
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말 - banmal) conjugation of 있다 (itda). It completes the present progressive tense, indicating an action in progress.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 있다
meaning: The present progressive tense, equivalent to 'be + V-ing' in English. It describes an action that is currently in progress.
example: 지금 책을 읽고 있어요. (I am reading a book now.)
level: Beginner

=== [13:03] 오 이렇게 작은 포장이 돼 있는 걸? ===
meaning: Oh, it's packaged in small ones like this?
tone: Surprised, curious, observational.
--- word ---
term: 오
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Oh
notes: A common exclamation of mild surprise or realization.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this
notes: Describes the manner in which something is done, as seen by the speaker. It's a contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage).
--- word ---
term: 작은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 작다 + -은
meaning: Small
notes: 작다 (jakda) means 'to be small.' The -은 (-eun) ending is the adjectival form that allows it to modify the following noun, 포장 (pojang).
--- word ---
term: 포장이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 포장 + 이
meaning: Packaging (subject)
notes: 포장 (pojang) means 'packaging' or 'wrapping.' The subject particle 이 (i) is used because the noun ends in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 + -어
meaning: is done
notes: 돼 (dwae) is a contraction of 되어 (doeeo). 되다 (doeda) means 'to become' or 'to be done.' Here it's used in a passive sense: 'the packaging is done.'
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -는
meaning: that is
notes: Part of the -어 있다 pattern, describing a continuing state. The -는 (-neun) ending turns the entire verb phrase '돼 있다' (to be in a state of being done) into a modifier for the following noun.
--- word ---
term: 걸?
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 것 + 을
meaning: ...is the thing?
notes: 걸 (geol) is a colloquial contraction of 것을 (geoseul). At the end of a sentence, -(으)ㄴ/는 걸? functions as a sentence ending expressing mild surprise or asking for confirmation about a newly discovered fact. It's softer than a direct question.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어 있다
meaning: Indicates a state that continues after an action has been completed. It's different from V-고 있다 (action in progress). For example, '문이 열려 있어요' (The door is open) means the action of opening is done, and it remains in that state.
example: 벽에 그림이 걸려 있어요. (A picture is hanging on the wall.)
level: Intermediate

=== [13:06] 그러면 가져가기 쉽겠다 이거는 ===
meaning: Then this will be easy to take with me.
tone: Informal, observational, assessing an item's portability.
--- word ---
term: 그러면
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Then / In that case
notes: A common connective word that links a previous statement to a logical conclusion or next step. It's a contraction of '그렇게 하면' (if you do it that way).
--- word ---
term: 가져가기
type: Noun
breakdown: 가져가다 + -기
meaning: taking (something)
notes: The verb stem '가져가다' (to take/carry) is turned into a noun by adding the nominalizing suffix -기. This is similar to using a gerund (-ing) in English. It's often used in the pattern 'V-기 쉽다/어렵다' (easy/difficult to do V).
--- word ---
term: 쉽겠다
type: Verb
breakdown: 쉽다 + -겠- + -다
meaning: it would be easy / it seems easy
notes: The suffix -겠- expresses supposition or a guess about a future state. It softens the statement to 'I guess it will be easy' rather than a direct 'It will be easy'. The -다 ending is the plain form, often used in casual speech when talking to oneself.
--- word ---
term: 이거는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이거 + -는
meaning: this (as for this)
notes: 이거 is the casual, spoken form of 이것 (this thing). The topic particle -는 sets 'this thing' as the topic of the sentence, often for emphasis or contrast. The full form would be 이것은.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-기 쉽다/어렵다
meaning: Expresses that doing a certain action (verb) is easy or difficult.
example: 한국어는 배우기 어려워요. (Korean is difficult to learn.)
level: Beginner

=== [13:19] 동일 인물이야 ===
meaning: It's the same person.
tone: Informal, declarative, likely comparing her past and present self.
--- word ---
term: 동일
type: Noun
breakdown: 동일 (同一)
meaning: same / identical
notes: A Sino-Korean word (from Hanja characters 同 'same' and 一 'one'). It's a slightly more formal or emphatic way to say 'same' compared to the pure Korean word '같은'.

term: 인물이야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 인물 + -이야
meaning: is a person
notes: 인물 (人物) means 'person' or 'figure'. '-이야' is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of the verb '이다' (to be), used after a noun ending in a consonant. The polite equivalent would be '인물이에요'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)야
meaning: The informal, non-polite (반말) form of the copula '이다' (to be). Use '-이야' after nouns ending in a consonant and '-야' after nouns ending in a vowel.

=== [13:21] 지금 지난주에 브라질에 왔는데 ===
meaning: I came to Brazil last week, and...
tone: Informal, narrative, setting up a contrast or story.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: While '지금' means 'now', speakers often use it colloquially to ground a story in the present moment, even when talking about the past. It adds a sense of immediacy to the narrative.
--- word ---
term: 지난주에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 지난주 + -에
meaning: last week
notes: 지난 means 'last' or 'past', and 주 means 'week'. The particle -에 is used to mark a specific point in time when an action occurred.
--- word ---
term: 브라질에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 브라질 + -에
meaning: to Brazil
notes: The particle -에 marks a destination of movement. It answers the question 'where to?'.
--- word ---
term: 왔는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -았- + -는데
meaning: came, but... / came, and...
notes: The -는데 ending is a very common connective in spoken Korean. It provides background information for the next clause, often implying a contrast or an unexpected situation. It can be translated as '..., and...' or '..., but...' depending on the context.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -는데 / -(으)ㄴ데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background or context for the main clause. It can also show a soft contrast or reason.
example: 어제 숙제했는데, 오늘 또 있어요? (I did homework yesterday, but there's more today?)
level: Intermediate

=== [13:22] 지금 지난주에 브라질에서 날아다닌 사람이랑 ===
meaning: the person who was flying around in Brazil last week and...
tone: Informal, descriptive, trailing off to build a comparison.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: Colloquially used to add immediacy to a story about the past. It's a speech habit that makes the narrative feel more present.
--- word ---
term: 지난주에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 지난주 + -에
meaning: last week
notes: The time-marking particle -에 specifies when the action of 'flying around' happened.
--- word ---
term: 브라질에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 브라질 + -에서
meaning: in Brazil
notes: The particle -에서 marks the location where an action takes place. Contrast with -에, which marks a static location or destination. Here, the action of 'flying around' happens within Brazil.
--- word ---
term: 날아다닌
type: Verb
breakdown: 날아다니다 + -ㄴ
meaning: who was flying around
notes: 날아다니다 is a compound verb from 날다 (to fly) and 다니다 (to go around). It implies being very busy and moving from place to place. The -ㄴ ending makes it a past-tense adjective modifying the next noun, '사람' (person).
--- word ---
term: 사람이랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사람 + -이랑
meaning: with the person / and the person
notes: -이랑 is a colloquial particle meaning 'and' or 'with', used after a noun ending in a consonant (use -랑 for vowels). It's the informal equivalent of -과/와 or -하고. The sentence is incomplete, suggesting a comparison is coming.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄴ + Noun
meaning: Creates a relative clause in the past tense. The verb describes an action that the noun has already completed.
example: 어제 먹은 빵 (the bread I ate yesterday)
level: Intermediate

meaning: eating / who is eating

type: noun + particle
breakdown: 사람 + -이

meaning: it is..., right?

meaning: is delicious
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) form of '맛있다' (to be delicious). It is a compound of '맛' (mat, taste) and '있다' (itta, to have/exist).

=== [14:09] 음 그리고 이 과자의 너무나도 좋은 점 ===
meaning: Mm, and a very, very good thing about this snack...
tone: Informal, transitional, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 음
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Mm, Hmm
notes: A sound made while thinking or transitioning to a new point.
--- word ---
term: 그리고
type: conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: and, and then
notes: A basic conjunction used to connect sentences, clauses, or ideas.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: this
notes: Used before a noun to specify something near the speaker.
--- word ---
term: 과자의
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 과자 + -의
meaning: of the snack / the snack's
notes: The particle '-의' indicates possession or attribution, similar to 'of' or 's in English. In casual speech, its pronunciation is often simplified to [에] (e).
--- word ---
term: 너무나도
type: adverb
breakdown: 너무 + -나 + -도
meaning: so very, extremely
notes: An emphasized form of '너무' (so/too). The particles '-나' and '-도' are added for extra emphasis, highlighting the degree of the following adjective.
--- word ---
term: 좋은
type: adjective
breakdown: 좋다 + -은
meaning: good
notes: The adjectival form of '좋다' (to be good), used to modify a noun. The '-은' ending is used to connect an adjective stem ending in a consonant to the noun it describes.
--- word ---
term: 점
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: point, aspect
notes: Here, it means a 'point' or 'thing' in the sense of a characteristic or feature. '좋은 점' means 'a good point' or 'a good thing about it'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective-(으)ㄴ Noun
meaning: The standard way to make an adjective describe a noun. Add '-ㄴ' to stems ending in a vowel (e.g., 예쁘다 -> 예쁜) and '-은' to stems ending in a consonant (e.g., 좋다 -> 좋은).
example: 작은 가방 (jageun gabang) - a small bag
level: Beginner

=== [14:12] 알갱이가 딱 좀 소분으로 포장돼 있으니까 ===
meaning: Since the little pieces are perfectly portioned out in small packages...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 알갱이가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 알갱이 + -가
meaning: the little pieces / grains
notes: 알갱이 refers to a small grain, pellet, or individual piece of something. The particle -가 marks it as the subject of the clause. -가 is used after nouns ending in a vowel, while -이 is used for nouns ending in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: perfectly, exactly
notes: 딱 is a common adverb emphasizing that something fits perfectly, happens at the exact right time, or is precisely correct. It adds a sense of satisfaction.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조금 -> 좀
meaning: a bit, kind of
notes: 좀 is a contraction of 조금 (a little). In colloquial speech, it's often used to soften a statement or make a request sound gentler, rather than literally meaning 'a small amount'.
--- word ---
term: 소분으로
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 소분 + -으로
meaning: in small portions
notes: 소분 (小分) means 'dividing into small portions'. The particle -(으)로 indicates the means or method, so this means 'packaged by means of small portions'.
--- word ---
term: 포장돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 포장되다 -> 포장되어 -> 포장돼
meaning: is packaged
notes: This is the passive form of 포장하다 (to package). 포장되다 means 'to be packaged'. The form 포장돼 is a common contraction of 포장되어, which is used in compound verb constructions.
--- word ---
term: 있으니까
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 있다 + -(으)니까
meaning: because it is
notes: The pattern V-아/어 있다 expresses a continuing state. The ending -(으)니까 indicates a reason or cause that the speaker has realized or wants to emphasize. It's often used to justify a subsequent statement or action.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 있다
meaning: Expresses a continuing state that resulted from a completed action. It means 'is in the state of V-ing'.
example: 문이 열려 있어요. (The door is open.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-(으)니까
meaning: A connective ending that indicates the reason or cause for the following clause. It often implies the speaker's personal discovery of that reason.
example: 비가 오니까 우산을 가져가세요. (Since it's raining, take an umbrella.)
level: Beginner

=== [14:42] 이거 너무 맛있는데? ===

meaning: A sentence ending used to express surprise, admiration, or a gentle contradiction. It can also be used to provide background information before asking a question or making a suggestion.

=== [14:48] 언니도 갖다 줘야지 ===
meaning: I have to give some to my sister, too.
tone: Informal, self-talk, expressing an intention.
--- word ---
term: 언니도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 (older sister) + -도 (too, also)
meaning: my older sister, too
notes: 언니 (eonni) is the term a female uses for her older sister or a close older female friend. The story bible confirms the speaker has two older sisters. The particle -도 (do) means 'also' or 'too' and is attached directly to the noun.
--- word ---
term: 갖다
type: Verb
breakdown: 가져다주다 (to take and give) -> 갖다주다 (contraction)
meaning: take and give
notes: 갖다 (gatda) is a common colloquial contraction of 가져다 (gajeoda). The full verb is 가져다주다 (gajeodajuda), which combines 가지다 (to have/take) and 주다 (to give). The full phrase here would be 갖다 줘야지 (gatda jwoyaji).
--- word ---
term: 줘야지
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 주다 (to give) + -어야지 (should/must do)
meaning: I should give
notes: The -아/어야지 (-a/eoyaji) ending expresses a personal intention or a sense of what one 'should' or 'must' do. It's often used when talking to oneself, like making a mental note. It's a casual, informal ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어야지
meaning: Expresses intention, will, or a sense of obligation, often used when talking to oneself. It implies that the action is something one naturally should or must do.
example: 이제 공부해야지. (I should study now.)
level: Intermediate

=== [14:56] 그다음은 바닐라 쿠키인 것 같아요 ===

breakdown: 그다음 (next) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: As for the next one
notes: 그다음 (geudaeum) means 'the next thing' in a sequence. The particle -은/는 (-eun/neun) marks the noun as the topic of the sentence, indicating what the speaker is about to talk about.

breakdown: 
meaning: vanilla
notes: A loanword from the English word 'vanilla'.

type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 쿠키 (cookie) + 이다 (to be) + -ㄴ (modifier form)
meaning: that is a cookie
notes: This is part of the '-(으)ㄴ 것 같다' pattern. The noun 쿠키 (cookie) is combined with the copula 이다 (to be), which is then conjugated with -ㄴ to modify the following noun, 것 (thing).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, fact
notes: 것 (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact'. It's used in many grammar patterns to turn a verb or adjective phrase into a noun clause.
--- word ---
term: 같아요

breakdown: 같다 (to be like, to seem) + -아요 (polite ending)
meaning: it seems
notes: The speaker uses the polite -요 (yo) ending here, even though she mostly speaks in informal 반말 (banmal). This is common for streamers and can soften a statement or be a simple habit. The base form is 같다 (gatda).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)ㄴ 것 같다
meaning: Used to express a guess or supposition about a noun. It translates to 'It seems to be (Noun)' or 'I think it's a (Noun)'.
example: 저 사람은 학생인 것 같아요. (That person seems to be a student.)

=== [15:18] 이렇게 박스에 테이프 붙여져 있으면 너무 싫지 않아? ===
meaning: When tape is stuck on a box like this, isn't it so annoying?
tone: Rhetorical, complaining, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: like this / in this way
notes: 이렇게 (ireoke) is an adverb formed from the descriptive verb 이렇다 (ireota), meaning 'to be like this'. It describes how an action is done.
--- word ---
term: 박스에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 박스 (box) + -에 (location particle)
meaning: on the box
notes: 박스 (bakseu) is a loanword from 'box'. The particle -에 (e) indicates a static location where something exists.
--- word ---
term: 테이프
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: tape
notes: The subject particle -가 (ga) is omitted after 테이프 (teipeu), which is very common in casual speech. The full form would be 테이프가 (teipeuga).
--- word ---
term: 붙여져
type: Verb (passive)
breakdown: 붙다 (to stick) -> 붙이다 (to attach) -> 붙여지다 (to be attached)
meaning: is stuck on
notes: This is the passive form. 붙여지다 (buchyeojida) means 'to be attached/stuck'. It's part of the V-아/어 있다 pattern, describing a resulting state.
--- word ---
term: 있으면
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 있다 (to be) + -(으)면 (if/when)
meaning: if it is / when it is
notes: The -(으)면 (-eumyeon) ending creates a conditional clause. The full phrase 붙여져 있으면 (buchyeojyeo isseumyeon) means 'if it is in a state of being stuck on'.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / too
notes: In modern colloquial Korean, 너무 (neomu) is used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative sentences, although traditionally it was used only for negative excess ('too much').
--- word ---
term: 싫지
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 싫다 (to dislike) + -지 (question tag)
meaning: dislike it, right?
notes: 싫다 (silta) means 'to be undesirable' or 'to dislike'. The -지 (ji) ending is a tag question that seeks agreement, implying the speaker is sure the listener feels the same way.
--- word ---
term: 않아?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 않다 (to not be) + -아? (informal question)
meaning: isn't it?
notes: The pattern -지 않아? (-ji ana?) is a negative question tag used to emphatically seek agreement. It's stronger than just -지? and translates well to 'Don't you think so?' or 'Isn't it...?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-지 않아(요)?
meaning: A negative question tag used to seek agreement from the listener, implying the speaker strongly believes something to be true. 'Isn't it...?' or 'Don't you think...?'
example: 날씨가 좋지 않아요? (Isn't the weather nice?)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 있다
meaning: Describes a state that continues after an action is completed. It is often used with passive verbs. '붙여져 있다' means 'is in a state of being stuck on'.
example: 창문이 닫혀 있어요. (The window is closed.)
level: Intermediate

=== [15:22] 그래서 이거 떼다가 손에 끈끈히 묻고 손톱에 ===
meaning: So while taking this off, it gets all sticky on my hands and fingernails.
tone: Informal, descriptive, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: A common conjunction used to link a cause or reason to a result. It's used in both spoken and written Korean.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: This thing
notes: A colloquial contraction of 이것 (igeot), meaning 'this thing'. In spoken Korean, 이거 (igeo), 그거 (geugeo), and 저거 (jeogeo) are used far more frequently than their full forms.
--- word ---
term: 떼다가
type: Verb
breakdown: 떼다 + -다가
meaning: while peeling/taking off
notes: The grammar pattern '-다가' indicates that an action (떼다 - to peel) is interrupted by another action (묻다 - to get on). It implies a shift from one action or state to another.
--- word ---
term: 손에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 손 + -에
meaning: on the hand
notes: The particle '-에' marks a static location where something exists or happens. Here, it indicates the stickiness is 'on' the hand.
--- word ---
term: 끈끈히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 끈끈하다 + -히
meaning: stickily
notes: An adverb formed by adding '-히' to the adjective stem '끈끈하-'. This is a common way to form adverbs from 하다 adjectives.
--- word ---
term: 묻고
type: Verb
breakdown: 묻다 + -고
meaning: gets on and...
notes: The verb 묻다 (mutda) means 'to be stained with' or 'to get on'. The connective ending '-고' links this clause to the next, meaning 'and then' or 'and'.
--- word ---
term: 손톱에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 손톱 + -에
meaning: on the fingernail(s)
notes: The sentence trails off here, which is common in casual speech. The listener is expected to understand the implied conclusion: '...it gets sticky on my fingernails, too.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -다가
meaning: Indicates an interruption or shift of action. The subject is doing one thing, and then something else happens or they switch to another action.
example: 집에 가다가 친구를 만났어요. (I met a friend while I was going home.)
level: Intermediate

=== [15:28] 근데 그건 또 잘 안 떨어지잖아 ===

--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well, easily
notes: When used with a negative verb like '안 떨어지다', '잘 안' means 'doesn't... well' or 'doesn't... easily'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: This is the short-form negation, placed directly before a verb or adjective. It's more common in spoken Korean than the long-form '-지 않다'.
--- word ---
term: 떨어지잖아
type: Verb
breakdown: 떨어지다 + -잖아
meaning: it comes off, you know
notes: 떨어지다 means 'to fall off' or 'to come off'. The ending '-잖아' is used to state a fact that the speaker assumes the listener already knows or agrees with. It creates a sense of shared understanding, like saying '...you know?' or '...right?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -잖아(요)
meaning: Used to state something the listener is likely already aware of, or to gently remind them. It seeks confirmation and builds rapport.
example: 오늘 춥잖아. (It's cold today, you know.)
level: Beginner

=== [16:18] 뭐라고 써져 있는 거야? ===
meaning: What is written here?
tone: Informal, curious, trying to decipher something.
--- word ---
term: 뭐라고
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 뭐 + -(이)라고
meaning: saying what
notes: A combination of 뭐 (what) and the quoting particle -(이)라고. It's used to ask what was said or, in this case, what is written.
--- word ---
term: 써져
type: Verb
breakdown: 써지다
meaning: to be written
notes: This is the passive form of the verb 쓰다 (sseuda), 'to write'. The passive is formed by adding -어지다 to the verb stem. It indicates that the action of writing was done to the subject.
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -는
meaning: that is
notes: The verb 있다 (itda) combined with the present-tense modifying ending -는 turns the preceding phrase '써져 있' into an adjective describing the following noun, '거'.
--- word ---
term: 거야?
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 것 + -이야?
meaning: is it the thing?
notes: 거 (geo) is a contraction of 것 (geot), 'thing'. The pattern '-는 거야?' is a very common informal way to ask a question, often seeking an explanation or expressing curiosity about a situation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuing state that results from a completed action. '써져 있다' means the action of writing is done, and the text now exists in a written state.
example: 문이 열려 있어요. (The door is open.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는 거야?
meaning: An informal and conversational question ending. It's softer than a standard verb ending and often implies the speaker is observing a situation and asking for clarification.
example: 지금 어디 가는 거야? (Where are you going now?)
level: Intermediate

=== [17:46] 이건 왜 이렇게 한... ===
meaning: Why is this one so...

--- word ---
term: 이건
type: Pronoun + Topic particle
breakdown: 이것 (this thing) + 은 (topic particle)
meaning: This one (is)
notes: A common contraction of '이것은'. '이것' means 'this thing,' and '은/는' is the topic particle. In casual speech, contractions like this are the norm.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Why
notes: A fundamental question word used to ask for a reason or cause.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: Like this / So
notes: Describes the manner or degree of an action or state. It can mean 'in this way' or, as here, 'to this extent' or 'so'. It's formed by adding the adverbial suffix -게 to the descriptive verb stem 이렇다.

type: Verb (incomplete)

meaning: done...
notes: This is an incomplete thought. '한' is the past-tense adnominal form of '하다' (to do/be), which would typically modify a noun that the speaker doesn't say (e.g., '이렇게 한 포장' - packaging done like this). The trailing ellipsis shows she's cut herself off.

type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 (to become) + -어 (infinitive ending)
meaning: become
notes: '돼' is a standard contraction of '되어'. This is the infinitive form of the verb '되다' (to become, to be done, to be made). Learners often confuse 되 and 돼; '돼' can replace '되어' or '되어요'.
--- word ---
term: 있다
type: Auxiliary verb
breakdown: 
meaning: is
notes: When combined with another verb in the '-어/아 있다' form, it indicates a continuing state that resulted from a completed action. So, '돼 있다' means 'is in a state of having been made/become'.
--- grammar ---

meaning: Indicates a state that continues after an action is completed. It's often used with passive verbs to describe the state of an object.
example: 창문이 열려 있어요. (The window is open.)

=== [17:59] 아 근데 궁금은 하고 ===
meaning: Ah, but I am curious...
tone: Informal, expressing a conflicting thought, trailing off.

term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common interjection expressing realization, surprise, or acknowledgement.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (but/by the way)
meaning: But / However
notes: '근데' is a contraction of '그런데'. It's one of the most common conjunctions in spoken Korean, used to mean 'but' or to change the subject ('by the way').

notes: Attaching the topic particle '은' to the adjective stem '궁금하-' is a colloquial way to emphasize the feeling of curiosity, often to contrast it with another unstated feeling or fact. It highlights 'curiosity' as the specific topic.
--- word ---
term: 하고
type: Verb + Connective ending
breakdown: 하다 (to do/be) + -고 (connective ending)
meaning: and...
notes: Here, '하고' functions to connect clauses. '궁금은 하고' literally means 'As for curiosity, I do (have it) and...'. The trailing '-고' ending leaves the sentence hanging, implying a contrast: 'I'm curious, *but* [there's a problem]...'. This is a common speech pattern to show hesitation.

breakdown: 
meaning: wrapping paper?
notes: The rising intonation, indicated by the question mark, turns this repeated noun into a question of disbelief, as if to say 'And even more wrapping paper?'.

=== [18:19] 아니잖아 ===
meaning: That's not it, is it?
tone: Informal, corrective, slightly complaining.
--- word ---
term: 아니잖아
type: Verb + Ending
breakdown: 아니다 + -잖아
meaning: it's not...
notes: This combines 아니다 (to not be) with the ending -잖아. The speaker is stating a fact she believes the listener should already know or agree with, like 'Come on, that's not it' or 'As you know, it's not that'.

pattern: -잖아(요)
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to state a reason or fact that the speaker assumes the listener is also aware of. It can be used for reminding, gently correcting, or sometimes showing slight frustration. The -요 is dropped for 반말 (informal speech).

=== [18:22] 어떡해 ===

breakdown: 어떻게 해
meaning: What should I do?
notes: A very common colloquial expression. It's a contraction of 어떻게 해 (literally 'how to do?'). It's often used as an exclamation like 'Oh no!' or 'What am I going to do?'. Learners often confuse it with the adverb 어떻게 (how), but 어떡해 is a complete predicate.

=== [18:35] 먹어볼게 ===
meaning: I'll try eating it.
tone: Informal, declarative, stating an intention.
--- word ---
term: 먹어볼게
type: Verb phrase + Ending
breakdown: 먹다 + -어 보다 + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I will try eating (it)
notes: This combines two grammar patterns. 먹다 (to eat) + -어 보다 (to try doing something) becomes 먹어 보다 (to try eating). Then, the ending -(으)ㄹ게 is added to show the speaker's intention, resulting in 먹어볼게. This is a very natural way to say you're about to try something.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 보다
meaning: This pattern means 'to try doing' or 'to experience' the verb. It's used when attempting an action to see what it's like.
example: 이 옷을 입어 보세요. (Please try on these clothes.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: An informal ending used to express the speaker's intention or will, often as a promise or response to the listener. It has a softer nuance than other future tense forms. The -요 is dropped for 반말 (informal speech).
example: 내가 할게. (I'll do it.)
level: Beginner

=== [19:24] 아니 나는 앰멘탈 치즈 같은 맛을 별로 안 좋아하거든? ===
meaning: No, I don't really like the taste of things like Emmental cheese, you know?
tone: Informal, explanatory, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 아니
type: Interjection/Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: No / I mean
notes: Often used at the beginning of a sentence not just as 'no', but as a conversational filler to correct, clarify, or express slight surprise, similar to 'Well,' or 'I mean...'. It's very common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + 는
meaning: As for me / I
notes: 나 is the informal pronoun for 'I'. The topic marker 는 (neun) is used here to emphasize the subject ('As for me...') in contrast to others, or to state a general fact about oneself.
--- word ---
term: 앰멘탈
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Emmental
notes: A loanword from English for 'Emmental' cheese. Korean often incorporates foreign words, especially for food items, by approximating their sounds.
--- word ---
term: 치즈
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: cheese
notes: A loanword from English for 'cheese'.
--- word ---
term: 같은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + ~는
meaning: like / similar to
notes: This is the adjective 같다 (to be like) in a form that can modify a following noun. It attaches to a noun to mean 'a noun like [the preceding noun]'. For example, '사과 같은 얼굴' (a face like an apple).
--- word ---
term: 맛을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 맛 + 을
meaning: taste (as an object)
notes: 맛 means 'taste' or 'flavor'. The particle 을 (eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb 좋아하다 (to like).
--- word ---
term: 별로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not really / not particularly
notes: This adverb is almost always used in negative sentences with verbs like 안 (an), 못 (mot), or 없다 (eopda). For example, '별로 안 비싸요' (It's not really expensive).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: This is the short-form negator in Korean. It is placed directly before a verb or adjective to make it negative. The long form would be ~지 않다 (e.g., 좋아하지 않다).
--- word ---
term: 좋아하거든?
type: Verb
breakdown: 좋아하다 + ~거든
meaning: I like it, you see?
notes: The ending ~거든 (geodeun) is used to provide a reason or background information for a previous or following statement. With a rising intonation (as indicated by the '?'), it invites the listener's agreement or understanding, like saying '...you know?' or '...you see.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 같은 + Noun
meaning: Used to mean 'a noun like [the first noun]'. It indicates similarity.
example: 연예인 같은 사람 (a person like a celebrity)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: 별로 + Negative Verb
meaning: A common pairing that means 'not really' or 'not particularly'. 별로 is rarely used in positive sentences.
example: 이 영화는 별로 재미없어요. (This movie isn't really fun.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + ~거든(요)
meaning: An ending used to provide a reason or background context. It implies 'because...' or 'you see...'. In conversation, it can create a friendly, explanatory tone.
example: 먼저 갈게. 약속이 있거든. (I'll go first. I have plans, you see.)
level: Intermediate

=== [20:51] 잠을 못 자서 이상한가 보다 ===
meaning: I guess I'm being weird because I couldn't sleep.
tone: Informal, self-deprecating, speculative.
--- word ---
term: 잠을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 잠 + 을
meaning: sleep (as an object)
notes: 잠 is the noun for 'sleep'. It's combined with the object particle 을/를 to indicate that 'sleep' is the object of the verb. The full phrase is 잠을 자다 (jam-eul jada), 'to sleep'.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: 못 (mot) is an adverb used to express inability to do something. It is placed before the verb. It's different from 안 (an), which expresses a lack of will or simple negation. '못 자다' (mot jada) means 'cannot sleep', while '안 자다' (an jada) means 'to not sleep'.
--- word ---
term: 자서
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 + -아서
meaning: because I slept/couldn't sleep
notes: The verb 자다 (jada, 'to sleep') is conjugated with the -아서/어서 (-aseo/eoseo) ending, which indicates reason or cause. It connects two clauses, meaning 'so' or 'because'. Here, it links 'not being able to sleep' to 'being weird'.
--- word ---
term: 이상한가
type: Adjective
breakdown: 이상하다 + -ㄴ가 보다
meaning: I guess (I'm) strange
notes: This is part of the grammar pattern -ㄴ/은/는가 보다 (-n/eun/neun-ga boda), used to express a guess or assumption. 이상하다 (isanghada) means 'to be strange/weird'.
--- word ---
term: 보다
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 
meaning: it seems / I guess
notes: This is the final part of the grammar pattern -ㄴ/은/는가 보다 (-n/eun/neun-ga boda). It softens the statement into a supposition based on some evidence or feeling. It's a very common colloquial ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)ㄴ/는가 보다
meaning: Used to express a guess or assumption based on an observation. It translates to 'I guess...', 'It seems that...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 비가 오는가 봐요. (Biga oneun-ga bwayo.) - It looks like it's raining.
level: Intermediate

=== [20:57] 먹어볼게 ===

--- word ---
term: 먹어볼게
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -어 보다 + -ㄹ게

=== [21:57] 우유랑 먹어야 되네 ===
meaning: Oh, I should eat this with milk.
tone: Informal, realization. A sudden thought or suggestion about how to eat the snack.

term: 우유랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 우유 + -(이)랑
meaning: with milk
notes: -(이)랑 is a colloquial particle meaning 'with' or 'and.' It's used in casual speech instead of the more standard 와/과 (wa/gwa) or 하고 (hago). Since 우유 (uyu) ends in a vowel, 랑 is attached directly.

term: 먹어야
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -어야 하다/되다
meaning: have to eat
notes: This is part of the -어야 하다/되다 grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity ('must,' 'have to'). Here, the 하다/되다 part is combined with the sentence ending -네.
--- word ---
term: 되네
type: Verb + Verb Ending
breakdown: 되다 + -네
meaning: it seems I should
notes: The -네 ending expresses mild surprise, realization, or a personal observation. It softens the statement of obligation from -어야 되다, turning it into a personal reflection like 'Oh, I see I should...' or 'It seems one must...'. It's very common in conversational Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb-아/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'have to.'
example: 지금 가야 돼요. (I have to go now.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -네(요)
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to express mild surprise, admiration, or a new realization upon observing something.
example: 날씨가 좋네. (Oh, the weather is nice.)
level: Beginner

=== [22:01] 응 ===
meaning: Yeah.
tone: Informal, affirmative. Agreeing with her own thought or a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 응
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: yeah / yes
notes: 응 (eung) is the most common informal way to say 'yes' in Korean, used with friends or people younger than you (반말, banmal). The formal equivalent is 네 (ne) or 예 (ye). Using 응 in a formal situation would be considered rude.

=== [22:08] 맛있는 거 같아 ===
meaning: I think it's delicious.
tone: Informal, speculative. Softening her opinion, making it less direct.
--- word ---
term: 맛있는
type: Adjective (modifier form)
breakdown: 맛있다 + -는
meaning: delicious (that is...)
notes: This is the modifier form of the adjective 맛있다 (masitda). While most descriptive adjectives use -(으)ㄴ to modify nouns, '있다/없다' adjectives like 맛있다 use -는. This is a common point of confusion for learners. For example, 예쁜 여자 (yeppeun yeoja - pretty woman) vs. 맛있는 음식 (masinneun eumsik - delicious food).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (contraction)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing
notes: 거 (geo) is the colloquial contraction of 것 (geot), meaning 'thing' or 'fact.' In this grammar pattern, it acts as a placeholder noun.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -아
meaning: it seems / it's like
notes: This is the informal (반말, banmal) conjugation of 같다 (gatta). It's used in the pattern -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다 to express a guess, opinion, or impression.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective/Verb + -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Used to express a guess, supposition, or opinion softly. It translates to 'I think...', 'It seems like...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (I think it's raining.)
level: Beginner

=== [22:12] 응 ===
meaning: Yeah.
tone: Informal, affirmative. Confirming her previous statement.
--- word ---
term: 응
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: yeah / yes
notes: 응 (eung) is the informal way to say 'yes' (반말, banmal), used among close friends. The formal equivalent is 네 (ne). It can be used to agree, confirm, or simply as a filler to show one is listening.

=== [22:12] 우유랑 먹으면 맛있겠다 ===
meaning: This would be delicious with milk.
tone: Informal, speculative.
--- word ---
term: 우유랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 우유 (milk) + -랑 (with)
meaning: with milk
notes: -(이)랑 is a colloquial particle meaning 'with' or 'and,' primarily used in casual speech instead of the more standard 와/과 or 하고. Use -랑 after a noun ending in a vowel (e.g., 우유랑) and -이랑 after a noun ending in a consonant (e.g., 빵이랑 - with bread).
--- word ---
term: 먹으면
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 (to eat) + -(으)면 (if/when)
meaning: if (one) eats
notes: The -(으)면 ending creates a conditional clause, meaning 'if' or 'when.' It attaches to verb and adjective stems. Use -으면 for stems ending in a consonant (e.g., 먹다 -> 먹으면) and -면 for stems ending in a vowel (e.g., 가다 -> 가면).
--- word ---
term: 맛있겠다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 맛있다 (to be delicious) + -겠- (would be/seems) + -다 (declarative ending)
meaning: it would be delicious
notes: The infix -겠- expresses supposition or a strong guess. Here, it conveys 'I bet it would be...' or 'It must be...'. It's often used when reacting to something, like imagining the taste of food. The -다 ending is the plain form, common in casual, self-directed speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-(으)면
meaning: A conditional clause marker meaning 'if' or 'when'.
example: 시간이 있으면 영화 봐요. (If I have time, I watch a movie.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-겠다
meaning: Expresses supposition, assumption, or intention ('I guess,' 'it must be,' 'I will').
example: 저 케이크는 정말 맛있겠다. (That cake must be really delicious.)
level: Intermediate

=== [22:57] 또 붙여있어 ===
meaning: There's another one attached.
tone: Informal, observational.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again / also
notes: An adverb meaning 'again' or 'in addition.' It can indicate repetition or add another item. It differs slightly from 다시 (again), as 또 can also mean 'also' or 'as well,' while 다시 specifically means 'to do over' or 'one more time'.
--- word ---
term: 붙여있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 붙다 (to be attached) + -어 있다 (state of being) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: is attached
notes: A colloquial form. The grammatically standard form is 붙어 있어 (from 붙다 + -어 있다). This sentence uses the V-어/아 있다 pattern, which describes a continuing state resulting from a completed action. The -어 ending signifies informal speech (banmal).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어/아 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuous state of being that resulted from a past action.
example: 창문이 열려 있어요. (The window is open.)
level: Intermediate

=== [23:05] 이런 소금 포장 너무 좋아 ===
meaning: I really like this kind of salt packaging.
tone: Informal, expressive of a personal preference.
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -ㄴ (adjective modifier form)
meaning: this kind of / like this
notes: The modifier form of 이렇다 ('to be like this'), used before a noun to describe its type or quality. Compare with 이 (this, specific), 저런 (that kind of, over there), and 그런 (that kind of, previously mentioned).
--- word ---
term: 소금
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: salt
notes: The standard Korean word for 'salt'.
--- word ---
term: 포장
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: packaging / wrapping
notes: This noun means 'packaging.' It can be combined with 하다 to form a verb, 포장하다 (to pack/wrap). When ordering food, '포장해 주세요' means 'for takeout, please'.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / very / really
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too much' in a negative sense, in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is commonly used to mean 'very' or 'really' in both positive and negative contexts, similar to 정말 or 아주. For example, '너무 좋아' (I really like it) is very common.
--- word ---

meaning: (I) like it / it's good
notes: The informal, non-polite (banmal) form of 좋다 (to be good). When the implied subject is 'I', 좋다 is often used to mean 'I like'. This is different from the action verb 좋아하다 (to like), which would be conjugated as 좋아해 in banmal.

notes: A common filler sound in Korean, similar to 'um' or 'uh' in English. It indicates hesitation or that the speaker is thinking.
--- word ---
term: 먹어볼게
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -어 보다 + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I'll try eating it.
notes: This combines two patterns. -어/아 보다 means 'to try doing (verb).' -(으)ㄹ게 is an informal future tense ending (반말, banmal) expressing the speaker's intention, often as a response to the current situation. It conveys a softer, more conversational will than other future tense forms.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -어/아 보다
meaning: Means 'to try doing (verb).' It's used when attempting an action to see what it's like.
example: 이 신발 신어 봐도 돼요? (Can I try on these shoes?)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: A first-person statement of intent or a promise, often made in the moment. It's softer than other future tenses. The speaker uses the informal -(으)ㄹ게.
example: 내가 먼저 갈게. (I'll go first.)
level: Beginner

=== [24:10] 짜잔 ===
meaning: Ta-da!
tone: Informal, playful, presentational.
--- word ---
term: 짜잔
type: Onomatopoeia / Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ta-da!
notes: 짜잔 is an onomatopoeic word used when revealing something, similar to 'Ta-da!' or a drumroll sound effect in English. It's often used in a playful or dramatic way.

=== [24:12] ㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋ ===
meaning: (Laughter)
tone: Amused, informal.
--- word ---
term: ㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋㅋ
type: Slang / Onomatopoeia
breakdown: 
meaning: (Laughter)
notes: This represents laughter in Korean online communication. Each 'ㅋ' is the consonant 'k' (키읔, kieuk), representing the 'k' sound in a chuckle (크크크, keukeukeu). The more 'ㅋ's, the harder the laughter. It is the equivalent of 'lol' or 'haha'.

=== [24:16] 처음에 먹은 게 제일 맛있는 것 같아 처음에 맛있는 게 짱이었어 ===
meaning: The first one I ate seems to be the most delicious. The one that was tasty at first was the best.
tone: Informal, reflective, giving an opinion on snacks.
--- word ---
term: 처음에
type: adverb
breakdown: 처음 + 에
meaning: at first
notes: 처음 means 'first' or 'the beginning'. The particle 에 marks a point in time. It's often used to contrast the beginning of something with a later point.
--- word ---
term: 먹은
type: verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 먹다 + -은
meaning: that (I) ate
notes: This is the past-tense adjective form of the verb 먹다 (to eat). The -(으)ㄴ ending turns a verb into a descriptive word that can modify a noun, like 'the food (I) ate' (먹은 음식).
--- word ---
term: 게
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 이
meaning: the thing that...
notes: A very common colloquial contraction of 것 (thing) and the subject particle 이. So, 먹은 게 is a casual way of saying 먹은 것이 (the thing that I ate).
--- word ---
term: 제일
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: the most, best
notes: Used to form the superlative, meaning 'the most'. It's interchangeable with 가장 (gajang), but 제일 is often preferred in spoken Korean. For example, 제일 좋아 (I like it the most).
--- word ---
term: 맛있는
type: adjective
breakdown: 맛있다 + -는
meaning: delicious
notes: While most descriptive adjectives use -(으)ㄴ to modify nouns (e.g., 예쁜 사람, a pretty person), adjectives ending in -있다 (to exist) or -없다 (to not exist), like 맛있다, use -는. This is a common exception for learners to remember.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing
notes: A dependent noun that means 'thing' or 'fact'. It's used in countless grammatical constructions, often to nominalize a verb or clause.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -아
meaning: it seems, I think
notes: The informal, present tense conjugation of 같다 (to be like, to seem). Used with a noun or a verb clause to express a guess, opinion, or resemblance.
--- word ---
term: 처음에
type: adverb
breakdown: 처음 + 에
meaning: at first
notes: Repetition of the first word for emphasis, contrasting the initial impression with later ones.
--- word ---
term: 맛있는
type: adjective
breakdown: 맛있다 + -는
meaning: delicious
notes: The present adjective form of 맛있다 (to be delicious). Here it modifies 게 (thing), meaning 'the thing that is delicious'.
--- word ---
term: 게
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 이
meaning: the thing that...
notes: Another instance of the colloquial contraction of 것 (thing) + 이 (subject particle).
--- word ---
term: 짱이었어
type: noun + verb
breakdown: 짱 + 이다 + -었어
meaning: was the best
notes: 짱 (jjang) is popular slang meaning 'the best,' 'awesome,' or 'cool.' Here it's combined with the past tense of 이다 (to be) in the informal 반말 style.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, opinion, or that something 'seems' or 'looks like' something. It softens the statement.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Beginner

=== [24:24] 잘 됐다 짐이 많이 줄었어 난 기준 높아 ===

breakdown: 되다 + -었다
meaning: it turned out well
notes: A common contraction of 되었다. The phrase 잘 됐다 is a very frequent expression meaning 'That's great!', 'Good!', or 'It worked out'.
--- word ---
term: 짐이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 짐 + 이
meaning: luggage (is)
notes: 짐 (jim) means 'luggage' or 'baggage'. 이 is the subject particle, used here because the noun ends in a consonant.

meaning: a lot, much

meaning: it decreased

--- word ---

meaning: I, as for me

--- word ---
