meaning: Verbs ending in '르' (like 모르다, 빠르다, 부르다) follow a special rule when a vowel ending is added. The '으' is dropped, and a 'ㄹ' is added as a final consonant to the preceding syllable.
example: 빠르다 (to be fast) + -아요 -> 빨라요 (It's fast).

breakdown: 있다
meaning: are there / exist
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말) form of 있다 (to exist, to be at a location). The subject (the real villains, the people) is omitted but understood from context, which is very common in Korean.

=== [43:40] 혈압 오르게 하는 사람 몇 명이 있어 ===
meaning: There are a few people who make my blood pressure rise.
tone: Informal, cynical humor. Comparing annoying people to scary monsters.
--- word ---
term: 혈압
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: blood pressure
notes: A common term used metaphorically in Korean to express extreme stress or anger. Saying someone 'raises your blood pressure' (혈압 오르게 하다) is a very common idiom for saying they are incredibly frustrating.
--- word ---
term: 오르게
type: Verb (adverbial form)
breakdown: 오르다 + -게
meaning: so that it rises
notes: The `-게` ending transforms a verb or adjective into an adverbial form, modifying the following verb. Here, it modifies `하는` (doing/making). The pattern `V-게 하다` means 'to make someone/something V'.
--- word ---
term: 하는
type: Verb (adjectival form)
breakdown: 하다 + -는
meaning: that does/makes
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of `하다` (to do). It creates a relative clause that modifies the following noun, `사람` (person). So, `오르게 하는 사람` means 'a person who makes (it) rise'.
--- word ---
term: 사람
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: person
notes: A general term for a person or people. It's being modified by the preceding clause `혈압 오르게 하는`.
--- word ---
term: 몇
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: a few, some
notes: Used before a noun (and its counter) to indicate an unspecified small number. It's similar to 'some' or 'a few' in English.
--- word ---
term: 명이
type: Counter + Particle
breakdown: 명 + -이
meaning: people (counter, as subject)
notes: `명` is the native Korean counter for people. `-이` is the subject particle, attached because `명` ends in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -어
meaning: there are
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) present tense form of `있다` (to exist, to be). The polite equivalent would be `있어요`.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-게 하다
meaning: Causative form: 'to make/cause someone/something to V'. It indicates that the subject causes the action of the verb to happen.
example: 엄마가 아이를 먹게 했어요. (The mom made the child eat.)
level: Intermediate

=== [43:43] 그 사람들이 더 무섭더라 ===
meaning: Those people are scarier, I've realized.
tone: Informal, reflective, stating a personal discovery.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that, those
notes: `그` refers to something previously mentioned or known to both the speaker and listener. Here, it refers to the 'people who make blood pressure rise' from the previous sentence.
--- word ---
term: 사람들이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사람들 + -이
meaning: people (as subject)
notes: `사람들` is the plural form of `사람` (person). The particle `-이` marks it as the subject of the sentence. It's used instead of `-가` because the noun ends in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more
notes: Used to make a comparison, similar to adding '-er' or 'more' in English. It precedes the adjective or adverb it modifies, in this case `무섭더라` (is scarier).
--- word ---
term: 무섭더라
type: Adjective
breakdown: 무섭다 + -더라
meaning: is scary (I recall/realized)
notes: The `-더라` ending is used to express a new realization or observation based on the speaker's past experience or memory. It conveys a sense of reflection, like 'I found that...' or 'I recall that...'. It's a common feature of informal, spoken Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -더라
meaning: A retrospective sentence ending used to state a fact the speaker newly realized or directly witnessed in the past. It adds a nuance of personal experience and reflection.
example: 어제 그 식당에 가니까 사람이 많더라. (When I went to that restaurant yesterday, I saw that it was crowded.)
level: Intermediate

=== [43:54] 어 여기 고층이야 ===
meaning: Oh, this is a high floor.
tone: Informal, observational. Likely responding to a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 어
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Oh, Ah
notes: A common interjection used to express mild surprise, realization, or acknowledgement. It's very informal.
--- word ---
term: 여기
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: here, this place
notes: Refers to the speaker's current location. Can be used as 'here' or 'this place'. Often used as the subject without a particle in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 고층이야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 고층 + -이야
meaning: it is a high floor
notes: `고층` (高層) means 'high floor' or 'high-rise'. `-이야` is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) form of 'is'. It's attached to nouns ending in a consonant. The polite form is `고층이에요`.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)야
meaning: The informal, non-polite (반말) sentence ending for 'is [Noun]'. Use `-이야` after a consonant and `-야` after a vowel.
example: 이건 선물이야. (This is a gift.) / 저 사람은 의사야. (That person is a doctor.)
level: Beginner

term: 여기

breakdown: 43층 + -이었다 + -나?
meaning: was it the 43rd floor?
notes: `43층` (사십삼층) is '43rd floor'. `-이었-` is the past tense stem of the copula `이다` (to be). The `-나?` ending turns this into a self-directed question, expressing uncertainty or trying to recall something.

meaning: A past-tense questioning ending used when the speaker is asking themselves something or wondering about a past event. It conveys uncertainty and reflection.

=== [43:58] 그래서 더 그런가? ===
meaning: Is that why it's even more like this?
tone: Informal, speculative, wondering aloud about a cause-and-effect relationship.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Adverb/Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + -어서
meaning: so, therefore, for that reason
notes: A contraction of `그리하여서`. It connects a cause (being on a high floor) to a result (the current situation, i.e., the thick fog).
--- word ---
term: 더
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more, even more
notes: Emphasizes the degree of the following adjective/verb. Here it means 'even more so'.
--- word ---
term: 그런가?
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 그렇다 + -ㄴ가?
meaning: is it like that?
notes: `그렇다` means 'to be so' or 'to be like that'. The `-(으)ㄴ가?` ending is a way of asking a question to oneself or gently to others, expressing wonder or speculation. It's softer than a direct question like `그래?`.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + -(으)ㄴ/는가?
meaning: A questioning ending used to express doubt, wonder, or a self-directed question. It feels softer and more speculative than a standard question.
example: 비가 오는가? (I wonder if it's raining?)
level: Intermediate

=== [44:00] 안개가 그래서 더 꼈나? ===

meaning: so, for that reason

--- word ---

meaning: did it form/gather?
notes: The verb `끼다` is specifically used for things like fog, clouds, dust, or dirt that form, gather, or settle. The past tense `-었-` and self-questioning `-나?` ending express wonder about the current state of the fog.

=== [44:02] 갑자기 괴물 나오면 어떡할 거냐고? ===

--- word ---
term: 갑자기
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: suddenly
notes: A common adverb used to describe an unexpected or abrupt action.
--- word ---
term: 괴물
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: monster
notes: Refers to any kind of monster or creature. The context is her talking about the horror movie 'The Mist'.
--- word ---
term: 나오면
type: Verb
breakdown: 나오다 + -(으)면
meaning: if (it) appears
notes: `나오다` means 'to come out' or 'to appear'. The `-(으)면` ending creates a conditional clause, meaning 'if' or 'when'.
--- word ---
term: 어떡할
type: Verb phrase (adjectival form)
breakdown: 어떡하다 + -(으)ㄹ
meaning: (the thing) to do
notes: `어떡할` is a contraction of `어떻게 할` (what to do). It's modifying the implied noun `것` (thing). `어떡하다` is a common colloquial verb meaning 'to do what'.
--- word ---
term: 거냐고?
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 것 + -이냐고?
meaning: are you asking if...
notes: This is a reported speech ending for a question. It's a contraction of `것이냐고?`. The full sentence would be `...어떡할 거냐고 물었어요?` (Did you ask what I would do?). In conversation, the ending alone is used to repeat a question just heard or read.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(느)냐고?
meaning: An indirect quotation ending used to report or repeat a question. In conversation, it's often used on its own to show that the speaker is repeating a question they just heard from the listener (or in this case, read in the chat).
example: 어디 가냐고? (Are you asking where I'm going?)
level: Intermediate

=== [44:15] 놀라겠지? ===
meaning: I'd be surprised, of course, right?
tone: Informal, stating the obvious with a rhetorical question.
--- word ---
term: 놀라겠지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 놀라다 + -겠- + -지?
meaning: I would be surprised, right?
notes: `놀라다` means 'to be surprised'. The `-겠-` suffix here indicates supposition or conjecture ('I would probably...'). The `-지?` ending seeks agreement or states something as obvious, similar to 'of course' or '..., right?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -겠-
meaning: A suffix that can indicate future tense, but is also frequently used to express the speaker's assumption, supposition, or conjecture about a situation. ('I guess...', 'I bet...', '...would probably...').
example: 지금 가면 춥겠지. (It'll probably be cold if we go now.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to confirm a fact that the speaker believes the listener already knows or will agree with. It can be a genuine question or a rhetorical one to soften a statement.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (The weather's nice today, isn't it?)
level: Beginner

=== [44:16] 갑자기 괴물이 나오면 ===
meaning: If a monster suddenly appears...
tone: Informal, hypothetical, setting up a scenario.
--- word ---
term: 갑자기
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: suddenly
notes: An adverb used to describe an event that happens without warning. It's very common in both spoken and written Korean. It's often used at the beginning of a sentence to set a scene, like '갑자기 비가 왔다' (Suddenly, it started raining).
--- word ---
term: 괴물이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 괴물 + 이
meaning: a monster (as the subject)
notes: 괴물 (goemul) means 'monster'. The particle 이 (i) is a subject marker used after nouns ending in a consonant. The alternative, 가 (ga), is used after nouns ending in a vowel. For example, '친구가' (chingu-ga, a friend).
--- word ---
term: 나오면
type: Verb
breakdown: 나오다 + -(으)면
meaning: if (it) appears
notes: 나오다 (naoda) means 'to come out' or 'to appear'. The ending -(으)면 (-(eu)myeon) is a common way to create a conditional clause, meaning 'if' or 'when'. Since the stem 나오- ends in a vowel, 면 is added directly.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)면
meaning: A conditional ending attached to verb or adjective stems, meaning 'if' or 'when'.
example: 시간이 있으면 영화 볼까요? (Sigani isseumyeon yeonghwa bolkkayo?) - If we have time, shall we watch a movie?
level: Beginner

=== [44:29] 근데 그 괴물이 청이라서 언니는 안 잡아먹는데 ===
meaning: But since that monster is a fan (a Cheong-i), it wouldn't eat me (Unni)...
tone: Informal, playful, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: but, however
notes: 근데 (geunde) is a very common colloquial contraction of 그런데 (geureonde). It can mean 'but' to show contrast, or 'by the way' to change the subject. Here, it shows contrast.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: 그 (geu) is used to refer to something that is far from the speaker but may be close to or known by the listener. It contrasts with 이 (i, 'this') and 저 (jeo, 'that over there').
--- word ---
term: 괴물이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 괴물 + 이
meaning: the monster (as the subject)
notes: 괴물 (goemul) means 'monster'. The particle 이 (i) marks it as the subject of the clause. It's used here because '괴물' ends in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 청이라서
type: Noun + Copula + Conjunction
breakdown: 청이 + -(이)다 + -라서
meaning: because it's a Cheong-i
notes: 청이 (Cheong-i) is the speaker's fandom name. The pattern Noun + -(이)라서 (-(i)raseo) means 'because it is (Noun)'. It's a common way to state the reason for something in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 언니는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 + 는
meaning: me (Unni)
notes: 언니 (eonni) is used by a female speaker to refer to an older sister or close older female friend. K-pop idols often refer to themselves as 언니 when talking to younger fans. The particle 는 (neun) is a topic marker, highlighting 'me' as the topic of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form negator placed directly before a verb or adjective. For example, '안 먹어요' (an meogeoyo, I don't eat). It's more common in spoken Korean than the long-form negation `-지 않다` (-ji anta).
--- word ---
term: 잡아먹는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 잡아먹다 + -는데
meaning: it eats, but...
notes: 잡아먹다 (jabameokda) is a compound verb from 잡다 (japda, to catch) and 먹다 (meokda, to eat). The ending -는데 (-neunde) is very versatile. Here, it provides background information and implies a following thought or contrast, leaving the sentence unfinished.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)라서
meaning: Used to give a noun as a reason for something. It means 'because it is (Noun)'.
example: 학생이라서 돈이 없어요. (Haksaeng-iraseo doni eopseoyo.) - I don't have money because I'm a student.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -는데/-(으)ㄴ데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background context, shows a soft contrast, or introduces a main clause.
example: 비가 오는데 우리 뭐 할까? (Biga oneunde uri mwo halkka?) - It's raining, so what should we do?
level: Intermediate

=== [44:34] 홍콩형을 내야지 ===
meaning: You have to give them a 'Hong Kong punishment'!
tone: Informal, assertive, humorous slang.
--- word ---
term: 홍콩형을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 홍콩형 + 을
meaning: a 'Hong Kong punishment'
notes: This is a creative, slang-like term. 홍콩 (Hongkong) is Hong Kong. 형 (hyeong) here likely comes from the Hanja character 刑, meaning 'punishment'. The phrase is probably a playful reference to old Hong Kong action movies. The particle 을 (eul) marks it as the object of the verb.
--- word ---
term: 내야지
type: Verb
breakdown: 내다 + -아야/어야지
meaning: you have to give
notes: 내다 (naeda) means 'to give' or 'to issue'. The grammar pattern -아야/어야지 (-aya/eoyaji) expresses obligation or a strong intention, similar to 'must' or 'should'. It often carries a nuance of 'of course' or 'naturally'. It's a contraction of -아야/어야 하지.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아야/어야지
meaning: Expresses obligation or strong intention, often in an assertive or self-talking manner. It means 'must do V' or 'should do V'.
example: 이제 숙제해야지. (Ije sukjehaeyaji.) - I've got to do my homework now.
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 
meaning: now

type: Verb Phrase
breakdown: 뭐 하다 + -는

type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이것이 (contraction)
meaning: this thing (is)
notes: 이게 (ige) is a contraction of 이것이 (igeos-i), which is 'this thing' (이것) plus the subject particle 이 (i). In this sentence, it's added at the end for emphasis, almost like saying 'What is this you're doing?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~는 거야?
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to ask questions, often expressing surprise, curiosity, or mild confrontation. Literally 'is it the thing that...?'
example: 어디 가는 거야? (Eodi ganeun geoya?) - Where are you going?

=== [44:49] 이렇게 혼내야지 ===
meaning: You have to scold them like this.
tone: Informal, explanatory, concluding the role-play.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이러하게 (contraction)
meaning: like this, in this way
notes: A contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage). It's used to demonstrate how something should be done. It's part of a set: 이렇게 (ireoke, like this), 그렇게 (geureoke, like that), 저렇게 (jeoreoke, like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 혼내야지
type: Verb
breakdown: 혼내다 + -아야/어야지
meaning: you have to scold
notes: 혼내다 (honnaeda) means 'to scold' or 'to tell off'. The grammar pattern -아야/어야지 (-aya/eoyaji) expresses obligation or a strong intention, similar to 'must' or 'should'. It implies this is the obvious or correct course of action.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아야/어야지
meaning: Expresses obligation or strong intention, often in an assertive or self-talking manner. It means 'must do V' or 'should do V'.
example: 늦었으니까 빨리 가야지. (Neujeosseunikka ppalli gayaji.) - I'm late, so I've got to go quickly.
level: Intermediate

=== [44:51] 내가 권력을 쥐어잡잖아 ===
meaning: I'm the one who holds the power, you know.
tone: Informal, assertive, explaining her logic in a hypothetical horror movie scenario.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~가
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: This is the subject form of 'I' (나). When the subject particle ~가 is attached to 나, it changes to 내가. This is a common irregularity for learners to memorize. The formal equivalent is 제가 (저 + ~가).
--- word ---
term: 권력을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 권력 + ~을
meaning: power (as the object)
notes: 권력 (gwol-lyeok) means 'power' or 'authority'. The particle ~을 marks it as the direct object of the verb. The choice between ~을 and ~를 depends on whether the noun ends in a consonant (~을) or a vowel (~를).
--- word ---
term: 쥐어잡잖아
type: Verb
breakdown: 쥐어잡다 + ~잖아
meaning: grasp, you know
notes: The base verb is 쥐어잡다, a compound of 쥐다 (to hold) and 잡다 (to catch), meaning 'to grasp firmly'. The ending ~잖아 is used to state something the listener is likely already aware of, or to emphasize a point. It translates to 'you know', 'as you know', or 'isn't it obvious?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~잖아(요)
meaning: Used to confirm a fact or remind the listener of something they both already know. It can sound like '...you know' or '...of course'.
example: 오늘 춥잖아. (It's cold today, you know.)
level: Beginner

=== [45:00] 괴물이랑 소통이 불가능한데 ===
meaning: But communication with the monster is impossible...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 괴물이랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 괴물 + ~이랑
meaning: with the monster
notes: 괴물 means 'monster'. The particle ~(이)랑 is a colloquial way to say 'with' or 'and', used frequently in spoken Korean instead of the more formal ~와/과. Use ~이랑 after a consonant and ~랑 after a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 소통이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 소통 + ~이
meaning: communication (as the subject)
notes: 소통 means 'communication'. The particle ~이 marks it as the subject of the clause. Since 소통 ends in a consonant, ~이 is used. If it ended in a vowel, ~가 would be used.
--- word ---
term: 불가능한데
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 불가능하다 + ~ㄴ데
meaning: is impossible, but/and...
notes: 불가능하다 means 'to be impossible'. The ending ~ㄴ데/~은데 is very versatile. Here, it connects two clauses, providing background information or a soft contrast. It often leaves the sentence unfinished, inviting a response or continuation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + ~(으)ㄴ/는데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background or context for the following clause. It can mean 'but', 'so', 'and', or simply set the scene. It's very common in conversation and often used to soften a statement or trail off.
example: 비가 오는데 우리 뭐 할까? (It's raining, so what should we do?)
level: Intermediate

=== [45:06] 내 팬이야? ===
meaning: Are you my fan?
tone: Informal, rhetorical, playful question to a fan.
--- word ---
term: 내
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 나의
meaning: my
notes: 내 is the contraction of 나의 (na-ui), the possessive form of 'I' (나). In spoken Korean, the particle ~의 is almost always dropped and 나 becomes 내 to mean 'my'.
--- word ---
term: 팬이야?
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 팬 + ~이야?
meaning: Are you a fan?
notes: This is the informal question form of 'to be' (이다). The ending ~이야? is used after nouns ending in a consonant, like 팬 (fan). For nouns ending in a vowel, ~야? would be used (e.g., 친구야? - Are you a friend?).

=== [45:07] 그런 게 어딨어 ===
meaning: That doesn't make any sense.
tone: Informal, dismissive, rhetorical.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: that kind of / such
notes: 그런 comes from the descriptive verb 그렇다 (to be so, to be like that). It modifies the following noun, meaning 'that kind of' or 'such'. The three related forms are 이런 (this kind of), 그런 (that kind of), and 저런 (that kind of, over there).
--- word ---
term: 게
type: Noun (Contraction)
breakdown: 것 + ~이
meaning: thing (is)
notes: 게 is a very common spoken contraction of 것이, which is the noun 것 (thing) plus the subject particle ~이. Learners should get used to hearing and using 게 in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 어딨어
type: Adverb + Verb (Contraction)
breakdown: 어디에 있어
meaning: where is it?
notes: 어딨어 is a contraction of 어디에 있어? (Where is it?). This is the standard way to ask 'where is...' in casual speech. 어디 means 'where', ~에 is the location particle, and 있어 is the informal present tense of 있다 (to be/exist).
--- grammar ---
pattern: 그런 게 어딨어(요)?
meaning: A common idiomatic expression literally meaning 'Where is such a thing?'. It's used rhetorically to mean 'That's ridiculous', 'No way', or 'That doesn't exist'.
example: 세상에 공짜가 어딨어. (There's no such thing as a free lunch.)
level: Intermediate

=== [45:26] 다들 귀신이 있는 거 같아? ===
meaning: Does everyone think ghosts exist?
tone: Informal, curious, asking the audience a general question.
--- word ---
term: 다들
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 다 + ~들
meaning: everyone
notes: 다 means 'all', and ~들 is the plural marker. 다들 is a friendly, colloquial way to address a group, similar to 'everybody' or 'you all'.
--- word ---
term: 귀신이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 + ~이
meaning: a ghost (as the subject)
notes: 귀신 (gwi-sin) means 'ghost' or 'spirit'. The particle ~이 marks it as the subject of the verb 있다 (to exist).
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + ~는
meaning: that exists
notes: This is the present tense modifying form of the verb 있다 (to exist). The ~는 ending turns the verb into an adjective that can describe the following noun, in this case, '것' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (Contraction)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing / fact
notes: 거 is the spoken form of 것 (geot), which means 'thing' or 'fact'. It's extremely common in daily conversation.
--- word ---
term: 같아?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다
meaning: does it seem like?
notes: 같다 means 'to be like' or 'to seem'. Here it's conjugated in the informal non-polite form. When combined with ~는 것, it forms a pattern for expressing opinion or conjecture.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, opinion, or impression about an action or state. It translates to 'I think that...', 'It seems that...', or 'It looks like...'. It's a softer way of stating an opinion than a direct statement.
example: 밖에 비가 오는 것 같아. (I think it's raining outside.)
level: Beginner

=== [45:28] 귀신이 있다고 믿어? ===
meaning: Do you believe that ghosts exist?
tone: Informal, direct question to the audience.
--- word ---
term: 귀신이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 + ~이
meaning: a ghost (as the subject)
notes: 귀신 (gwi-sin) means 'ghost'. The particle ~이 marks it as the subject of the embedded clause 'ghosts exist'.
--- word ---
term: 있다고
type: Verb + Particle
breakdown: 있다 + ~고
meaning: that (it) exists
notes: The particle ~고 is a quoting particle. When attached to a verb or adjective stem (with ~다~), it means 'that...'. So, 있다고 means 'that (it) exists'. This is used to report or quote speech, thoughts, or beliefs.
--- word ---
term: 믿어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 믿다
meaning: do you believe?
notes: The informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of the verb 믿다 (mit-da), meaning 'to believe' or 'to trust'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~다고 믿다
meaning: A pattern used to express belief in a certain action or state. It combines a verb with the quoting particle ~다고 and the verb 믿다 (to believe).
example: 저는 그가 올 거라고 믿어요. (I believe that he will come.)
level: Intermediate

=== [45:34] 그런 거 같아? ===
meaning: Does it seem like that?
tone: Informal, seeking confirmation from the audience.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: that kind of / such
notes: From the verb 그렇다 (to be so). It modifies the following noun, '거', to mean 'that kind of thing'.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (Contraction)
breakdown: 것
meaning: thing
notes: The colloquial, spoken form of 것 (geot), meaning 'thing'. '그런 거' means 'that kind of thing' or 'something like that'.
--- word ---
term: 같아?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다
meaning: does it seem like?
notes: The informal conjugation of 같다 (to be like, to seem). The phrase '그런 거 같아?' is a very common way to ask 'Does it seem that way?' or 'Do you think so?'.

=== [45:41] 아까 유리에게 비친 귀신을 봤어? ===
meaning: Did you see the ghost reflected in the glass earlier?
tone: Informal, asking about a specific fan comment in the chat.
--- word ---
term: 아까
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a little while ago / earlier
notes: 아까 (akka) refers to a time in the recent past, usually within the same day. It's distinct from 지금 (now) and 이따가 (a little later).
--- word ---
term: 유리에게
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 유리 + ~에게
meaning: in the glass
notes: 유리 means 'glass'. The particle ~에게 is typically used for people or animals to mean 'to' or 'from'. Using it with an inanimate object like 'glass' is less common than using ~에, but it can be done for stylistic or poetic effect, personifying the object.
--- word ---
term: 비친
type: Verb
breakdown: 비치다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: reflected
notes: The past-tense modifier form of 비치다 (to be reflected, to shine). The ~ㄴ ending turns the verb into an adjective that describes the following noun, 귀신 (ghost).
--- word ---
term: 귀신을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 + ~을
meaning: the ghost (as the object)
notes: 귀신 (ghost) is marked with the object particle ~을 because it is the thing that was seen.
--- word ---
term: 봤어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 + ~았어?
meaning: did you see?
notes: The informal, non-polite past tense question form of the verb 보다 (to see).

=== [45:43] 뭘까? ===
meaning: What could it be?
tone: Informal, questioning, self-reflective.
--- word ---
term: 뭘까?
type: Pronoun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 뭐 (what) + -ㄹ까? (interrogative ending)
meaning: I wonder what...
notes: 뭘 is a contraction of 무엇을 (what, object), but here it functions as a contraction of 뭐 + 일까 (is it?). The -ㄹ까? ending is used when wondering about something to oneself or when making a suggestion to others. This is a very common phrase in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ까?
meaning: An interrogative ending used to express supposition, ask a question to oneself (wondering), or make a suggestion.
example: 오늘 저녁 뭐 먹을까? (What should we eat for dinner?)
level: Beginner

=== [45:44] 난 잘 모르겠는데 ===
meaning: I don't really know...
tone: Informal, hesitant, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + -는 (topic particle)
meaning: I / As for me
notes: 난 is the common spoken contraction of 나는. The topic particle -는/은 often introduces a topic with a slight nuance of contrast, as in 'As for me (unlike others)...'
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well / really
notes: While 잘 means 'well', when paired with a negative verb like 모르다 (to not know) or 못하다 (to be unable to do), it means 'not really' or 'not well'. For example, '잘 모르겠어요' means 'I don't really know.'
--- word ---
term: 모르겠는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 (to not know) + -겠- (conjecture) + -는데 (background ending)
meaning: I'm not sure, but...
notes: The -겠- infix softens the statement, expressing an assumption or guess ('I guess I don't know'). The -는데 ending leaves the sentence unfinished, which is common in conversation to sound less abrupt or to imply there's more to say.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -는데
meaning: A connective ending used to provide background information, show a soft contrast, or trail off a sentence in conversation.
example: 지금 바쁜데 나중에 전화할게. (I'm busy now, so I'll call you later.)
level: Beginner

=== [45:46] 귀신이 ===
meaning: A ghost...
tone: Informal, introductory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 귀신이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 (ghost) + -이 (subject particle)
meaning: A ghost (is/does...)
notes: The sentence is incomplete, with the speaker just stating the subject. The particle -이 is used after a noun ending in a consonant to mark it as the subject of the verb. The alternative, -가, is used for nouns ending in a vowel.

=== [45:47] 귀신은 없고 ===
meaning: There are no ghosts, and...
tone: Informal, declarative, connecting thoughts.
--- word ---
term: 귀신은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 (ghost) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: As for ghosts...
notes: The speaker switches from the subject particle (-이) to the topic particle (-은). This sets 'ghosts' as the topic and often implies a contrast with something else that will be mentioned next.
--- word ---
term: 없고
type: Adjective
breakdown: 없다 (to not exist) + -고 (connective ending)
meaning: there isn't, and...
notes: 없다 means 'to not exist' or 'to not have'. The -고 ending is a basic connective that links two clauses, similar to 'and' or 'and then'. It simply lists facts or actions in sequence.

=== [45:49] 미인은 있었던 거 같은데 말이지 ===
meaning: but I think there was a beautiful woman, you know.
tone: Informal, speculative, adding emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 미인은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 미인 (beautiful person) + -은 (topic particle)
meaning: As for a beautiful woman...
notes: 미인 literally means 'beautiful person' and is typically used for women. The topic particle -은 continues the contrast set up in the previous sentence: 'There are no ghosts, but as for a beautiful woman...'
--- word ---
term: 있었던
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 있다 (to exist) + -었- (past tense) + -던 (retrospective modifier)
meaning: that existed / that was there
notes: -던 is a retrospective adnominal ending. It modifies a noun by describing a past action or state that is being recalled from memory. It differs from -(으)ㄴ, which simply states a completed past fact.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: thing / fact
notes: 거 is the colloquial, spoken form of the dependent noun 것 (geot), which means 'thing' or 'fact'. It is extremely common in everyday conversation.
--- word ---
term: 같은데
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 (to be like) + -은데 (background ending)
meaning: it seems like, but...
notes: The pattern '-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다' means 'it seems like' or 'I think that...'. The -ㄴ데 ending softens the assertion and leaves it slightly open-ended.
--- word ---
term: 말이지
type: Phrase/Ending
breakdown: 말 (word) + 이다 (to be) + -지 (ending)
meaning: you know / I mean
notes: 말이지 is a common conversational tag used at the end of a sentence for emphasis or to seek implicit agreement, similar to 'you know?' or 'I mean...'. The polite form is 말이죠 (marijyo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, opinion, or impression. It's a softer way of stating something than a direct declaration.
example: 저 사람 학생인 것 같아요. (I think that person is a student.)
level: Beginner

=== [45:52] 아니 근데 진짜 귀신은 있는데 ===
meaning: No, but really, ghosts do exist...
tone: Informal, contradictory, emphatic.
--- word ---
term: 아니
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: No / Wait
notes: While 아니 means 'no', it's frequently used in conversation as an interjection to mean 'Wait a minute,' 'Oh, but,' or 'I mean...' before correcting oneself or introducing a contrasting thought.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: but / by the way
notes: 근데 is the spoken contraction of 그런데. It can mean 'but' or 'however' to show contrast, or it can be used to change the subject, like 'by the way'.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really / truly
notes: A very common word in spoken Korean for emphasis. It can be used as an adverb ('진짜 맛있다' - really delicious) or as a standalone utterance ('진짜?' - Really?).
--- word ---
term: 귀신은

notes: Here, the -는데 ending creates a sense of suspense. She is stating a fact ('ghosts exist') as background for a following thought or question, leaving the listener waiting for what comes next.

=== [46:15] 말해봐 ===
meaning: Tell me.
tone: Informal, imperative, direct.
--- word ---
term: 말해봐
type: Verb
breakdown: 말하다 (to speak) + -아/어 보다 (to try doing)
meaning: Try telling me / Go on, tell me.
notes: This is the informal imperative (command) form. The pattern V + -아/어 보다 means 'to try doing V'. As a command, -아/어 봐 is a softer, more suggestive command than a direct order like 말해! (Speak!). It invites the other person to act.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 보다
meaning: Expresses trying or experiencing an action. In the imperative form (V + -아/어 봐), it's a soft command or suggestion.
example: 이 김치 한번 먹어봐. (Try this kimchi.)

=== [46:21] 일하면서 귀신을 봤어? ===
meaning: You saw a ghost while working?
tone: Informal, inquisitive, repeating a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 일하면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 일하다 (to work) + -면서 (while)
meaning: while working
notes: The connective ending -(으)면서 attaches to a verb stem to indicate two actions happening at the same time by the same person. Since 일하다 ends in a vowel, -면서 is used. Example: '노래하면서 청소해요' (I clean while singing).
--- word ---
term: 귀신을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 귀신 (ghost) + -을 (object particle)
meaning: a ghost (as the object)
notes: 귀신 is a common word for a ghost or spirit. The particle -을 marks it as the direct object of the verb. -을 is used after nouns ending in a consonant; -를 is used after vowels.
--- word ---
term: 봤어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 (to see) + -았- (past tense) + -어? (informal question ending)
meaning: Did you see?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) past tense question form. It's a contraction of 보았어?. The speaker uses this casual speech style with her fans. The question is formed by a rising intonation at the end of the sentence.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)면서
meaning: Indicates simultaneous actions by the same subject; 'while V-ing'.
example: 영화를 보면서 팝콘을 먹었어요. (I ate popcorn while watching a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [46:25] 어떻게 생겼어? ===
meaning: What did it look like?
tone: Informal, curious, follow-up question.
--- word ---
term: 어떻게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 어떻다 (to be how) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: how / in what way
notes: A common question word. Learners often confuse 어떻게 (how) with 어떡해 (what to do?). 어떡해 is a contraction of '어떻게 해' and is an exclamation of distress, e.g., '나 어떡해?' (What should I do?).
--- word ---
term: 생겼어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 생기다 (to look like, to be formed) + -었- (past tense) + -어? (informal question ending)
meaning: did it look like?
notes: When describing appearance, 생기다 is always used in the past tense (생겼다), even if describing a present state. For example, '잘생겼어' means 'He is handsome,' not 'He was handsome.' This is a common point of confusion for learners.

=== [46:30] 살면서 귀신을 세 번 봤다고? ===
meaning: You've seen a ghost three times in your life?
tone: Informal, surprised, repeating a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 살면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 살다 (to live) + -면서 (while)
meaning: while living / in your life
notes: This is a very common fixed expression that means 'in the course of one's life' or 'as I've lived.' It literally translates to 'while living.'
--- word ---
term: 귀신을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 귀신 (ghost) + -을 (object particle)
meaning: a ghost
notes: The particle -을 marks 'ghost' as the direct object of the verb 'saw'.
--- word ---
term: 세
type: Number
breakdown: 셋 (three)
meaning: three
notes: When native Korean numbers (하나, 둘, 셋, 넷...) are used before a counter noun (like 번), they often shorten. 셋 (three) becomes 세. For example, '세 사람' (three people).
--- word ---
term: 번
type: Counter noun
breakdown: 
meaning: times (frequency)
notes: 번 is a counter for the number of times an action occurs. It is always used with native Korean numbers (한 번, 두 번, 세 번...).
--- word ---
term: 봤다고?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 보다 (to see) + -았- (past tense) + -다고?
meaning: You said you saw?
notes: The -다고? ending is used to quote or repeat something someone said, often to express surprise, disbelief, or to ask for confirmation. Here, the speaker is repeating a fan's comment from the chat.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(ㄴ/는)다고?
meaning: A reported speech ending used to repeat what was heard, often expressing surprise or seeking confirmation.
example: 내일 시험이 있다고? (You're saying there's a test tomorrow?!)
level: Intermediate

=== [46:32] 말도 안 돼 ===
meaning: No way.
tone: Informal, expressive, disbelief.
--- word ---
term: 말도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 말 (word, speech) + -도 (also, even)
meaning: even words
notes: 말 means 'speech' or 'words.' The particle -도 adds emphasis, meaning 'even.'
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 is a short and common way to negate a verb or adjective, placed directly before it. It is more common in spoken language than the longer form, -지 않다.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 (to become, to be possible) + -어
meaning: it works / is possible
notes: 돼 is a contraction of 되어. The entire phrase '말도 안 돼' is a very common idiom used to express disbelief, meaning 'No way,' 'That's ridiculous,' or 'That makes no sense.' Literally, 'It doesn't even become words.'

=== [46:47] 휴게실인데 2층 침대거든 ===
meaning: It's a break room, and it has a bunk bed, you see.
tone: Informal, explanatory, setting up a story.
--- word ---
term: 휴게실인데
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 휴게실 (break room) + -이다 (to be) + -ㄴ데 (connective)
meaning: It's a break room, and...
notes: The -(으)ㄴ데/는데 ending is a versatile connective that provides background information for the following clause. It can mean 'and,' 'but,' or 'so,' depending on the context. Here, it sets the scene for her story.
--- word ---
term: 2층
type: Noun
breakdown: 이 (two) + 층 (floor/layer counter)
meaning: second floor / double layer
notes: 층 is a counter for floors or layers. Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...) are used with it. '2층 침대' (two-layer bed) is the Korean term for a bunk bed.
--- word ---
term: 침대거든
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 침대 (bed) + -이다 (to be) + -거든 (explanatory ending)
meaning: it's a bed, you see.
notes: The -거든 ending provides a reason or explanation that the listener may not know. It adds a nuance of 'because...' or 'you see...' and is very common in conversational Korean. The speaker uses the informal -거든 instead of the polite -거든요.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -거든(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to provide a reason or background information, often with a 'you see...' or 'because...' nuance.
example: 밖에 비가 오거든. (It's raining outside, you see.)
level: Intermediate

=== [46:49] 누워서 잠깐 잠들었는데 ===
meaning: I lay down and briefly fell asleep, and then...
tone: Informal, narrative, building suspense.
--- word ---
term: 누워서
type: Verb
breakdown: 눕다 (to lie down) + -어서 (sequential connective)
meaning: lying down and...
notes: 눕다 is a ㅂ-irregular verb. When followed by a vowel, the ㅂ changes to 우. So, 눕 + -어서 becomes 누워서. The -아/어서 ending indicates a sequence of actions: first she lay down, then she fell asleep.
--- word ---
term: 잠깐
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: for a moment, briefly
notes: A common adverb. It can also be used as an interjection, '잠깐!' or '잠깐만요!', meaning 'Wait a moment!' or 'Excuse me!'
--- word ---
term: 잠들었는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 잠들다 (to fall asleep) + -었- (past tense) + -는데 (connective)
meaning: I fell asleep, and/but...
notes: 잠들다 is a compound verb from 잠 (sleep) + 들다 (to enter). The -는데 ending here connects the first event (falling asleep) to a subsequent, often contrasting or unexpected, event. It leaves the listener in suspense, waiting for what happened next.

notes: The base verb is 치다 (chida), which has many meanings like 'to hit' or 'to play'. The ending -더라고 (deorago) is used to state something the speaker personally witnessed or experienced in the past. It conveys a sense of recalling a past observation, often with a hint of mild surprise.
--- grammar ---

=== [47:10] 귀신들의 그 와우 포인트가 있어 ===

term: 귀신들의

breakdown: 귀신 + -들 + -의
meaning: of ghosts
notes: 귀신 (gwisin) means 'ghost'. -들 (-deul) is the plural marker. -의 (-ui) is the possessive particle, like 's in English. In spoken Korean, -의 is often pronounced as '에' (e) and is frequently omitted when the context is clear (e.g., 귀신들 와우 포인트).
--- word ---
term: 그
type: determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: 그 (geu) means 'that' and refers to something known to both the speaker and the listener, or something previously mentioned. It's one of the three basic demonstratives: 이 (i - this), 그 (geu - that), 저 (jeo - that over there).
--- word ---
term: 와우
type: noun (loanword)
breakdown: 
meaning: wow
notes: A direct borrowing from the English word 'wow'. Here it's used as a noun to describe an impressive or surprising element.
--- word ---

breakdown: 포인트 + -가
meaning: a point (is)
notes: 포인트 (pointeu) is a loanword from 'point'. The particle -가 (-ga) is a subject marker, used here because '포인트' ends in a vowel. If it ended in a consonant, -이 (-i) would be used.
--- word ---
term: 있어

breakdown: 있다 (to exist/have) + -어
meaning: there is / (they) have
notes: This is the informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of 있다 (itda). It's part of the 반말 (banmal) speech style Hyejin uses with her fans, indicating closeness.

=== [47:52] 이런 게 진짜 좀 ===
meaning: This kind of thing is really a bit...
tone: Informal, hesitant, trailing off. Expressing her feeling of fear.
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) conjugated
meaning: this kind of, like this
notes: 이런 (ireon) is the adjectival form of 이렇다 (ireota), meaning 'to be like this'. It's used to modify nouns. The set is: 이런 (ireon - like this), 그런 (geureon - like that), 저런 (jeoreon - like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 게
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 이
meaning: thing (as subject)
notes: 게 (ge) is a very common contraction of 것 + 이 (geot + i), which means 'thing' plus the subject particle. In spoken Korean, contractions like this are used almost exclusively over the full form.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) is a very common adverb used in casual speech to mean 'really'. It can also be used as a noun ('the real thing') or an interjection ('Really?').
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 조금 (jogeum) contraction
meaning: a bit, a little
notes: 좀 (jom) is a contraction of 조금 (jogeum). While it literally means 'a little', it's often used to soften a statement, request, or opinion, making it sound less direct or harsh. Here, it adds to the hesitant, trailing-off feel of the sentence.

=== [47:54] 이런 것도 되게 공포스러워 상상해보면 ===
meaning: This kind of thing is also really scary, if you imagine it.
tone: Informal, reflective. Explaining what she finds frightening.
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) conjugated
meaning: this kind of
notes: 이런 (ireon) modifies the following noun, specifying 'this kind of' thing. It's derived from the descriptive verb 이렇다 (ireota).
--- word ---
term: 것도
type: pronoun + particle (contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 도
meaning: thing also
notes: 것도 (geotdo) is a common spoken contraction of 것 + 도 (geot + do), meaning 'thing' plus the particle 도 (do), which means 'also' or 'too'.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really
notes: 되게 (doege) is a colloquial adverb meaning 'very' or 'extremely'. It's very common in casual conversation, often used interchangeably with other adverbs like 진짜 (jinjja) or 정말 (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 공포스러워
type: adjective
breakdown: 공포스럽다 + -어
meaning: is scary
notes: The suffix -스럽다 (-seureopda) attaches to some nouns to turn them into adjectives, meaning 'to be like' or 'to have the quality of'. 공포 (gongpo) is 'fear' or 'horror', so 공포스럽다 (gongposeureopda) means 'to be frightening/scary'.
--- word ---
term: 상상해보면
type: verb ending
breakdown: 상상하다 + -아/어 보다 + -(으)면
meaning: if you try to imagine
notes: This combines two grammar patterns. -아/어 보다 (-a/eo boda) means 'to try doing something'. -(으)면 (-(eu)myeon) means 'if' or 'when'. Together, they form 'if you try to...'. 상상하다 (sangsanghada) means 'to imagine'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -스럽다
meaning: A suffix that turns a noun into an adjective, meaning 'to be like' or 'to have the quality of' that noun.
example: 사랑 (love) -> 사랑스럽다 (to be lovely).
level: Intermediate

=== [47:57] 생각해봐 ===
meaning: Think about it.
tone: Informal, imperative. Directly engaging the audience.
--- word ---
term: 생각해봐
type: verb (imperative)
breakdown: 생각하다 + -아/어 보다
meaning: try thinking about it
notes: This is the informal imperative form of 생각하다 (saenggakhada - to think) combined with the pattern -아/어 보다 (-a/eo boda - to try doing). The resulting form, -아/어 봐 (-a/eo bwa), is a soft, friendly command or suggestion, like 'Go ahead and try...' or 'Why don't you...'. It's much less forceful than a direct command.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 봐(요)
meaning: An informal, friendly command or suggestion meaning 'Try doing V'. It's the imperative form of the '-아/어 보다' (to try doing) pattern.
example: 이거 한번 먹어봐. (Try eating this.)
level: Beginner

=== [48:03] 내가 이렇게 교실에 앉아서 ===
meaning: I'm sitting in the classroom like this, and...
tone: Informal, narrative, setting a scene.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + ~가 (subject particle)
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: 내가 is the subject form of 나 (I, informal). It's used when the speaker is the subject of the verb. It's often contrasted with 나는 (na-neun), which uses the topic particle ~는 and is used for introducing a topic or making a comparison. In casual speech, 내가 is very common.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + ~게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: like this, in this way
notes: This is one of a set of three adverbs: 이렇게 (ireoke, like this), 그렇게 (geureoke, like that, near the listener or previously mentioned), and 저렇게 (jeoreoke, like that, far from both speaker and listener). It's used to describe how an action is performed.
--- word ---
term: 교실에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 교실 (classroom) + ~에 (location particle)
meaning: in the classroom
notes: The particle ~에 is used to mark a static location where something exists or an event takes place. It's often confused with ~에서 (eseo), which marks the location where an action happens. For 'sitting in a classroom', both can be used, but ~에 emphasizes the state of being there.
--- word ---
term: 앉아서
type: Verb
breakdown: 앉다 (to sit) + ~아서 (connective ending)
meaning: sitting and...
notes: The ~아/어서 ending connects two clauses. It can indicate a sequence of events (A happens, then B happens) or a cause-and-effect relationship (because of A, B happens). Here, it's sequential: 'I sit, and then...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어서
meaning: A connective ending that links two clauses, indicating sequence ('and then') or reason ('so'/'because').
example: 배고파서 밥을 먹었어요. (I was hungry, so I ate.)
level: Beginner

=== [48:09] 수업을 듣다가 창문을 봤는데 ===
meaning: while listening to class, I looked at the window and...
tone: Informal, narrative, building suspense.
--- word ---
term: 수업을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 수업 (class) + ~을 (object particle)
meaning: class (as the object)
notes: The particle ~을/를 marks the direct object of a verb. Here, 'class' is the object of the verb 'to listen to' (듣다).
--- word ---
term: 듣다가
type: Verb
breakdown: 듣다 (to listen) + ~다가 (connective ending)
meaning: while listening...
notes: The ~다가 grammar pattern indicates that an action was in progress when it was interrupted by another action. The subject of both actions must be the same. It implies a switch from the first action to the second.
--- word ---
term: 창문을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 창문 (window) + ~을 (object particle)
meaning: the window (as the object)
notes: The particle ~을 marks 'window' as the direct object of the verb 'to see/look at' (보다).
--- word ---
term: 봤는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 (to see) + ~았~ (past tense) + ~는데 (connective ending)
meaning: I saw, and... / I saw, but...
notes: The ~는데/은데 ending is extremely common in spoken Korean. It's used to provide background information or context for the following clause. It can translate to 'and...', 'but...', or simply create a soft pause before the main point.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~다가
meaning: Indicates an interruption or shift of action. The action in the first clause is stopped or paused, and a new action begins.
example: 집에 가다가 친구를 만났어요. (On my way home, I met a friend.)
level: Intermediate

=== [48:13] 되게 비정상적으로 ===
meaning: abnormally...
tone: Informal, emphatic, descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really, extremely
notes: In colloquial speech, 되게 is frequently used as an intensifier, similar to 너무 (neomu), 정말 (jeongmal), or 아주 (aju). While its original meaning is related to 'becoming,' it has evolved to mean 'very' in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 비정상적으로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 비정상 (abnormality) + ~적 (suffix) + ~으로 (particle)
meaning: abnormally, in an abnormal way
notes: The suffix ~적 (jeok) turns a noun into an adjectival noun (like adding '-al' or '-ic' in English). Adding ~으로 (uro) then turns that into an adverb. So, 비정상 (abnormality) -> 비정상적 (abnormal) -> 비정상적으로 (abnormally).

=== [48:17] 키 큰 사람이 ===
meaning: a tall person
tone: Informal, descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 키
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: height
notes: To say someone is tall, you use the expression 키가 크다 (kiga keuda), which literally means 'height is big'. To say someone is short, you use 키가 작다 (kiga jakda), 'height is small'.
--- word ---
term: 큰
type: Adjective
breakdown: 크다 (to be big/tall) + ~ㄴ (adjective modifier form)
meaning: tall / big
notes: This is the modifier form of the adjective 크다. To use an adjective to describe a noun that follows it, you attach ~ㄴ (after a vowel) or ~은 (after a consonant) to the adjective stem.
--- word ---
term: 사람이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사람 (person) + ~이 (subject particle)
meaning: a person (as the subject)
notes: The subject particle ~이/가 is used to mark the subject of the sentence. ~이 is used after a consonant, and ~가 is used after a vowel. It often marks new information in the sentence.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective Stem + ~(으)ㄴ + Noun
meaning: This is the standard way to modify a noun with an adjective. The adjective comes before the noun it describes.
example: 예쁜 꽃 (a pretty flower); 작은 집 (a small house)
level: Beginner

=== [48:20] 서 있어 ===
meaning: is standing.
tone: Informal, descriptive, concluding the description.
--- word ---
term: 서
type: Verb Stem
breakdown: 서다 (to stand)
meaning: stand
notes: This is the stem of the verb 서다 (seoda), meaning 'to stand'. It's combined with 있다 to form a new grammatical structure.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 (to be/exist) + ~어 (informal present tense ending)
meaning: is/exists
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) present tense conjugation of 있다. The speaker, Hyejin, uses banmal when talking to her fans, the 'Twits'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuous state that results from a completed action. It describes the state of being 'in' a certain position.
example: 의자에 앉아 있어요. (I am sitting in the chair.) / 문이 열려 있어요. (The door is open.)

=== [48:23] 그런 거 너무 싫어 ===
meaning: I hate things like that so much.
tone: Informal, emphatic, expressing strong dislike.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be like that) + ~ㄴ (modifier form)
meaning: that kind of, like that
notes: 그런 modifies the following noun. It refers to something previously mentioned or understood from context. The set is 이런 (ireon, this kind of), 그런 (geureon, that kind of), 저런 (jeoreon, that kind of over there).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (colloquial)
breakdown: 것 (thing) -> 거
meaning: thing, stuff
notes: 거 is the common spoken contraction of the noun 것 (geot), which means 'thing'. In almost all casual conversation, you will hear 거 instead of 것.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, too, very
notes: Traditionally, 너무 (neomu) meant 'too much' in a negative sense. However, in modern Korean, it is widely used as a general intensifier for both positive and negative sentences, meaning 'very' or 'so'.
--- word ---
term: 싫어
type: Adjective
breakdown: 싫다 (to be dislikeable) + ~어 (informal present tense ending)
meaning: I dislike/hate
notes: 싫다 is an adjective, so the thing being disliked is the subject (e.g., '그것이 싫어' - That thing is dislikeable to me). This is different from the action verb 싫어하다 (to dislike), where the thing being disliked is the object (e.g., '나는 그것을 싫어해' - I dislike that thing). In casual speech, the distinction can be blurry.

=== [48:26] 일본 귀신이 더 무서워 ===
meaning: Japanese ghosts are scarier.
tone: Informal, comparative, stating an opinion.
--- word ---
term: 일본
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Japan
notes: When used before another noun, like 귀신 (ghost), it acts as a modifier, meaning 'Japanese'.
--- word ---
term: 귀신이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀신 (ghost) + ~이 (subject particle)
meaning: ghosts (as the subject)
notes: 귀신 (gwisin) refers to ghosts, spirits, or demons in Korean folklore and pop culture. The particle ~이 marks 'ghosts' as the subject of the adjective 'scary'.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more
notes: 더 is placed before an adjective or verb to make a comparison, equivalent to adding '-er' or 'more' in English. For example, 더 커요 (deo keoyo) means 'it's bigger'.
--- word ---
term: 무서워
type: Adjective
breakdown: 무섭다 (to be scary) + ~어 (informal present tense ending)
meaning: is scary
notes: This is an example of a 'ㅂ' irregular verb/adjective. When conjugating a stem ending in 'ㅂ' before a vowel, the 'ㅂ' changes to '우' (or '오' for some words like '돕다'). So, 무섭다 (museopda) becomes 무서워 (museowo).

breakdown: 맞다 (to be correct) + ~아요 (informal polite ending)

This switch to a polite ending often happens in livestreams when an idol reads a fan's comment from the chat and agrees with it, showing respect to the individual commenter.

=== [49:02] 근데 또 이렇게 뭔가 ===

tone: Informal, transitional, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: But, by the way
notes: A common contraction of 그런데. In conversation, it's frequently used to introduce a contrasting idea or to change the subject, similar to 'but' or 'anyway' in English.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Again, also
notes: Indicates repetition or adds another item/idea to the list. Here it suggests she's adding another thought to her previous statement.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this, in this way
notes: Formed from the descriptive verb 이렇다 (to be like this) and the adverbial suffix -게. It refers to a manner or state that is apparent to the speaker and listener.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 무엇 + -ㄴ가
meaning: Something
notes: A contraction of 무엇인가. It refers to an unspecified thing or feeling. Speakers often use it as a filler word when they are trying to articulate a vague thought, much like 'like' or 'kind of' in English.

=== [49:04] 가족들이 지켜주는 듯한 느낌도 있고 ===
meaning: There's also a feeling like my family is protecting me,
tone: Informal, reflective, continuing a thought.
--- word ---
term: 가족들이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 가족 + -들 + -이
meaning: Family members
notes: 가족 (family) with the plural marker -들 and the subject particle -이. While -들 is often omitted in Korean if the context is clear, its use here emphasizes that she's thinking of multiple family members.
--- word ---
term: 지켜주는
type: Verb
breakdown: 지키다 + -어 주다 + -는
meaning: that protects (for me)
notes: This combines 지키다 (to protect) with the auxiliary verb -어/아 주다, which adds the nuance that the action is done for someone's benefit. The -는 ending turns the entire verb phrase into a present-tense adjective modifying the noun '느낌' (feeling).
--- word ---
term: 듯한
type: Grammatical ending
breakdown: -는 듯하다 + -ㄴ
meaning: like, as if
notes: This is part of the '-는/은 듯하다' pattern, which expresses a feeling or assumption. Here, '지켜주는 듯한' modifies '느낌' to mean 'a feeling as if they are protecting me'.
--- word ---
term: 느낌도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 느낌 + -도
meaning: a feeling too
notes: 느낌 means 'feeling' or 'sensation'. The particle -도 means 'also' or 'too', adding this idea to a previous one.
--- word ---
term: 있고
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -고
meaning: there is, and...
notes: The verb 있다 (to exist, to have) is connected to the next clause with the -고 ending, which means 'and' or 'and then'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -는/은 듯하다
meaning: Expresses a guess, appearance, or feeling that something is the case. Translates to 'it seems like...' or 'it feels as if...'.
example: 밖에 비가 오는 듯해요. (It seems like it's raining outside.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 주다
meaning: An auxiliary verb construction indicating that the action is performed for the benefit of someone else.
example: 친구가 숙제를 도와줬어요. (My friend helped me with my homework.)
level: Beginner

=== [49:08] 뭔지 알지? ===
meaning: You know what I mean, right?
tone: Informal, seeking agreement, conversational tic.
--- word ---
term: 뭔지
type: Pronoun + verb ending
breakdown: 무엇 + -인지
meaning: what it is
notes: A common contraction of 무엇인지. It's an embedded question form. 무엇 (what) + 이다 (to be) + -ㄴ지 (ending for embedded questions).
--- word ---
term: 알지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -지?
meaning: you know, right?
notes: 알다 (to know) + -지? (informal sentence ending). This ending is used to confirm information the speaker assumes the listener already knows. It creates a friendly, inclusive tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek agreement or confirm something the speaker believes is mutually known. The informal version -지? is extremely common in conversations between friends.
example: 이 영화 재미있지? (This movie is fun, right?)
level: Beginner

meaning: that is a spike

meaning: is it the case that...?

=== [49:25] 안 돼 지금 자면 안 되는데 ===

breakdown: 
meaning: Not
notes: The short-form negator in Korean. It is placed directly before the verb or adjective it negates. The long form is -지 않다.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다
meaning: it's okay
notes: The verb 되다 means 'to become', but in this context, it means 'to be okay' or 'to be allowed'. 안 돼 is a very common phrase meaning 'No way', 'Don't', or 'It's not allowed'.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: Refers to the current moment in time. It is often compared with 이제, which also means 'now' but implies a change from a previous state (e.g., 'from now on').
--- word ---
term: 자면
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 + -면
meaning: If I sleep
notes: The verb 자다 (to sleep) is combined with the conditional ending -(으)면, which means 'if' or 'when'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Not
notes: The short-form negator, used here again to negate the second 되다.
--- word ---
term: 되는데
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -는데
meaning: ...it's not allowed, but...
notes: The -는데 ending provides background information or implies a contrast. By ending the sentence here, the speaker leaves the consequence unsaid, implying '...but I'm so sleepy'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)면 안 되다
meaning: A grammar pattern used to express prohibition. It literally means 'if one does (verb), it is not okay'.
example: 수업 시간에 자면 안 돼요. (You shouldn't sleep during class.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -는데/은데
meaning: A connective ending that can provide background context, show contrast, or soften a statement. When used at the end of a sentence, it often creates a trailing-off effect, implying more to be said.
example: 배고픈데 먹을 거 있어요? (I'm hungry, is there anything to eat?)

=== [50:07] 조식 먹고 자 ===
meaning: Eat breakfast and then sleep.
tone: Informal, reading a fan's suggestion aloud.
--- word ---
term: 조식
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Breakfast
notes: A Sino-Korean word (朝食) often used in formal settings like hotels or restaurants. The more common, native Korean word is '아침' (achim) or '아침밥' (achim-bap).
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: Eat and (then)
notes: The -고 ending connects two actions, indicating that the first action (eating) is completed before the second one (sleeping) begins. It's a fundamental connective ending for listing actions in sequence.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다
meaning: Sleep
notes: This is the imperative (command) form in the casual '반말' (banmal) speech style, made by dropping -다 from the verb stem. She is reading a fan's direct suggestion to her.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고
meaning: Connects two verbs or clauses to mean 'and' or 'and then', indicating a sequence of actions.
example: 영화를 보고 저녁을 먹었어요. (I watched a movie and then ate dinner.)
level: Beginner

=== [50:16] 아 조식을 먹고 자라고? ===
meaning: Ah, (you're telling me to) eat breakfast and then sleep?
tone: Informal, questioning, confirming a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah
notes: Used here to show she has just understood the fan's previous comment. It signals a moment of realization.
--- word ---
term: 조식을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 조식 + -을
meaning: Breakfast (as object)
notes: The particle -을 marks '조식' (breakfast) as the direct object of the verb '먹다' (to eat). -을 is used after a noun ending in a consonant, while -를 is used after a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: Eat and
notes: The connective ending -고 links the two verbs, '먹다' (to eat) and '자다' (to sleep), in sequence.
--- word ---
term: 자라고?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 자다 + -(으)라고?
meaning: (You're telling me to) sleep?
notes: This is an indirect quotation for a command, used as a question to confirm what was said. The full form is '자라고 하다' (to say 'sleep!'). Here, it's used to mean 'Are you telling me to sleep?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)라고(요)?
meaning: An indirect quotation ending used to confirm a command or request one has heard. It translates to 'Did you say to...?' or 'Are you telling me to...?'.
example: 이거 다 먹으라고? (You're telling me to eat all of this?)
level: Intermediate

=== [50:18] 지금 잔다면 ===
meaning: If I sleep now...
tone: Informal, hypothetical, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: Refers to this specific point in time. Learners often confuse it with '이제' (ije), which also means 'now' but implies a change from the past or a new beginning (e.g., 'From now on...').
--- word ---
term: 잔다면
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 자다 + -ㄴ다면
meaning: If I sleep
notes: A conditional ending used for hypothetical or unlikely situations. It carries a stronger 'what if' nuance than the more common conditional '-(으)면'. The sentence is left unfinished, implying she is thinking about the consequences.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(ㄴ)다면 / A-다면
meaning: A conditional ending for 'if', used to talk about hypothetical or counterfactual situations. It's often used for things that are unlikely to happen.
example: 로또에 당첨된다면 집을 살 거예요. (If I won the lottery, I would buy a house.)
level: Intermediate

=== [50:33] 근데 여기서 계속 이 시간에 잠들긴 했어 ===
meaning: But I have been falling asleep around this time here.
tone: Informal, explanatory, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: But / However
notes: A very common contraction of '그런데'. In spoken Korean, '근데' is used far more frequently to introduce a contrasting idea or to change the subject.
--- word ---
term: 여기서
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 여기 + -에서
meaning: Here (at this place)
notes: The particle '-에서' marks the location where an action takes place. It is often shortened to '-서' in casual speech, as seen here.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Continuously / Keep
notes: Indicates that an action or state has been persisting over a period of time.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: This
notes: Used before a noun to indicate something close to the speaker. The set is 이 (this), 그 (that, near listener), 저 (that, over there).
--- word ---
term: 시간에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 시간 + -에
meaning: At this time
notes: The particle '-에' is attached to nouns of time to indicate a specific point when something happens.
--- word ---
term: 잠들긴
type: Verb + Particle
breakdown: 잠들다 + -기는
meaning: Fall asleep (indeed)
notes: A contraction of '잠들기는'. The '-기는 하다' pattern (often shortened to '-긴 하다') is used to acknowledge a fact, often before introducing a contrasting point. It means 'It's true that I fell asleep...'
--- word ---
term: 했어
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -었- + -어
meaning: did
notes: The informal, past-tense ending for '하다' (to do). It completes the '-긴 하다' grammar pattern, making it '잠들긴 했어'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-긴 하다
meaning: Used to acknowledge or admit a point, often with an implied contrast. Translates to 'It is true that... but...' or 'I did... but...'.
example: 음식이 맛있긴 한데 너무 비싸요. (The food is indeed delicious, but it's too expensive.)
level: Intermediate

=== [50:58] 다 쉬나요? ===
meaning: Is everyone resting?
tone: Informal, questioning.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: all, everyone
notes: This adverb can be used to refer to all people in a group ('everyone') or all items ('everything'). It's a very common and versatile word in both spoken and written Korean.
--- word ---
term: 쉬나요?
type: Verb
breakdown: 쉬다 + -나요?
meaning: are you resting?
notes: The `-나요?` ending is an informal and gentle way to ask a question. It sounds softer and less direct than the standard `-아요/어요?` ending. It's often used when asking something you don't know, creating a friendly and curious tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + -나요?
meaning: A gentle and soft-sounding question ending, used in informal but polite speech.
example: 지금 비가 오나요? (Is it raining now?)

=== [51:10] 이번 추석 되게 길겠다 그치? ===
meaning: This Chuseok holiday must be really long, right?
tone: Informal, observational, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 이번
type: Determiner
breakdown: 이 + 번
meaning: this time
notes: Literally 'this occasion'. It's used to refer to the current or upcoming instance of a recurring event, like a holiday, exam, or vacation. For example, `이번 주` (this week), `이번 시험` (this exam).
--- word ---
term: 추석
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Chuseok
notes: Chuseok is a major Korean harvest festival, similar to Thanksgiving. It's a multi-day national holiday where families gather, honor ancestors, and share food.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really
notes: A colloquial adverb used to intensify adjectives or other adverbs. It's very common in spoken Korean and is similar in meaning to `아주` or `매우`, but has a more casual feel.
--- word ---
term: 길겠다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 길다 + -겠다
meaning: it must be long
notes: The `-겠다` suffix here expresses the speaker's supposition or guess based on available information. It's not a future tense marker but rather indicates conjecture, like 'I guess...' or 'It must be...'. For example, `맛있겠다` (That looks delicious).
--- word ---
term: 그치?
type: Tag question
breakdown: 그렇지?
meaning: right?
notes: A very common contraction of `그렇지?` (isn't that so?). It's used at the end of a sentence to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener, similar to 'right?' or 'you know?' in English. It's a key feature of informal, conversational Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -겠다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's assumption, guess, or supposition about a situation.
example: 피곤하겠다. (You must be tired.)
level: Intermediate

=== [51:13] 한국 일요일이구나 ===
meaning: Oh, it's Sunday in Korea.
tone: Informal, expressing realization.
--- word ---
term: 한국
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Korea
notes: Refers to South Korea (대한민국). North Korea is typically referred to as `북한` (Bukhan).
--- word ---
term: 일요일이구나
type: Noun + Copula ending
breakdown: 일요일 + 이다 + -구나
meaning: oh, it's Sunday!
notes: The `-구나` ending is used to express a new realization, surprise, or discovery. It's like saying 'Oh, I see that...' or 'So it's...'. For nouns ending in a consonant, `-이구나` is used. For nouns ending in a vowel, it's just `-구나` (e.g., `의자구나` - Oh, it's a chair!).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)구나
meaning: An exclamatory ending used when the speaker realizes something for the first time.
example: 이 사람이 그 가수구나! (Oh, so this person is that singer!)
level: Beginner

=== [51:38] 미안 나는 이제 일어난 청희들에게 ===
meaning: Sorry, to the Twits who just woke up.
tone: Informal, apologetic, trailing sentence.
--- word ---
term: 미안
type: Noun / Adjective root
breakdown: 
meaning: Sorry
notes: The most casual way to apologize, used between close friends. It's the root of the verb `미안하다` (to be sorry). Using it alone is very informal, similar to 'my bad'.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -는
meaning: I
notes: `나` is the informal pronoun for 'I', used when speaking in `반말` (informal language). `-는` is the topic particle, indicating that 'I' is the topic of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: `이제` means 'now' but often implies a change of state or 'from this point on'. It's different from `지금` (jigeum), which means 'right now'. Here, it implies 'now that you have woken up', as opposed to before.
--- word ---
term: 일어난
type: Verb (Adnominal form)
breakdown: 일어나다 + -(으)ㄴ
meaning: who woke up
notes: This is a relative clause modifying the next noun. The `-(으)ㄴ` ending attaches to a verb stem to turn it into a past-tense adjective, describing a noun with an action that has already happened. `일어난 청희들` means 'the Twits who (have) woken up'.
--- word ---
term: 청희들에게
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 청희 + -들 + -에게
meaning: to the Twits
notes: `청희` is the fandom name. `-들` is the plural marker. The particle `-에게` means 'to' and is used for people and animals. For objects, you would use `-에`.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄴ + Noun
meaning: Creates a past-tense relative clause to modify a noun. It describes a noun with an action that has already been completed.
example: 어제 먹은 빵 (the bread I ate yesterday)
level: Intermediate

meaning: now

=== [52:01] 알았어 물 마셔볼게 ===
meaning: Okay, I'll try drinking some water.
tone: Informal, agreeable, responsive.
--- word ---
term: 알았어
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -았어
meaning: Okay / Got it
notes: Literally 'I knew' or 'I understood', but it's a very common conversational phrase to show acknowledgement and agreement, like 'Okay', 'Alright', or 'I get it'.
--- word ---
term: 물
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: water
notes: 
--- word ---
term: 마셔볼게
type: Verb
breakdown: 마시다 + -어 보다 + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I'll try drinking
notes: This combines two grammar patterns. `V-어/아 보다` means 'to try doing V'. `V-(으)ㄹ게` is an informal ending expressing the speaker's intention or will, often as a response to someone. So, `마셔볼게` means 'I will try drinking it (as you suggested)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -어/아 보다
meaning: Indicates trying or experiencing an action. It adds the nuance of 'giving something a shot'.
example: 이 옷을 입어 보세요. (Try on these clothes.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or decision, often as a promise or reaction to the listener. It can only be used for first-person subjects in statements.
example: 내가 먼저 갈게. (I'll go first.)
level: Intermediate

=== [52:03] 지금 들어온 사람들은 정말 아리송하겠다 ===
meaning: People who just came in must be really confused.
tone: Informal, speculative, self-aware.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 지금 (jigeum) means 'right now, at this very moment'. It's often compared to 이제 (ije), which means 'now' in the sense of 'from this point forward' or indicates a change from the past. For example, '지금 바빠요' (I'm busy right now) vs. '이제 갈게요' (I'll get going now).
--- word ---
term: 들어온
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 들어오다 + -ㄴ
meaning: who came in
notes: This is the past tense adnominal (or modifier) form of 들어오다 (deureooda, 'to come in'). The ending -(으)ㄴ attaches to a verb to turn it into an adjective that describes a noun with a completed action. For example, '어제 먹은 빵' (the bread I ate yesterday).
--- word ---
term: 사람들은
type: Noun + particles
breakdown: 사람 + -들 + -은
meaning: people (as for)
notes: 사람 (saram) means 'person'. -들 (-deul) is the plural marker. -은 (-eun) is the topic particle, indicating that 'people who just came in' is the topic of the sentence. It often implies a contrast that isn't explicitly stated (e.g., 'as for the new people... unlike those who were already here').
--- word ---
term: 정말
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: 정말 (jeongmal) is a common adverb used to add emphasis, similar to 'very' or 'really'. It can be used interchangeably with 진짜 (jinjja) in most casual conversations, though 진짜 can feel slightly more colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 아리송하겠다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 아리송하다 + -겠다
meaning: must be confusing/puzzling
notes: 아리송하다 (arisonghada) means 'to be confusing, puzzling, or vague'. The ending -겠다 (-getda) is used here to express the speaker's supposition or assumption about someone else's state of mind. It's like saying 'I bet they are...' or 'They must be...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -겠다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's supposition, assumption, or guess about a situation or someone's feelings.
example: 그 소식을 들으면 부모님이 정말 좋아하시겠다. (Your parents must be so happy if they hear that news.)
level: Intermediate

=== [52:11] 갑자기 뭔가에 취해 있는 표정으로 ===

--- word ---
term: 갑자기
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: suddenly
notes: 갑자기 (gapjagi) means 'suddenly' or 'all of a sudden'. It's used to describe an abrupt, unexpected change or action.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가에
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 뭔가 + -에
meaning: on/by something
notes: 뭔가 (mwonga) is a contraction of 무엇인가 (mueosinga) and means 'something'. The particle -에 (-e) is used here to indicate the cause or source of the state of 'being drunk on'.
--- word ---
term: 취해
type: Verb
breakdown: 취하다 + -아/어
meaning: being drunk/intoxicated
