--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 있다 + -는
meaning: that is (in a state of)
notes: This is the present tense adnominal form of 있다 (itda, 'to exist'). Combined with the previous verb, '취해 있는' describes the state of 'being drunk' and modifies the following noun, 표정 (pyojeong, 'facial expression').
--- word ---
term: 표정으로
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 표정 + -으로
meaning: with an expression
notes: 표정 (pyojeong) means 'facial expression'. The particle -(으)로 indicates the means, method, or tool by which something is done. Here, it means 'with an expression' or 'by means of an expression'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuing state that resulted from a completed action. It's often used with intransitive verbs.
example: 창문이 열려 있어요. (The window is open.)
level: Intermediate

=== [52:14] 아까까지만 해도 멀쩡했어 오해하지 마 ===
meaning: I was perfectly fine until just a moment ago, so don't misunderstand.
tone: Informal, defensive, reassuring.
--- word ---
term: 아까까지만
type: Noun + particles
breakdown: 아까 + -까지 + -만
meaning: only until a moment ago
notes: A combination of three parts: 아까 (akka, 'a little while ago'), -까지 (-kkaji, 'until'), and -만 (-man, 'only'). This emphasizes the very recent change in her state.
--- word ---
term: 해도
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -아도
meaning: even so
notes: This is part of the set phrase 'N까지만 해도', which means 'up until just N' or 'even just N'. It's used to contrast a recent past situation with the present. For example, '어제까지만 해도 괜찮았는데...' (I was fine up until yesterday, but...).
--- word ---
term: 멀쩡했어
type: Adjective
breakdown: 멀쩡하다 + -았어
meaning: was fine/normal
notes: 멀쩡하다 (meoljjeonghada) means 'to be fine, intact, or sober'. The -았어 (-asseo) ending is the informal, non-polite past tense conjugation, used with friends or people younger than you. The speaker uses this casual 반말 (banmal) with her fans.
--- word ---
term: 오해하지
type: Verb
breakdown: 오해하다 + -지
meaning: misunderstand
notes: 오해하다 (ohaehada) means 'to misunderstand'. The -지 (-ji) is the stem of the negative command form '-지 마'.
--- word ---
term: 마
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 
meaning: don't
notes: The ending -지 마 (-ji ma) is the informal negative command, used to tell someone not to do something. The polite version is -지 마세요 (-ji maseyo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -지 마
meaning: An informal negative command used to tell someone not to do something. It's a form of 반말 (banmal), or casual speech.
example: 너무 걱정하지 마. (Don't worry too much.)
level: Beginner

=== [52:17] 아까까지는 굉장히 ===
meaning: Until a moment ago, I was very...
tone: Informal, trailing off, emphatic.
--- word ---
term: 아까까지는
type: Noun + particles
breakdown: 아까 + -까지 + -는
meaning: until a moment ago (as for)
notes: 아까 (akka) means 'a little while ago'. -까지 (-kkaji) means 'until'. The topic particle -는 (-neun) is added for emphasis or contrast, highlighting the time frame 'until a moment ago' as the topic.
--- word ---
term: 굉장히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, extremely
notes: 굉장히 (goengjanghi) is an intensifier meaning 'very, greatly, extremely'. It's often used in both spoken and written Korean and is similar to 아주 (aju) or 정말 (jeongmal).

=== [52:21] 아니야 아까까지는 멀쩡했잖아 ===
meaning: No, I was fine until a moment ago, you know.
tone: Informal, defensive, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 아니야
type: Copula (negative)
breakdown: 아니다 + -야
meaning: No / It's not
notes: The informal, non-polite way to say 'no' or 'it is not'. It's the negative counterpart to 이야/야 (iya/ya). The polite form is 아니에요 (anieyo).
--- word ---
term: 아까까지는
type: Noun + particles
breakdown: 아까 + -까지 + -는
meaning: until a moment ago (as for)
notes: 아까 (akka) means 'a little while ago'. -까지 (-kkaji) means 'until'. The topic particle -는 (-neun) adds contrast, implying '...but now I'm not'.
--- word ---
term: 멀쩡했잖아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 멀쩡하다 + -았잖아
meaning: was fine, you know
notes: The sentence ending -잖아 (-janha) is used to state a fact that the listener should already know or agree with. It can be translated as 'you know', 'as you know', or 'come on'. It creates a sense of shared context or gentle reminding.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -잖아(요)
meaning: Used to state something that the listener is or should be aware of. It can be used to remind, gently correct, or emphasize an obvious fact.
example: 우리 어제 만났잖아. (We met yesterday, you know.)
level: Intermediate

=== [52:40] 너희들 왜 그래 ===
meaning: Why are you guys like that?
tone: Informal, playful, complaining.
--- word ---
term: 너희들
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 너희 + -들
meaning: you guys
notes: 너희 (neohui) is the plural form of 'you'. The plural marker -들 (-deul) is often added for emphasis, though 너희 is already plural. This is used when speaking casually to a group of friends or people younger than the speaker.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: why
notes: 왜 (wae) is the standard question word for 'why'. It's used to ask for a reason or cause.
--- word ---
term: 그래
type: Verb
breakdown: 그렇다 + -아/어
meaning: are like that
notes: This is the informal present tense conjugation of 그렇다 (geureota), which means 'to be so' or 'to be like that'. '왜 그래?' is a very common phrase meaning 'What's wrong?' or 'Why are you being like this?'

=== [52:45] 아 이렇게 누우면 안 되는데 ===
meaning: Ah, I shouldn't lie down like this...
tone: Informal, self-talk, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah
notes: A common interjection expressing a range of emotions like realization, surprise, or mild frustration.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: like this
notes: 이렇게 (ireoke) means 'in this way' or 'like this'. It's part of a set with 그렇게 (geureoke, 'like that') and 저렇게 (jeoreoke, 'like that over there').
--- word ---
term: 누우면
type: Verb
breakdown: 눕다 + -(으)면
meaning: if (I) lie down
notes: This shows the conjugation of a 'ㅂ' irregular verb. The 'ㅂ' in the stem of 눕다 (nupda, 'to lie down') changes to '우' before a vowel ending like -(으)면 (-(eu)myeon, 'if/when'). So, 눕 + 으면 becomes 누우면 (nuumyeon).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is used for short-form negation and is placed directly before a verb or adjective. The alternative is the long-form negation using '-지 않다' (e.g., 눕지 않으면).
--- word ---
term: 되는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 + -는데
meaning: it's not okay, but...
notes: 되다 (doeda) here means 'to be okay' or 'permissible'. The ending -는데 (-neunde) provides background information or implies a contrast. The sentence trails off, suggesting a following thought like '...but I'm so sleepy'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)면 안 되다
meaning: Expresses prohibition, meaning 'one should not' or 'it is not allowed'.
example: 수업 시간에 자면 안 돼요. (You shouldn't sleep during class.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -는데/-(으)ㄴ데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background context for the following clause. It can also show a soft contrast between two clauses.
example: 날씨가 좋은데 공원에 갈까요? (The weather is nice, so shall we go to the park?)
level: Intermediate

=== [52:47] 그래서 다들 이제 오늘 뭐 하려고? ===
meaning: So, what is everyone planning to do today?
tone: Informal, conversational, changing the subject.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, therefore
notes: 그래서 (geuraeseo) connects two clauses based on a cause-and-effect relationship. It's also commonly used to start a sentence when changing the topic or moving the conversation forward, like 'So...' or 'In that case...'.
--- word ---
term: 다들
type: Noun/Adverb
breakdown: 다 + -들
meaning: everyone
notes: 다 (da) means 'all'. While -들 (-deul) is typically a plural marker for nouns, 다들 (dadeul) is a very common colloquial term for 'everyone' or 'everybody'.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now', but often with a sense of 'from this point on' or a change from a previous state. It's subtly different from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right at this moment'. Here, it's used to transition to a new topic.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: 오늘 (oneul) is the noun for 'today'.
--- word ---
term: 뭐
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: what
notes: 뭐 (mwo) is the common spoken contraction of 무엇 (mueot), meaning 'what'.
--- word ---
term: 하려고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -(으)려고
meaning: planning to do?
notes: This is a shortened form of the grammar pattern '-(으)려고 하다' which expresses intention or a plan. In casual questions, the final 하다 (hada) is often dropped. The full question would be '뭐 하려고 해?' (mwo haryeogo hae?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)려고 하다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or plan to do something. It translates to 'plan to', 'intend to', or 'am going to'.
example: 이번 주말에 책을 읽으려고 해요. (I plan to read a book this weekend.)
level: Intermediate

=== [53:10] 이거 너무 웃긴다 ===

--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: This thing, this
notes: A common contraction of '이것' (this thing). In spoken Korean, '이거' (this), '그거' (that), and '저거' (that over there) are used far more frequently than their full forms.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So, very
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too' or 'excessively', in modern colloquial Korean, '너무' is widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in positive contexts, similar to '아주' or '정말'. For example, '너무 예뻐요' (so pretty).
--- word ---
term: 웃긴다
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 + -ㄴ다
meaning: is funny
notes: The plain form declarative ending '-ㄴ다/-는다' is used with action verbs. While it can sound neutral or narrative (like in a diary), in conversation it's often used to express a realization, exclamation, or to talk to oneself. It's part of the 'haera-che' (해라체) speech level.

=== [53:16] 라이브 켜고 청희한테 안 잔다고 이상하다 하기 ===
meaning: Turn on the live, tell the Twits you're not sleeping, and say it's weird.
tone: Reading a fan's summary of her actions aloud.
--- word ---
term: 라이브
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Live (stream)
notes: A loanword from English, referring to the live broadcast. The speaker is reading a fan's comment that summarizes the events of the stream so far.
--- word ---
term: 켜고
type: Verb
breakdown: 켜다 + -고
meaning: turn on and...
notes: The verb '켜다' means 'to turn on' (lights, electronics). The '-고' ending is a versatile connector that links clauses, often indicating a sequence of actions ('do A and then do B').
--- word ---
term: 청희한테
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 청희 + -한테
meaning: to the Twits
notes: '-한테' is a colloquial particle meaning 'to' or 'from' a person or animal. It's the spoken equivalent of the more written-form particle '에게'. '청희' is the name of the speaker's fandom.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Not
notes: A short-form negator placed directly before a verb or adjective. It's generally interchangeable with the long-form negation '-지 않다' (e.g., '안 자다' is the same as '자지 않다', to not sleep).
--- word ---
term: 잔다고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 자다 + -ㄴ다고
meaning: that (you) sleep
notes: This is part of the indirect quotation form '-ㄴ다고/는다고 하다' (to say that...). Here, the final '하다' (to say/do) is omitted, which is common in lists or summaries. The full phrase would be '안 잔다고 하기' (to say that you're not sleeping).
--- word ---
term: 이상하다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 
meaning: to be strange/weird
notes: This is the dictionary form of the adjective. It's used here as part of a list of actions, linked to the final '하기'.
--- word ---
term: 하기
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -기
meaning: doing/saying
notes: The suffix '-기' is a nominalizer; it turns a verb or adjective into a noun. It's often used to create lists of actions, like in this fan's summary comment: 'A 하기, B 하기, C 하기' (Doing A, doing B, doing C).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -고
meaning: Connects two clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It indicates a sequence of actions.
example: 밥을 먹고 학교에 가요. (I eat and then go to school.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(ㄴ/는)다고 (하다)
meaning: Used for quoting a declarative statement (what someone said). The final '하다' (to say) is often omitted in casual speech or lists.
example: 친구가 예쁘다고 했어요. (My friend said it's pretty.)
level: Intermediate

=== [53:38] 이거 아는 사람? ===

tone: Casual, asking a question to her audience.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun (colloquial)
breakdown: 이것
meaning: This thing
notes: 이거 (igeo) is the common spoken form of 이것 (igeot). In casual conversation, the final 'ㅅ' is almost always dropped.
--- word ---
term: 아는
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 알다 + -는
meaning: that knows
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of the verb 알다 (alda), 'to know.' The ending -는 turns the verb into a modifier that describes the following noun, 사람 (saram).

term: 사람?
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Person?
notes: The sentence is a fragment, which is very common in informal speech. A more complete sentence would be '이거 아는 사람 있어요?' (Is there anyone who knows this?). The rising intonation on 사람 turns the statement into a question.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Action Verb + -는 + Noun
meaning: A grammar pattern used to describe a noun with a verb in the present tense. It means 'the noun that is V-ing' or 'the noun who V-s'.
example: 지금 책을 읽는 사람 (The person who is reading a book now).

=== [53:48] 아는구나 ===

term: 유행
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: trend, fashion
notes: Refers to something that is currently popular, whether in fashion, culture, or general interest. It can be used as a noun or as part of a verb, 유행하다 (to be in fashion, to be popular).
--- word ---
term: 중인데?
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 중 (in the middle of) + -이다 (to be) + -는데 (connective ending)
meaning: is in the middle of..., but?/and?
notes: The pattern '-는 중이다' means 'to be in the middle of doing something.' The ending -는데 is very common in spoken Korean. Here, it's used at the end of a question to add a sense of curiosity or to provide a soft, conversational tone, rather than asking abruptly.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 중이다
meaning: Indicates that an action or state is currently in progress. Literally 'in the middle of (noun)'.
example: 지금 회의 중이에요. (I'm in a meeting right now.)
level: Beginner

=== [54:28] 뭐가? ===
meaning: What is?
tone: Informal, questioning. A quick follow-up for clarification.
--- word ---
term: 뭐가?
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 뭐 (what) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: What?
notes: A simple, direct question asking for the subject. It's the most basic way to ask 'What?' when you want to know what someone is referring to. This is very common in casual conversation (반말, banmal).

breakdown: 
meaning: Wow

=== [54:36] 시작됐다 하품지옥 ===
meaning: The yawn-hell has begun.
tone: Informal, humorous, self-deprecating. A playful exaggeration.
--- word ---
term: 시작됐다
type: Verb
breakdown: 시작되다 (to be started) + -었- (past tense) + -다 (declarative ending)
meaning: It has started/begun
notes: 시작되다 is the passive form of 시작하다 (to start). It means 'to be started' or 'to begin'. The -ㅆ다 ending indicates a completed action in the past. This form is common for announcing that an event has commenced.
--- word ---
term: 하품지옥
type: Noun
breakdown: 하품 (yawn) + 지옥 (hell)
meaning: yawn hell
notes: A creative, colloquial compound noun. The speaker humorously combines 'yawn' and 'hell' to describe a situation where she can't stop yawning. This kind of playful word creation is common in casual online contexts.

=== [54:40] 홍콩에서 지금 뭐가 유행이야? ===
meaning: What's trending in Hong Kong right now?
tone: Informal, direct question. Following up on a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 홍콩에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 홍콩 (Hong Kong) + -에서 (location particle)
meaning: in Hong Kong
notes: The particle -에서 marks a location where an action takes place. It's often translated as 'in', 'at', or 'from'. It's different from -에, which typically marks a destination or static location.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 지금 refers to the present moment, 'right now'. It's often confused with 이제, but 이제 means 'now' in the sense of a change from the past (e.g., 'From now on...'). 지금 is for the immediate present.
--- word ---
term: 뭐가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 뭐 (what) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: What (as the subject)
notes: 뭐 is the casual contraction of 무엇 (what). The particle -가 marks it as the subject of the verb 'to be trending' (유행이다).
--- word ---
term: 유행이야?
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 유행 (trend) + -이다 (to be) + -이야? (casual question ending)
meaning: is it a trend?
notes: -이야? is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) question form of -이에요?. It's used with friends or people younger than you. Since '유행' ends in a consonant, -이야 is used. If the noun ended in a vowel, -야 would be used (e.g., '사과야? - Is it an apple?').

=== [54:56] 베이블레이드는 뭔가요? ===
meaning: What is Beyblade?
tone: Informal-polite, questioning. A slightly softer, more curious way of asking.
--- word ---
term: 베이블레이드는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 베이블레이드 (Beyblade) + -는 (topic particle)
meaning: As for Beyblade...
notes: The topic particle -은/는 introduces 'Beyblade' as the main subject of the question. Using it here helps to clearly define what she is asking about.

--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ가요?
meaning: A polite and soft question ending used with adjectives and 이다/아니다. It expresses gentle curiosity or inquiry.

=== [54:59] 탑블레이드 아니야? ===

meaning: 2nd generation
notes: A compound word using the Sino-Korean number '이' (i) for 'two' and '세대' (sedae) for 'generation'. Sino-Korean numbers are often used for counting generations, floors, minutes, etc.

breakdown: 가
meaning: (subject marker)

breakdown: 있다 + -어?
meaning: Is there? / Does it exist?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite question form of the verb '있다' (itda), which means 'to exist' or 'to have'. The polite form would be '있어요?' (isseoyo?). This casual form is called '반말' (banmal).

meaning: do not do

'혼나다' is the passive form of '혼내다' (to scold someone).

=== [56:21] 구매줄이 매우 길다고? ===

=== [56:28] 말도 안 돼 ===
meaning: No way.
tone: Informal, exclamatory, disbelieving.
--- word ---
term: 말도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 말 (words/speech) + -도 (also/even)
meaning: even words
notes: The particle '-도' (do) means 'also' or 'too,' but in this idiomatic phrase, it adds emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: '안' is a short adverb used before verbs or adjectives to negate them. It's a common and simple way to form negative sentences, e.g., '안 가요' (I'm not going).
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 (to become/to be possible) + -어 (present tense conjugation)
meaning: it becomes / it works
notes: '돼' is the present tense, informal (해체, haeche) conjugation of '되다'. The phrase '안 돼' literally means 'it doesn't become' and is used to mean 'no' or 'don't'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 말도 안 되다
meaning: An idiom meaning 'That's nonsense,' 'No way,' or 'That's ridiculous.' It literally translates to 'it doesn't even make sense.'
example: 그가 복권에 당첨됐대. 말도 안 돼! (They said he won the lottery. No way!)

meaning: That's ridiculous.

term: 말도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 말 (words/speech) + -도 (also/even)

=== [57:34] 미치겠다 ===

tone: Informal, exasperated, humorous.
--- word ---

breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: Indicates that an action is done well or to a good degree.

breakdown: 데우다 + -어지다
meaning: is heated up
notes: The base verb is 데우다 (to heat something up). The -어지다 ending creates a passive form, meaning 'to become heated' or 'to be heated'.
--- word ---

type: Verb + Modifier
breakdown: 있다 + -는
meaning: which is in the state of
notes: The pattern '-어/아 있다' describes a continuing state that results from a completed action. Here, '데워져 있는' means it has been heated and is currently in that warm state.

term: 상태야

breakdown: 상태 + -이야
meaning: it's a state
notes: 상태 means 'state' or 'condition'. -이야 is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of the copula 'to be' used after a noun ending in a consonant. The polite form would be 상태예요.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -어/아 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuing state resulting from a completed action. It describes the result that remains.
example: 창문이 열려 있어요. (The window is open.)

=== [58:22] 뭔지 아냐고 ===
meaning: I'm asking if you know what it is.
tone: Informal, rhetorical, slightly insistent.
--- word ---
term: 뭔지
type: Pronoun + verb ending
breakdown: 뭐 (what) + -ㄴ지 (indirect question ending)
meaning: what it is
notes: This is a common contraction of '무엇인지'. The grammar pattern '-(으)ㄴ/는지' is used to embed a question within a larger sentence, often translated as 'whether/if...' or 'what/who/where...'.
--- word ---
term: 아냐고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 알다 (to know) + -냐고 (indirect question ending)
meaning: asking if (you) know
notes: This is an indirect quotation of a question in casual speech (반말). The full form is '아느냐고'. It's used to report or repeat a question. The 'ㄹ' in '알다' is dropped before certain consonants like 'ㄴ', which is a common irregularity for verbs ending in 'ㄹ'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -냐고 (하다)
meaning: Used to quote or repeat a question in an informal way. The full form is V + -느냐고 하다, but the final '하다' (to do/say) is often omitted in conversation.
example: 친구가 어디 가냐고 물었어요. (My friend asked where I was going.)
level: Intermediate

=== [58:30] 묻는 거 뿐이야 ===
meaning: I'm just asking.
tone: Informal, clarifying, slightly defensive.
--- word ---
term: 묻는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 묻다 (to ask) + -는 (present tense adjective ending)
meaning: asking
notes: The '-는' ending transforms an action verb into a descriptive form that can modify a noun, similar to an adjective. '묻는 거' literally means 'the thing of asking' or 'the act of asking'.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 것 (thing) -> 거 (colloquial form)
meaning: thing, fact, the act of
notes: '거' is the very common spoken, colloquial form of the noun '것' (thing, fact). It is used extensively in daily conversation but is generally avoided in formal writing.
--- word ---
term: 뿐이야
type: Particle + verb
breakdown: 뿐 (only, just) + -이다 (to be) -> 이야 (casual form)
meaning: it's only...
notes: The pattern 'Noun + 뿐이다' means 'it is only/just Noun'. '뿐이야' is the informal, non-polite (반말) conjugation. It emphasizes that there is nothing else besides the noun mentioned.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 뿐이다
meaning: Expresses that something is 'only' or 'just' the noun, with no other options or factors involved.
example: 내가 믿는 사람은 너 뿐이야. (The only person I trust is you.)
level: Intermediate

=== [58:38] 16시 반이잖아 ===
meaning: It's 4:30 PM, you know.
tone: Informal, stating a fact, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 16시
type: Noun (time)
breakdown: 16 (십육) + 시 (hour)
meaning: 16:00 / 4 PM
notes: Korea commonly uses the 24-hour clock system (military time), especially in official contexts and for schedules. '시' is the counter for hours.
--- word ---
term: 반이잖아
type: Noun + particle + verb ending
breakdown: 반 (half) + -이다 (to be) + -잖아 (ending)
meaning: it's half, you know
notes: '반' means 'half' and is used after '시' (hour) to mean ':30'. The ending '-잖아' is used to state something the listener should already know or agree with, similar to saying '...you know,' or '...isn't it?' in English. It's very common in casual conversation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A/N + -잖아(요)
meaning: Used to state a fact that the speaker assumes the listener is also aware of, or to gently remind them. It can mean 'as you know,' 'of course,' or 'don't you see?'.
example: 날씨가 춥잖아. (The weather is cold, you know.)
level: Beginner

=== [59:33] 이따 비행기에서 푹 자고 싶어서 ===

--- word ---
term: 이따
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Later (today)
notes: 이따 (itta) refers to a time later on the same day. It's often confused with 나중에 (najunge), which means 'later' in a more general sense (e.g., tomorrow, next week, in the future).
--- word ---
term: 비행기에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 비행기 + -에서
meaning: On the plane
notes: The particle -에서 (-eseo) indicates the location where an action takes place. It's different from -에 (-e), which indicates a static location or destination.
--- word ---
term: 푹
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Soundly, deeply
notes: An adverb that describes doing something thoroughly or deeply. It's very commonly paired with verbs like 자다 (jada - to sleep) as in '푹 자다' (to sleep soundly) or 쉬다 (swida - to rest) as in '푹 쉬다' (to rest well).
--- word ---
term: 자고 싶어서
type: Verb Conjugation
breakdown: 자다 + -고 싶다 + -아/어서
meaning: Because I want to sleep
notes: This combines two grammar points: -고 싶다 (-go sipda) for 'want to do' and -아/어서 (-a/eoseo) for 'so/because'. The sentence trails off, implying this is the reason for a previous or upcoming action.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 싶다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's desire to do an action; 'want to (verb)'.
example: 영화를 보고 싶어요. (I want to watch a movie.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어서
meaning: A connective ending indicating reason or cause. The first clause is the reason for the second clause.
example: 배고파서 밥을 먹었어요. (I was hungry, so I ate.)
level: Beginner

--- grammar ---
pattern: V-자마자
meaning: Indicates that the second action happens immediately after the first action is completed; 'as soon as'.
example: 집에 도착하자마자 전화할게. (I'll call you as soon as I get home.)
level: Intermediate

=== [59:41] 딱 눈 떴는데 ===
meaning: And when I open my eyes...
tone: Informal, setting up a hypothetical situation.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Just, exactly
notes: Here, 딱 (ttak) emphasizes the specific moment of opening her eyes. It adds a sense of 'and at that very moment...'
--- word ---
term: 눈
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Eye(s)
notes: 눈 (nun) can mean both 'eye' and 'snow'. The context makes it clear that it means 'eye' here.
--- word ---
term: 떴는데
type: Verb Conjugation
breakdown: 뜨다 + -었- + -는데
meaning: I opened (my eyes), and...
notes: The verb for opening one's eyes is 뜨다 (tteuda). The -는데 (-neunde) ending is used to provide background information for the clause that will follow. It sets the scene: 'I open my eyes, and then...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-는데/은데
meaning: A versatile connective ending that provides background context, shows a contrast, or introduces a topic.
example: 어제 영화를 봤는데 정말 재미있었어. (I saw a movie yesterday, and it was really fun.)
level: Beginner

=== [59:43] 착륙하고 있었으면 좋겠어 ===
meaning: I hope we're landing.
tone: Informal, hopeful, expressing a wish.
--- word ---
term: 착륙하고 있었으면
type: Verb Phrase Conjugation
breakdown: 착륙하다 + -고 있다 + -았/었으면
meaning: If we were landing
notes: This combines the present progressive form -고 있다 (-go itda, 'to be doing') with the wish pattern -았/었으면 (-ass/eoss-eumyeon, 'I wish'). Literally, 'I wish we were in the state of landing'.
--- word ---
term: 좋겠어
type: Adjective Conjugation
breakdown: 좋다 + -겠어
meaning: It would be good
notes: The second part of the 'I wish' pattern. The -겠- (-get-) suffix adds a sense of conjecture or will, and in this pattern, it completes the hopeful meaning. The informal -어 (-eo) ending is used.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -았/었으면 좋겠다
meaning: Expresses a wish or hope; 'I wish...' or 'I hope...'. The past tense is used to indicate a hypothetical situation.
example: 휴가였으면 좋겠어. (I wish it were vacation.)
level: Intermediate

=== [59:45] 얼마나 행복할까 ===
meaning: How happy would I be?
tone: Informal, rhetorical, dreamy.
--- word ---
term: 얼마나
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: How
notes: When used with an adjective or another adverb in an exclamatory sentence like this, 얼마나 (eolmana) means 'how' or 'so' and adds a great deal of emphasis. For example, '얼마나 좋은지!' (How great it is!).
--- word ---
term: 행복할까
type: Adjective Conjugation
breakdown: 행복하다 + -ㄹ까
meaning: Would I be happy?
notes: The -(으)ㄹ까 (-eulkka) ending is used here as a rhetorical question to oneself, expressing a strong feeling of wonder or anticipation. It's like saying 'I wonder how happy I would be,' but with more emotional weight.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 얼마나 + A/V + -(으)ㄴ/는지
meaning: An exclamatory pattern meaning 'How...!'. The sentence is often completed with a verb like 알다 (to know) or 모르다 (to not know), but can also stand alone as a rhetorical question with -까.
example: 그 사람이 얼마나 착한지 몰라요. (You have no idea how kind that person is.)
level: Intermediate

=== [59:48] 그래서 이제 다들 ===
meaning: So now, everyone...
tone: Informal, transitional, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: A common conjunction used to connect a reason or cause to a result. It's a staple for starting sentences that logically follow from the previous context.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: Indicates a change from the past to the present, meaning 'now' or 'from now on'. It differs from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now/at this moment'. 이제 marks a new stage or situation.
--- word ---
term: 다들
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 다 + 들
meaning: Everyone, you all
notes: A friendly, colloquial way to say 'everyone'. It combines 다 (da), meaning 'all', with the plural marker 들 (deul). It's very common in spoken Korean to address a group.

=== [1:00:06] 오늘부터 거의 이제 ===
meaning: From today, pretty much...
tone: Informal, clarifying, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 오늘부터
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 오늘 + 부터
meaning: From today
notes: The particle 부터 (buteo) marks a starting point in time or space. It is often paired with 까지 (kkaji), which means 'until'. For example, '여기부터 저기까지' (yeogibuteo jeogikkaji) means 'from here to there'.
--- word ---
term: 거의
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Almost, nearly
notes: Used to indicate that something is very close to a certain state, number, or time, but not completely. For example, '거의 다 했어' (geoui da haesseo) means 'I'm almost done'.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: Repeated from the previous sentence, emphasizing that the holiday period is starting right at this point in time. It marks the beginning of the action she's about to describe.

=== [1:00:10] 여행을 시작할 때 ===
meaning: ...when you start your trips.
tone: Informal, completing a thought.
--- word ---
term: 여행을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 여행 + 을
meaning: Trip (as an object)
notes: 여행 (yeohaeng) means 'travel' or 'trip'. The particle 을 (eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb. The object particle is 를 (reul) after a vowel and 을 (eul) after a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 시작할
type: Verb
breakdown: 시작하다 + -(으)ㄹ
meaning: that will start
notes: This is the future noun-modifying form of the verb 시작하다 (sijakhada), 'to start'. The -(으)ㄹ ending allows the verb to describe a noun that follows it, in this case, 때 (ttae).
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: When, at the time of
notes: A dependent noun that means 'time' or 'moment'. It must be preceded by a verb or noun that describes what kind of time it is, as in '점심 먹을 때' (jeomsim meogeul ttae), 'at lunchtime'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time 'when' a future action takes place. It connects a verb clause to the noun 때 (time).
example: 한국에 갈 때 선물을 살 거예요. (Hanguge gal ttae seonmureul sal geoyeyo.) - When I go to Korea, I'm going to buy a gift.
level: Beginner

=== [1:00:26] 그러면 명 뭐냐 ===
meaning: Then, holi— what is it?
tone: Informal, confused, self-correcting.
--- word ---
term: 그러면
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: Then, in that case
notes: A contraction of 그렇게 하면 (geureoke hamyeon), meaning 'if you do that'. It's used to logically connect a following statement based on the preceding one. Often shortened to 그럼 (geureom) in speech.
--- word ---
term: 명
type: Noun (fragment)
breakdown: 
meaning: holi-
notes: This is likely a slip of the tongue. The speaker probably started to say 명절 (myeongjeol), the Korean word for a traditional holiday like Chuseok, but then stopped.
--- word ---
term: 뭐냐
type: Pronoun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 뭐 + -(이)냐
meaning: What is it?
notes: A very casual, blunt question form used in 반말 (banmal), or informal speech. The -(이)냐 ending is used between close friends or when talking to oneself. It can sound confrontational if used improperly.

=== [1:00:33] 가족들도 만나고 하는 것인가 ===
meaning: Is it a thing where you also meet family and stuff?
tone: Informal, questioning, seeking confirmation.
--- word ---
term: 가족들도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 가족 + 들 + 도
meaning: Family too
notes: 가족 (gajok) is 'family', 들 (deul) is the plural marker, and 도 (do) is a particle meaning 'also' or 'too'. 도 replaces subject/object particles like 이/가 or 은/는.
--- word ---
term: 만나고
type: Verb
breakdown: 만나다 + -고
meaning: meet and...
notes: The -고 (go) ending is a connective that links two actions or clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It's used to list activities in sequence.
--- word ---
term: 하는
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -는
meaning: that one does
notes: The present tense noun-modifying form of 하다 (hada), 'to do'. The -는 ending allows the verb to describe the following noun, 것 (geot).
--- word ---
term: 것인가
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 것 + -인가
meaning: is it the thing?
notes: The -인가 (inga) ending is a question form of 이다 (ida), 'to be'. It expresses doubt or poses a question, often to oneself. It's softer than the more direct -이야? (iya?) or -이냐? (inya?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 것
meaning: A common grammar pattern used to nominalize a verb phrase, turning it into a noun. It can mean 'the act of V-ing', 'the thing that one does', or 'the fact that...'.
example: 제가 좋아하는 것은 영화 보기예요. (Jega joahaneun geoseun yeonghwa bogiyeyo.) - The thing I like is watching movies.
level: Intermediate

=== [1:00:45] 그렇군 ===
meaning: I see.
tone: Informal, realization, acknowledgement.
--- word ---
term: 그렇군
type: Adjective
breakdown: 그렇다 + -군
meaning: Oh, so that's how it is.
notes: The sentence ending -군 (gun) or -구나 (guna) is used to express a new realization, surprise, or mild exclamation about a fact. It's often used when talking to oneself after learning something new.

=== [1:00:52] 요새도 제사 지내고 해 ===
meaning: Do you still hold ancestral rites and stuff these days?
tone: Informal, curious, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 요새도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 요새 + 도
meaning: These days too / Even now
notes: 요새 (yosae) is a synonym for 요즘 (yojeum), meaning 'these days' or 'recently'. The particle 도 (do) adds the nuance of 'even', implying that the practice might be less common now.
--- word ---
term: 제사
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Ancestral rites
notes: 제사 (jesa) is a traditional ceremony to honor ancestors, often performed on holidays like Chuseok. It's a significant part of traditional Korean culture.
--- word ---
term: 지내고
type: Verb
breakdown: 지내다 + -고
meaning: hold (a ceremony) and...
notes: The phrase 제사를 지내다 (jesareul jinaeda) is a set expression meaning 'to perform ancestral rites'. The -고 (go) ending connects this action to the next verb, 해 (hae).
--- word ---
term: 해
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다
meaning: do you do?
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말) form of the verb 하다 (hada), 'to do'. Here it functions as a general verb asking if people 'do' this and other related activities. The question is implied by context and intonation.

=== [1:00:57] 전이랑도 붙이고 ===
meaning: And make jeon (Korean pancakes), too.
tone: Informal, adding an example, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 전이랑도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 전 + -이랑 + 도
meaning: Jeon also, and jeon too
notes: 전 (jeon) is a type of Korean pan-fried pancake, a staple holiday food. -(이)랑 (irang) is a colloquial particle for 'and' or 'with', used in speech instead of 와/과 (wa/gwa). 도 (do) adds the meaning of 'also'.
--- word ---
term: 붙이고
type: Verb
breakdown: 부치다 + -고
meaning: pan-fry and...
notes: The standard verb for making jeon is 부치다 (buchida). However, it is very commonly misspelled and pronounced as 붙이다 (buchida), which means 'to attach/stick'. This is a frequent error even among native speakers. The -고 (go) ending connects this action to an unstated next one.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)랑
meaning: A colloquial particle used in spoken Korean to mean 'and' (when listing nouns) or 'with' (when indicating accompaniment). Use -이랑 after a consonant and -랑 after a vowel.
example: 친구랑 영화 봤어. (Chingurang yeonghwa bwasseo.) - I saw a movie with a friend.
level: Beginner

=== [1:00:59] 나는 안 온 지 되게 오래된 거 같아 ===
meaning: I think it's been a really long time since I last came.
tone: Informal, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + 는 (topic particle)
meaning: As for me
notes: 나는 is the informal way to say 'I' while marking it as the topic of the sentence. The topic particle 는/은 can also add a nuance of contrast, like 'As for me (compared to others)...'
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A common way to negate verbs and adjectives in spoken Korean. It is placed directly before the verb/adjective. It's generally more colloquial than the longer ~지 않다 form.
--- word ---
term: 온
type: verb (modifier)
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + ~ㄴ (past tense modifier)
meaning: that came
notes: The ~(으)ㄴ ending transforms a verb into an adjective that modifies a following noun, describing a past action. Here it modifies 지 (the time since).
--- word ---
term: 지
type: grammatical noun
breakdown: 
meaning: since
notes: Used in the pattern 'Verb-(으)ㄴ 지 [time] 되다' to express the amount of time that has passed since an action occurred. For example, '한국에 온 지 1년 됐어요' (It's been one year since I came to Korea).
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really
notes: A colloquial adverb meaning 'very' or 'extremely.' It is very common in casual conversation, often used interchangeably with 정말 (really) or 아주 (very).
--- word ---
term: 오래된
type: verb (modifier)
breakdown: 오래되다 (to be a long time) + ~ㄴ (past tense modifier)
meaning: which has been a long time
notes: 오래되다 is a compound of 오래 (a long time) and 되다 (to become). It means for a long time to have passed.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: noun (bound)
breakdown: 것 (thing) -> 거 (colloquial contraction)
meaning: thing, fact
notes: 거 is the very common spoken form of the noun 것 (thing, fact). It is used frequently in patterns like ~는 거 같아요 (I think...).
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective
breakdown: 같다 (to be like) + ~아 (informal ending)
meaning: it seems like
notes: The pattern ~(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다 is a very common and useful way to express an opinion, guess, or impression softly. It translates to 'I think...' or 'It seems like...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄴ 지 [시간] 되다
meaning: Indicates how much time has passed since an action occurred.
example: 밥 먹은 지 3시간 됐어요. (It's been 3 hours since I ate.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Used to express a guess, opinion, or impression in a gentle, non-assertive way. Translates to 'I think...' or 'It seems like...'
example: 저 사람 학생인 것 같아. (I think that person is a student.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:01:04] 이번에 ===
meaning: This time,
tone: Informal, introductory.
--- word ---
term: 이번에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 이번 (this time) + 에 (time particle)
meaning: this time
notes: 이번 means 'this current time' or 'this occasion.' The particle 에 marks it as a point in time. It's often used to introduce what happened on a specific occasion.

=== [1:01:05] 이번에 오랜만에 ===
meaning: This time, for the first time in a while,
tone: Informal, elaborative.
--- word ---
term: 이번에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 이번 (this time) + 에 (time particle)
meaning: this time
notes: The speaker repeats this phrase, which can be for emphasis or simply as a filler while gathering her thoughts.
--- word ---
term: 오랜만에
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 오랜만 (a long time) + 에 (time particle)
meaning: for the first time in a while
notes: A very common set phrase. 오랜만 is a contraction of 오래간만, meaning 'a long interval of time.' It's used when doing something you haven't done in a while.

=== [1:01:08] 성묘를 다녀왔거든 ===
meaning: I went to visit our ancestral graves, you see.
tone: Informal, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 성묘를
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 성묘 (visiting ancestral graves) + 를 (object particle)
meaning: visiting ancestral graves
notes: 성묘 (seongmyo) is a traditional Korean custom of visiting and caring for ancestral graves, especially during major holidays like Chuseok (Korean harvest festival). The speaker mentions it because it's the Chuseok period.
--- word ---
term: 다녀왔거든
type: verb
breakdown: 다녀오다 (to go and come back) + ~았~ (past tense) + ~거든 (explanatory ending)
meaning: I went and came back, you see
notes: The ending ~거든 is used in conversation to provide a reason or background for a previous statement. It has a nuance of '...you see,' or 'it's because...'. It assumes the listener is interested in the reason. This is the informal 반말 form; the polite form is ~거든요.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-거든(요)
meaning: An explanatory sentence ending used to provide a reason or background information for something previously said. It implies '...you see,' or 'because...'
example: 어제 못 잤거든. (I couldn't sleep yesterday, you see.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:14] 많이 못 찾아뵈어 가지고 ===
meaning: because I haven't been able to visit them often.
tone: Informal, explanatory, slightly apologetic.
--- word ---
term: 많이
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a lot, often
notes: The adverb form of the adjective 많다 (to be many/much). It modifies the verb that follows.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: This adverb expresses inability to do something. It's different from 안, which expresses a lack of will. For example, 못 가요 (I can't go) vs. 안 가요 (I'm not going).
--- word ---
term: 찾아뵈어
type: verb
breakdown: 찾아뵙다 (to visit an elder) + ~어 (connective ending)
meaning: visit (an elder) and...
notes: 찾아뵙다 is the humble/honorific form of 찾아가다 (to go and visit). It is used to show respect to the person being visited, in this case, her ancestors.
--- word ---
term: 가지고
type: verb ending
breakdown: 가지다 (to have) + ~고 (connective)
meaning: so, because
notes: The pattern V-아/어 가지고 is a colloquial way to show a cause-and-effect relationship, similar to V-아/어서. It's very common in spoken Korean. It literally means 'having done V...' and implies the next clause is the result.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 가지고
meaning: A conversational way to link clauses, where the first clause is the cause or reason for the second. Similar in meaning to ~아/어서.
example: 늦잠을 자 가지고 학교에 늦었어요. (I overslept, so I was late for school.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:18] 근데 너무 신기하다 ===
meaning: But it's so fascinating.
tone: Informal, expressing wonder, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 -> 근데 (contraction)
meaning: but, by the way
notes: 근데 is the common spoken contraction of 그런데. It can mean 'but' or 'however,' or it can be used to change the subject, like 'by the way.'
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too' (in a negative sense), in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts. For example, 너무 좋아 (It's so good).
--- word ---
term: 신기하다
type: adjective
breakdown: 
meaning: to be fascinating, amazing
notes: This descriptive verb is used to express a sense of wonder or amazement at something new, strange, or unexpected.

=== [1:01:21] 내가 ===
meaning: I...
tone: Informal, introductory.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + 가 (subject particle)
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: 내가 is the informal way to say 'I' when it is the subject of an action. Learners often confuse it with 내 (my). To say 'you' (informal subject), 네가 is used, but it's often pronounced and even written as 니가 to avoid confusion with 내가.

=== [1:01:23] 아 ===
meaning: Ah.
tone: Exclamatory, filler.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: ah, oh
notes: A very common interjection used to express a wide range of emotions like surprise, realization, or pain. It can also be used as a simple filler sound while thinking of what to say next.

=== [1:01:25] 왜 이렇게 ===
meaning: Why so much...
tone: Informal, questioning, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: why
notes: A fundamental question word in Korean. Used at the beginning of a clause to ask for a reason, similar to 'why' in English.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이러하게
meaning: like this, so, this much
notes: A contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage). It's used to indicate the manner or degree of an action or state. It's very common in spoken Korean. The '이렇게' trio includes 그렇게 (geureoke - like that) and 저렇게 (jeoreoke - like that over there).

=== [1:01:26] 가고 싶지 ===
meaning: ...do I want to go?
tone: Informal, self-questioning, musing aloud.
--- word ---
term: 가고
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 + -고
meaning: going
notes: The stem of the verb 가다 (gada), meaning 'to go', connected to the next part of the grammar pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶지
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 + -지
meaning: want to?
notes: Combines 싶다 (sipda), meaning 'to want to', with the sentence ender -지 (-ji). In this context, -지 is used for self-questioning or talking to oneself, like 'I wonder why I want to...?' It can also be used to seek agreement from a listener ('..., right?').
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 싶다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's desire to do an action. 'To want to (verb)'.
example: 영화를 보고 싶어요. (I want to watch a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:01:29] 그래서 그런 거야 ===
meaning: So that's why.
tone: Informal, expressing a moment of realization.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + -어서
meaning: so, therefore, that's why
notes: A fundamental connector used to show a cause-and-effect relationship. It links a preceding reason with a resulting action or state.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Adjective
breakdown: 그렇다 + -ㄴ
meaning: that kind of, like that
notes: The adjectival form of 그렇다 (geureota), meaning 'to be so' or 'to be like that'. It modifies the following noun, in this case, '것' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: it is (the thing that...)
notes: A very common informal contraction of 것이다 (geosida). It's used to state a fact or provide an explanation in a casual, definitive way. The full form is 것 + 이다 (thing + to be).
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ/는 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to explain a situation or state a conclusion, often with a sense of discovery or emphasis. It translates to 'it's that...' or 'the thing is...'.
example: 피곤해서 일찍 자는 거야. (It's because I'm tired that I'm sleeping early.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:32] 이제 성묘를 왜 이렇게 ===
meaning: Now, why do I... visit the ancestral graves so much...
tone: Informal, restating a question to herself.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now' in the sense of a new situation starting from this point on. It's often contrasted with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment'. Here, it marks a shift in her train of thought.
--- word ---
term: 성묘를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 성묘 + -를
meaning: visiting ancestral graves (as object)
notes: 성묘 (seongmyo) is the cultural practice of visiting and tidying ancestral graves, especially during holidays like Chuseok. The particle -를 (reul) marks it as the object of the implied verb 'to do' or 'to go'.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: why
notes: The question word for 'why', used to ask for a reason.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이러하게
meaning: like this, so much
notes: A contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage). It emphasizes the degree of her desire to go, meaning 'so much' or 'this strongly'.

=== [1:01:34] 이렇게 가고 싶을까 ===
meaning: ...why would I want to go so much?
tone: Informal, contemplative, wondering aloud.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이러하게
meaning: so much, like this
notes: Repeated for emphasis from the previous sentence fragment. It highlights the intensity of her feeling.
--- word ---
term: 가고
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 + -고
meaning: going
notes: The stem of the verb 가다 (gada), 'to go', used as part of the 'V-고 싶다' pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶을까
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 + -(으)ㄹ까
meaning: I wonder if I want to
notes: Combines 싶다 (sipda), 'to want to', with the ending -(으)ㄹ까 (-eulkka). This ending is used to express doubt, wonder, or ask a question to oneself. It conveys a sense of musing, like 'I wonder why...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-(으)ㄹ까(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to ask a question to oneself ('I wonder...'), suggest an action ('Shall we...?'), or ask for the listener's opinion.
example: 오늘 저녁에 뭐 먹을까? (What shall we eat for dinner tonight? / I wonder what to eat...)
level: Beginner

=== [1:01:36] 아 안 되겠다 ===
meaning: Ah, this won't do.
tone: Informal, decisive, making a resolution.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah, Oh
notes: A common interjection used to express surprise, realization, or understanding.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: The most common way to negate verbs and adjectives in Korean. It is placed directly before the verb/adjective.
--- word ---
term: 되겠다
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 + -겠다
meaning: it will be okay
notes: Combines 되다 (doeda), meaning 'to become' or 'to be okay', with -겠다 (-getda). The pattern '안 되겠다' is a very common fixed expression meaning 'This won't do' or 'I can't let this be'. It implies that the speaker has decided to take action.

=== [1:01:38] 가야겠다 했는데 ===
meaning: I thought, 'I have to go,' and then...
tone: Informal, narrative, connecting a thought to an action.
--- word ---
term: 가야겠다
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 가다 + -아야겠다
meaning: I must go, I should go
notes: This is a combination of the verb 가다 (gada, 'to go') and the grammar pattern -아야/어야겠다 (-ayagetda). This pattern expresses a strong intention or necessity felt by the speaker, like a personal resolution.
--- word ---
term: 했는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -했- + -는데
meaning: I did, and... / I thought, and...
notes: Here, 하다 (hada) is used to mean 'to think' or 'to say'. The past tense marker -했- (-haet-) shows the thought occurred in the past. The connective ending -는데 (-neunde) provides background information for the next clause, translating to 'I thought X, and then Y happened'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아야/어야겠다
meaning: Expresses a strong intention, resolution, or perceived necessity. It's stronger than 'V-아야/어야 하다' (must do) as it often implies a spontaneous decision. 'I've decided I must...'
example: 너무 피곤해서 이제 자야겠다. (I'm so tired, I'd better go to sleep now.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:40] 첫째 언니한테 전화를 해봤어 ===
meaning: I tried calling my oldest sister.
tone: Informal, narrative, stating a specific action.
--- word ---
term: 첫째
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: first, oldest
notes: Used to denote the first in a sequence. When referring to siblings, it means the oldest. The sequence is 첫째 (cheotjjae - first), 둘째 (duljjae - second), 셋째 (setjjae - third), etc.
--- word ---
term: 언니한테
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 언니 + -한테
meaning: to my older sister
notes: 언니 (eonni) is the term a female uses for her older sister or a close older female friend. -한테 (-hante) is a colloquial particle meaning 'to' a person or animal. It's the spoken equivalent of the more formal/written particle 에게 (ege).
--- word ---
term: 전화를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 전화 + -를
meaning: a phone call
notes: 전화 (jeonhwa) means 'telephone' or 'phone call'. The phrase 전화를 하다 (jeonhwareul hada) literally means 'to do a phone call' and is the standard way to say 'to call someone'.
--- word ---
term: 해봤어
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -아/어 보다 + -았어
meaning: I tried doing (it)
notes: This is the past tense informal form of 해보다 (haeboda), which is a compound verb from 하다 (hada, 'to do') and 보다 (boda, 'to see'). The combined form means 'to try doing something'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 보다
meaning: This pattern means 'to try doing' or 'to experience' an action. It's used when attempting something to see what it's like.
example: 이 김치를 먹어 봤어요? (Have you tried this kimchi?)
level: Beginner

=== [1:01:42] 근데 언니가 ===
meaning: But my sister...
tone: Informal, narrative, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: But, however
notes: A very common contraction of '그런데' (geureonde) used in casual speech. It can mean 'but' or be used to change the subject, like 'by the way'. It is less formal than '하지만' (hajiman).
--- word ---
term: 언니가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 언니 + -가
meaning: My sister (subject)
notes: '언니' (eonni) is what a female calls her older sister or a close older female friend. The particle '-가' (ga) marks the noun as the subject of the sentence. '-가' is used after nouns ending in a vowel, while '-이' (i) is used after nouns ending in a consonant.

=== [1:01:44] 지금 나한테 그런 말을 하는 거야 ===
meaning: is saying that kind of thing to me now.
tone: Informal, narrative, recounting a conversation.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: '지금' (jigeum) means 'now' or 'at this very moment'. It's often compared to '이제' (ije), which also means 'now' but can carry a nuance of change from a previous state ('from now on').
--- word ---
term: 나한테
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + -한테
meaning: To me
notes: '나' (na) is the informal pronoun for 'I/me'. The particle '-한테' (hante) means 'to (a person)' or 'from (a person)' and is used mainly in spoken, informal Korean. The more formal or written equivalent is '-에게' (ege).
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 그렇다 + -ㄴ
meaning: That kind of
notes: '그런' (geureon) is an adjectival form of '그렇다' (geureota), meaning 'to be like that'. It modifies the following noun, '말' (mal). It's part of a set: '이런' (ireon - this kind of), '그런' (geureon - that kind of), '저런' (jeoreon - that kind of over there).
--- word ---
term: 말을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 말 + -을
meaning: Words, speech (object)
notes: '말' (mal) means 'word(s)' or 'speech'. The particle '-을' (eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb. '-을' is used after nouns ending in a consonant, while '-를' (reul) is used after vowels.
--- word ---
term: 하는
type: Verb modifier
breakdown: 하다 + -는
meaning: (that she) is doing/saying
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of the verb '하다' (hada - to do). It modifies the following noun, '거' (geo). The phrase '말을 하다' means 'to speak' or 'to say something'.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: is the thing that...
notes: A very common informal sentence ending. It's a contraction of '것이야' (geosiya), which is '것' (geot - thing, fact) plus the informal copula '-이야' (iya). The pattern '-는 거야' is used to state a fact or explain a situation in a casual, narrative way.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to explain a situation or describe an action with a slightly emphatic or narrative tone. It's the casual speech version of '-는 것이다'.
example: 지금 집에 가는 거야. (I'm on my way home now.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:01:47] 아 왜 이렇게 가고 싶지 ===

tone: Informal, quoting her sister's emphatic words.
--- word ---
term: 아

breakdown: 
meaning: Ah, oh
notes: A common interjection expressing a range of emotions like surprise, realization, or frustration. Here, it conveys a strong sense of longing.

breakdown: 
meaning: Why
notes: The standard Korean question word for 'why'.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this, so much

--- word ---
term: 가고

term: 싶지

breakdown: 싶다 + -지
meaning: want to...?
notes: Combines '-고 싶다' (go sipda - to want to do) with the ending '-지' (ji). '-지' can be used to ask a rhetorical question or to state something as if seeking confirmation, creating a musing tone: 'I wonder why I want to go...'

=== [1:01:51] 이러면서 ===

meaning: While being/saying this
notes: A contraction of '이렇게 말하면서' (ireoke malhamyeonseo - while saying this). The '-면서' (myeonseo) ending indicates two actions are happening simultaneously. Here, it connects the quoted thought to the following statement.

=== [1:01:52] 가 ===
meaning: Go.
tone: Informal, direct, likely part of the quoted speech.
--- word ---
term: 가
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다
meaning: Go
notes: The most basic informal command form of '가다' (gada - to go). It is formed by simply using the verb stem and is used only with people you are very close to, such as family or close friends.

=== [1:01:53] 이번 달을 이렇게 가고 싶다고 ===
meaning: (She says) that she wants to go so much this month.
tone: Informal, reported speech, emphasizing desire.
--- word ---
term: 이번
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: This (time)
notes: '이번' (ibeon) refers to the current or immediately upcoming instance of a recurring event, e.g., '이번 주' (ibeon ju - this week), '이번 달' (ibeon dal - this month).
--- word ---
term: 달을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 달 + -을
meaning: month (object)
notes: '달' (dal) means 'month'. Using the object particle '-을' (eul) here instead of a time particle like '-에' (e) can add emphasis to the time period itself as the object of the desire.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this, so much
notes: The adverbial form of '이렇다' (ireota - to be like this). It is used here to emphasize the degree of her sister's desire to go.
--- word ---
term: 가고
type: Verb stem
breakdown: 가다 + -고
meaning: to go
notes: The stem of the verb '가다' (gada - to go), part of the '-고 싶다' (go sipda - want to go) pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶다고
type: Auxiliary verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 + -고
meaning: (she says) that she wants to
notes: An informal reported speech ending. It's a shortened form of '-다고 하다' (dago hada - to say that...). It's used to quote what someone else said or thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -다고 (하다)
meaning: An indirect quotation ending used to report what someone said. The final '하다' (to say) is often omitted in casual conversation. The specific form changes based on the verb/adjective type.
example: 친구가 춥다고 했어요. (My friend said it's cold.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:55] 무리를 해서라도 이렇게 너무 가고 싶다고 ===
meaning: (She says) that she wants to go so badly, even if she has to overdo it.
tone: Informal, reported speech, emphasizing strong determination.
--- word ---
term: 무리를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 무리 + -를
meaning: strain, unreasonableness (object)
notes: '무리' (muri) means an unreasonable demand or a strain. The phrase '무리를 하다' (murireul hada) is a common idiom meaning 'to overdo it,' 'to push oneself too hard,' or 'to go out of one's way'.
--- word ---
term: 해서라도
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -아/어서 + -라도
meaning: even by doing
notes: This combines the connective '-아/어서' (a/eoseo) with the particle '-라도' (rado - even if, at least). The pattern 'V-(아/어)서라도' expresses a strong will to do something, even if it requires an extreme or undesirable measure.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this, so much
notes: Used here for emphasis, highlighting the intensity of her sister's desire.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So, very
notes: Originally meaning 'too (much),' '너무' (neomu) is now commonly used in colloquial speech to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts, similar to '아주' (aju) or '정말' (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 가고
type: Verb stem
breakdown: 가다 + -고
meaning: to go
notes: The stem of the verb '가다' (gada - to go), part of the '-고 싶다' (go sipda - want to go) pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶다고
type: Auxiliary verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 + -고
meaning: (she says) that she wants to
notes: The informal reported speech ending, quoting her sister's strong desire.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(아/어)서라도
meaning: Expresses determination to achieve a goal, even if it requires an undesirable or difficult action. It translates to 'even if I have to V...' or 'by at least V-ing...'.
example: 택시를 타서라도 늦지 않을 거야. (I won't be late, even if I have to take a taxi.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:01:58] 언니가 막 그러는 거야 ===

tone: Informal, narrative, summarizing her sister's words.
--- word ---
term: 언니가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 언니 + -가
meaning: My sister (subject)
notes: '언니' (eonni) is what a female calls her older sister. '-가' (ga) is the subject marking particle for nouns ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Just, intensely
notes: '막' (mak) is a colloquial adverb with many nuances. It can mean 'carelessly' or 'intensely'. Here, it adds emphasis, suggesting her sister was saying these things passionately and without restraint.
--- word ---
term: 그러는
type: Verb modifier
breakdown: 그렇다 + -는
meaning: (the act of) saying that
notes: A contraction of '그렇게 하는' (geureoke haneun - doing like that) or '그렇게 말하는' (geureoke malhaneun - saying like that). It functions as an adjective describing the following noun, '거' (geo).
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: is the thing that...
notes: A common informal sentence ending, short for '것이야' (geosiya). The pattern '-는 거야' concludes the speaker's description of her sister's actions, presenting it as a fact within her story.

=== [1:02:01] 어 언니 나도야 ===

tone: Casual, responsive, affectionate.
--- word ---
term: 어
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Oh, ah
notes: A common, simple interjection used to express mild surprise, realization, or to get someone's attention. Its meaning is highly dependent on intonation.
--- word ---
term: 언니
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Older sister
notes: A term used by a female speaker to refer to her older sister or a slightly older, close female friend. The speaker, Hyejin, is the youngest of three sisters.
--- word ---

breakdown: 나 (I/me) + -도 (also) + -이야 (is/am)
meaning: It's me too.
notes: A very casual and friendly way of saying 'me too'. It's a contraction of 나도 + 이다 (to be) + -야 (informal ending). The full form would be '나도이다'. The -이야 ending attaches to nouns ending in a consonant, and -야 to nouns ending in a vowel, like 나도.

=== [1:02:05] 우리가 보고 싶나 보다 ===
meaning: I guess they miss us.
tone: Speculative, informal, affectionate.
--- word ---
term: 우리가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 우리 (we/us) + -가 (subject marker)
meaning: We
notes: 우리 means 'we' or 'us', but is also frequently used to mean 'my' in contexts involving family, school, or country (e.g., 우리 엄마 'my mom'). Here, it means 'us', referring to Hyejin and her fans, the 'Twits'.
--- word ---
term: 보고
type: Verb stem
breakdown: 보다 (to see)
meaning: see
notes: Part of the common phrase '보고 싶다' (to miss someone), which literally translates to 'want to see'.
--- word ---
term: 싶나
type: Auxiliary Adjective
breakdown: 싶다 (to want to) + -나
meaning: want to
notes: 싶다 is an auxiliary adjective that attaches to a verb stem (like 보고) to express desire. The -나 ending is part of the '-나 보다' pattern used for guessing.
--- word ---
term: 보다
type: Grammar Pattern Ending
breakdown: 
meaning: it seems / I guess
notes: This is the second part of the grammar pattern '-나 보다', which is used to express a guess or assumption based on some observation or feeling.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-나 보다 / -(으)ㄴ가 보다
meaning: Used to express a guess or assumption, like 'it seems that...' or 'I guess...'. '-나 보다' is used with action verbs and 있다/없다. '-(으)ㄴ가 보다' is used with descriptive verbs (adjectives).
example: 비가 오나 봐요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:02:07] 그냥 우리 가자 해가지고 갔다 왔거든 ===
meaning: We just said, 'Let's go,' and so we went (and came back).
tone: Narrative, informal, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: A versatile adverb that can mean 'just' (without any special reason), 'as it is', or 'to just continue doing something'. Here, it implies a spontaneous decision.
--- word ---
term: 우리
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: we
notes: Refers to the speaker (Hyejin) and her sisters in this context.
--- word ---
term: 가자
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 (to go) + -자 (let's...)
meaning: Let's go
notes: The -자 ending is an informal, non-polite propositive form used to make suggestions among close friends or people of similar or younger age. It's a key part of 반말 (casual speech).
--- word ---
term: 해가지고
type: Verb Connector
breakdown: 하다 (to do/say) + -아/어 가지고
meaning: saying that, and so...
notes: A colloquial connector often used in speech instead of -아서/어서. It indicates that the first action (saying 'let's go') is the reason for or leads directly to the second action (going). It can be shortened to 해갖고.
--- word ---
term: 갔다
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 (to go) + -았/었- (past tense)
meaning: went
notes: This is part of the compound verb 갔다 오다 (to go and come back), a very common expression for indicating that you have visited a place and returned.
--- word ---
term: 왔거든
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + -았/었- (past tense) + -거든
meaning: ...came back, you see.
notes: The -거든 ending is used in casual conversation to provide a reason or background information that the speaker assumes the listener doesn't know. It adds a nuance of '...you see' or 'as a matter of fact...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -거든(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to provide background information or a reason for a preceding or following statement. It implies that the listener may not be aware of this fact.
example: 어제 못 갔어요. 너무 피곤했거든요. (I couldn't go yesterday. I was so tired, you see.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:02:11] 너무 신기했어 ===

tone: Expressive, informal, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too' (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative sentences, similar to 정말 or 아주.
--- word ---
term: 신기했어

breakdown: 신기하다 (to be fascinating) + -았/었어 (past tense, informal)
meaning: was fascinating
notes: 신기하다 describes something that is novel, amazing, or fascinating. The -았/었어 ending is the standard past tense informal non-polite (반말) conjugation.

=== [1:02:12] 그랬어 ===
meaning: It was.
tone: Affirmative, informal, simple.
--- word ---
term: 그랬어

breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) + -았/었어 (past tense, informal)
meaning: It was like that.
notes: A common way to affirm or agree with a previous statement. It's the past tense of 그래 (Yes / That's right). The base form 그렇다 means 'to be so' or 'to be like that'.

=== [1:02:22] 언니들 인터뷰 ===
meaning: An interview with my sisters.
tone: Topic-setting, informal.
--- word ---
term: 언니들
type: Noun
breakdown: 언니 (older sister) + -들 (plural marker)
meaning: Older sisters
notes: The -들 suffix makes a noun plural. While not always necessary in Korean if the context is clear, it's used here for emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 인터뷰
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Interview
notes: A loanword from the English 'interview'. This is a sentence fragment, common in casual speech, likely in response to a fan's comment.

=== [1:02:24] 언니들 너무 웃기지? ===
meaning: My sisters are so funny, right?
tone: Conversational, engaging, informal.
--- word ---
term: 언니들
type: Noun
breakdown: 언니 (older sister) + -들 (plural marker)
meaning: Older sisters
notes: The speaker is the youngest sister (막내, maknae), so she refers to both of her older siblings as 언니.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: In colloquial speech, 너무 is frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so'. It intensifies the adjective that follows, which is 웃기다 (to be funny) in this case.
--- word ---
term: 웃기지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 (to be funny) + -지? (right?)
meaning: are funny, right?
notes: 웃기다 means 'to be funny' or 'to make someone laugh'. The -지? ending is used to confirm information or seek agreement from the listener, similar to adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek confirmation or agreement on something the speaker believes is true or that the listener already knows.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (The weather's nice today, right?)
level: Beginner

--- word ---
term: 근데

breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: But, however, by the way

'-은/는' is the topic marker, indicating that 'my sisters' is the topic of the sentence. The speaker, Hyejin, is the youngest of three sisters, so this refers to her two older siblings.

=== [1:02:29] 그런 걸 좀 이렇게 뭐랄까 ===
meaning: That kind of thing, a bit... how should I put it?
tone: Informal, hesitant, searching for words.
--- word ---
term: 그런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 그렇다 + -ㄴ
meaning: That kind of, such
notes: This is the adjectival form of '그렇다' (to be like that). It modifies the following noun, '걸' (thing). It refers to something previously mentioned or understood from context.
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: Noun + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 것 + -을 (contraction)
meaning: thing (object)
notes: A common spoken contraction of '것을'. '것' means 'thing' or 'fact', and '-을' is the object particle. Using '걸' instead of '것을' makes speech sound more natural and fluid.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조금 (contraction)
meaning: A bit, a little
notes: A contraction of '조금'. It's frequently used in conversation to soften a statement, make a request sound more polite, or, as in this case, to fill a pause while thinking.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: Like this, in this way
notes: Formed from the descriptive verb '이렇다' (to be like this) and the adverbial ending '-게'. It's used to describe how an action is performed.
--- word ---
term: 뭐랄까
type: Expression
breakdown: 뭐라고 할까 (contraction)
meaning: How should I put it?
notes: A very common filler phrase used when the speaker is thinking of the right words to use. It literally means 'What should I call it?' or 'What should I say?'. It's a sign of spontaneous, unscripted speech.

=== [1:02:33] 드러나는 걸 되게 두려워해 ===
meaning: They're really afraid of it being revealed.
tone: Informal, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 드러나는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 드러나다 + -는
meaning: being revealed
notes: '드러나다' means 'to be revealed' or 'to come to light'. The '-는' ending turns it into a present-tense adjective that modifies the following noun, '걸' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: Noun + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 것 + -을 (contraction)
meaning: the act of / the thing that is
notes: A contraction of '것을'. Here, '것' acts as a nominalizer, turning the entire preceding clause '드러나는' (being revealed) into a noun phrase that can be the object of the verb '두려워해' (to be afraid of).
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Really, very
notes: A colloquial adverb meaning 'very' or 'extremely'. It's very common in casual conversation, often used in place of more standard adverbs like '아주' or '매우'.
--- word ---
term: 두려워해
type: Verb
breakdown: 두렵다 -> 두려워하다 + -어
meaning: is afraid of
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) conjugation of '두려워하다' (to fear). Many feeling adjectives like '두렵다' (scary) add '-어/아하다' to become verbs describing someone expressing or having that feeling.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 것 / V-는 걸
meaning: A grammar pattern used to turn a verb clause into a noun phrase, meaning 'the act of V-ing' or 'the thing that is V-ing'. It allows the clause to function as a subject or object in a sentence.
example: 매일 운동하는 것은 건강에 좋아요. (Exercising every day is good for your health.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:02:37] 그래서 ===
meaning: So...
tone: Informal, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: Used to connect two clauses where the first is the cause or reason for the second. It indicates a logical progression from one thought to the next.

=== [1:02:38] 첫째 언니한테 되게 감동받았었는데 ===
meaning: I was really touched by my oldest sister...
tone: Informal, reminiscent, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 첫째
type: Noun / Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: First, oldest
notes: Used to denote the first in a sequence. When referring to siblings, '첫째 언니' means 'oldest sister' and '첫째 동생' means 'oldest younger sibling'.
--- word ---
term: 언니한테
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 + -한테
meaning: by my older sister
notes: The particle '-한테' is a colloquial equivalent of '-에게' (to/from) and '-에게서' (from). It's primarily used in spoken Korean with people and animals. Here, with '감동받다' (to receive an impression), it indicates the source of the feeling.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Really, very
notes: A colloquial adverb meaning 'very' or 'extremely'. It's very common in casual conversation, often used in place of more standard adverbs like '아주' or '매우'.
--- word ---
term: 감동받았었는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 감동받다 + -았었- + -는데
meaning: I had been touched/impressed, and...
notes: '감동받다' means 'to be touched' or 'impressed'. The '-았었-' is the past perfect tense, indicating an action completed in the past that is no longer ongoing. The '-는데' ending provides background information for the next clause and often leaves the sentence feeling unfinished, inviting the listener to hear what comes next.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -았/었었-
meaning: Past perfect tense. It indicates an action or state that was completed in the past and is disconnected from the present. It often implies a more remote past than the simple past tense '-았/었-'.
example: 어렸을 때 여기 왔었어요. (I had come here when I was young.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-는데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background or context for the following clause. It can mean 'but', 'and', or 'so', depending on the context. It's very common in conversation for setting a scene or introducing a topic.
example: 비가 오는데 우리 뭐 할까? (It's raining, so what should we do?)
level: Beginner

=== [1:02:43] 언니는 그냥 조용히 살길 바라는 사람이야 ===
meaning: My sister is someone who just wants to live quietly.
tone: Informal, descriptive, affectionate.
--- word ---
term: 언니는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 + -는
meaning: My sister (as for her)
notes: '언니' (eonni) is what a female calls an older sister. The topic marker '-는' sets 'my sister' as the main subject of the description that follows.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Just, simply
notes: A versatile adverb that can mean 'just' or 'simply'. Here it implies she wants nothing more than a simple, quiet life.
--- word ---
term: 조용히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조용하다 + -히
meaning: Quietly
notes: The adverb form of the adjective '조용하다' (to be quiet). The suffix '-히' is one way to turn certain adjectives into adverbs.
--- word ---
term: 살길
type: Verb + Noun + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 살다 + -기를 (contraction)
meaning: to live
notes: This is a contraction of '살기를', which is part of the grammar pattern 'V-기를 바라다' (to hope/wish to V). '살다' (to live) + '-기' (nominalizer) + '-를' (object particle). The contraction is common in speech.
--- word ---
term: 바라는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 바라다 + -는
meaning: who wants/wishes
notes: '바라다' means 'to wish' or 'to hope'. The '-는' ending turns it into a present-tense adjective that modifies the following noun, '사람' (person).
--- word ---
term: 사람이야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 사람 + -이야
meaning: is a person
notes: '사람' means 'person'. '-이야' is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of the copula '이다' (to be) used after a noun ending in a consonant.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-기를 바라다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's wish or hope for something to happen. It means 'to hope/wish to V'.
example: 시험에 합격하기를 바라요. (I hope to pass the exam.)
level: Intermediate

meaning: But, however

=== [1:02:49] 진짜 웃겨 ===
meaning: It's really funny.

meaning: Really, truly

--- word ---
term: 웃겨

breakdown: 웃기다 + -어
meaning: It's funny
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) conjugation of '웃기다'. While '웃기다' is technically the causative form of '웃다' (to laugh), meaning 'to make someone laugh', it is most commonly used to simply mean 'to be funny'.

=== [1:02:51] 첫째 언니 ===
meaning: My oldest sister.

term: 언니
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: older sister
notes: This term is used by a female speaker to refer to her biological older sister or a close older female friend. The male equivalent is '누나' (nuna).

=== [1:02:52] 특히 첫째 언니는 진짜 웃겨 ===

term: 특히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: especially, particularly
notes: Used to single out or emphasize a specific item from a group. It often appears at the beginning of a sentence or clause.
--- word ---
term: 첫째
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: first
notes: Refers to the first-born. When talking about siblings, it means 'oldest'.
--- word ---
term: 언니는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 + 는
meaning: older sister (as for)
notes: The topic particle '-는' (neun) is used here to introduce the oldest sister as the main subject of the description. It can also add a nuance of contrast, which becomes clearer in the next sentence.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, for real
notes: A very common colloquial adverb for emphasis, similar to '정말' (jeongmal). '진짜' often feels slightly more casual and conversational than '정말'.
--- word ---
term: 웃겨
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 -> 웃겨
meaning: is funny
notes: This is the informal present tense conjugation of '웃기다' (to be funny). It's different from '웃다' (utda), which means 'to laugh'. So, '언니가 웃어' (eonniga useo) means 'My sister laughs,' while '언니는 웃겨' (eonnineun utgyeo) means 'My sister is funny.'

=== [1:02:55] 둘째 언니는 다른 결로 웃기고 ===

meaning: second
notes: The native Korean ordinal number for 'second'. Used here to refer to the second-born sister.
--- word ---
term: 언니는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 언니 + 는
meaning: second sister (as for)
notes: The topic particle '-는' (neun) here creates a clear contrast with the '첫째 언니는' (oldest sister) from the previous sentence, highlighting how they are different.
--- word ---
term: 다른
type: Adjective
breakdown: 다르다 -> 다른
meaning: different
notes: This is the adjective '다르다' (dareuda, to be different) in its modifier form. The '-ㄴ' ending allows it to describe the following noun, '결' (gyeol).
--- word ---
term: 결로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 결 + 으로
meaning: in a way/style
notes: '결' (gyeol) literally means 'grain' (like in wood) or 'texture', but here it's used figuratively to mean 'type,' 'style,' or 'vein.' The particle '-(으)로' (euro) indicates the means or manner, so '다른 결로' means 'in a different way/style'.
--- word ---
term: 웃기고
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 + -고
meaning: is funny, and...
notes: The '-고' (go) ending is a connective particle that links two clauses. It can mean 'and' or 'and then'. Here, it suggests the speaker has more to say, leaving the sentence unfinished.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -고
meaning: Connects two clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It's used to list actions or states in sequence or in parallel.
example: 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요. (I ate and then watched a movie.)
level: Beginner
