In banmal (informal speech), using the dictionary form as a statement is common and feels declarative or slightly dramatic, like a line in a diary or drama. The more common conversational form is '미안해' (mianhae). For formal situations, '죄송합니다' (joesonghamnida) is used.

=== [1:09:42] 근데 ===
meaning: But...
tone: Informal, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: but, however, by the way
notes: '근데' (geunde) is a very common contraction of '그런데' (geureonde) in spoken Korean. While it often means 'but' or 'however' like '하지만' (hajiman), '근데' is also frequently used to change the subject, similar to 'by the way'.

=== [1:09:44] 그 카페를 들라고 그랬는데 ===
meaning: I was going to stop by that cafe, but...
tone: Informal, explaining a past intention, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: '그' (geu) refers to something that was just mentioned or is known to both the speaker and the listener. It's distinct from '이' (i - this, close to speaker) and '저' (jeo - that, far from both).
--- word ---
term: 카페를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 카페 + -를
meaning: the cafe (object)
notes: '-을/를' is the object marking particle, attached to the noun that is the direct object of the verb. Here, the cafe is the object of the action '들르다' (to stop by).
--- word ---
term: 들라고
type: Verb + quoted command
breakdown: 들르다 + -라고 (하다)
meaning: (I told myself) to stop by
notes: This is a colloquial shortening of '들르라고' (deulleorago). The base verb is '들르다' (deulleuda), meaning 'to stop by'. The '-(으)라고 하다' grammar is for quoting a command. Here, it's used to express one's own intention, as if telling oneself, 'Let's stop by the cafe'.
--- word ---
term: 그랬는데
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 그렇다 + -았/었- + -는데
meaning: I was going to, but...
notes: A very common phrase that combines '그렇다' (to be so) with the past tense and the '-는데' ending. It's used to state a past action or intention as background for a contrasting situation that follows. The sentence trails off, implying the contrast.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-는데/-(으)ㄴ데
meaning: A versatile connective ending used to provide background information, show contrast ('but'), or give a reason ('so'). Here, it indicates a past intention that contrasts with the current feeling.
example: 어제 영화 봤는데 재미없었어. (Eoje yeonghwa bwanneunde jaemieopseosseo.) - I saw a movie yesterday, but it wasn't fun.
level: Intermediate

=== [1:09:46] 의지가 굉장히 약해질 것만 같아 ===
meaning: I feel like my willpower is going to get really weak.
tone: Informal, expressing a feeling or prediction about herself.
--- word ---
term: 의지가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 의지 + -가
meaning: willpower (subject)
notes: '의지' (uiji) means 'will' or 'volition'. The particle '-가' (ga) marks it as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 굉장히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 굉장하다 + -히
meaning: very, extremely
notes: An adverb that emphasizes the degree of an adjective or another adverb. It's stronger than '아주' (aju) or '매우' (maeu) and is common in both spoken and written Korean.
--- word ---
term: 약해질
type: Verb
breakdown: 약하다 + -아/어지다 + -ㄹ
meaning: that will become weak
notes: This combines the adjective '약하다' (to be weak) with the '-아/어지다' pattern, which means 'to become'. So, '약해지다' means 'to become weak'. The '-ㄹ' ending makes it a future modifier for the noun '것' (geot).
--- word ---
term: 것만
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 것 + -만
meaning: only the thing / just that
notes: '것' (geot) is a generic noun for 'thing'. The particle '-만' (man) means 'only' or 'just'. Here, it adds emphasis, suggesting that becoming weak is the only likely outcome.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -아
meaning: it seems like
notes: The informal present tense form of '같다' (gatda). When used in the '-(으)ㄹ 것 같다' pattern, it expresses a guess, feeling, or opinion about a future event.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A-아/어지다
meaning: This pattern attaches to an adjective stem to create a verb that signifies a change of state, meaning 'to become (adjective)'.
example: 날씨가 추워졌어요. (Nalssiga chuwojyeosseoyo.) - The weather became cold.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-(으)ㄹ 것 같다
meaning: Expresses supposition, a guess, or a feeling about a future or present situation. It translates to 'I think...', 'It seems like...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 비가 올 것 같아요. (Biga ol geot gatayo.) - I think it's going to rain.
level: Beginner

=== [1:09:52] 그지? ===
meaning: Right?
tone: Informal, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 그지?
type: Interjection / Question tag
breakdown: 그렇지? (contraction)
meaning: Right?
notes: A very common colloquial contraction of 그렇지? (geureochi?). It's used in casual speech (반말, banmal) to confirm something or seek agreement, similar to 'right?' or 'isn't it?'. The full form is derived from 그렇다 (geureota, 'to be so') + the question ending -지?.

=== [1:10:03] 지금 잘 못 갈 것 같지? ===
meaning: It seems like I can't really go right now, right?
tone: Informal, self-reflective, seeking confirmation.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 지금 (jigeum) means 'now' or 'at this very moment.' It differs from 이제 (ije), which also means 'now' but implies a change from the past ('from now on').
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: 잘 (jal) means 'well.' When used with a negative like 못 (mot), as in 잘 못 가다, it softens the meaning to 'can't really go' or 'can't go well,' emphasizing the difficulty.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: 못 (mot) is an adverb expressing inability. It's placed before a verb. This is different from 안 (an), which expresses a lack of will or simple negation. For example, 못 가요 (mot gayo) is 'I can't go,' while 안 가요 (an gayo) is 'I'm not going.'
--- word ---
term: 갈
type: Verb (future determiner form)
breakdown: 가다 + -ㄹ
meaning: that will go
notes: This is the future determiner form of the verb 가다 (gada, 'to go'). The -(으)ㄹ ending attaches to a verb stem to modify a following noun, in this case, 것 (geot).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, fact
notes: 것 (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'fact.' It's used here as part of the -(으)ㄹ 것 같다 grammar pattern to express a guess or opinion.
--- word ---
term: 같지?
type: Adjective + question ending
breakdown: 같다 + -지?
meaning: seems like, right?
notes: The base adjective 같다 (gatda) means 'to be like' or 'to seem.' The informal question ending -지? is added to seek confirmation or agreement from the listener.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)ㄹ 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, supposition, or opinion about a future action or current state. Translates to 'I think...' or 'It seems/looks like...'
example: 비가 올 것 같아요. (It looks like it's going to rain.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:10:05] 어우 ===
meaning: Ugh...
tone: Exclamatory, expressing fatigue.
--- word ---
term: 어우
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ugh / Oof / Oh my
notes: 어우 (eou) is a common interjection expressing a range of emotions like surprise, being overwhelmed, or mild disgust. The meaning is highly dependent on the speaker's tone. Here, it conveys her sleepiness.

=== [1:10:07] 이제부터는 ===
meaning: From now on...
tone: Declarative, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 이제부터는
type: Adverb + Particles
breakdown: 이제 + -부터 + -는
meaning: From now on (as for...)
notes: A combination of 이제 (ije, 'now'), -부터 (-buteo, 'from'), and the topic particle -는 (-neun). 이제부터 means 'starting from now,' implying a change. The particle -는 adds emphasis, setting 'from now on' as the topic of the following clause.

=== [1:10:22] 말 안 하기 게임 ===
meaning: The 'not talking' game.
tone: Playful, announcing a game.
--- word ---
term: 말
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: speech, words
notes: 말 (mal) means 'speech' or 'words.' It is the noun component of the verb 말하다 (malhada), 'to speak.'
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is an adverb that negates the verb or adjective that follows it. It typically indicates a lack of will or a simple negative state.
--- word ---
term: 하기
type: Verb (nominalized form)
breakdown: 하다 + -기
meaning: doing
notes: The -기 suffix is attached to a verb stem to turn it into a noun, a process called nominalization. 하다 (hada, 'to do') becomes 하기 (hagi, 'the act of doing'). This form is often used in lists, titles, and signs.
--- word ---
term: 게임
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: game
notes: A loanword from the English word 'game'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -기
meaning: A grammar pattern that nominalizes a verb, turning it into a noun that refers to the act of doing that verb.
example: 책 읽기가 제 취미예요. (Reading books is my hobby.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:10:24] 자 ===
meaning: Okay...
tone: Transitional, getting ready to start something.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Okay / Alright / Now
notes: 자 (ja) is a very common interjection used to get someone's attention, to signal the start of an action, or to encourage someone. It's similar to saying 'Alright, then...' or 'Here we go...' in English.

=== [1:10:26] 시작 ===
meaning: Start.
tone: Declarative, command.
--- word ---
term: 시작
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: start, beginning
notes: 시작 (sijak) is a noun meaning 'start.' It is often used by itself as an exclamation or command to 'Begin!' or 'Go!'. The verb form is 시작하다 (sijakhada), 'to start'.

=== [1:10:29] 정신 차리자 ===
meaning: Let's get it together.
tone: Self-motivating, propositive.
--- word ---
term: 정신
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: mind, consciousness
notes: 정신 (jeongsin) refers to one's mind, spirit, or consciousness. It's a key component of many idiomatic expressions in Korean.
--- word ---
term: 차리자
type: Verb (propositive form)
breakdown: 차리다 + -자
meaning: let's pull (it) together
notes: This is the propositive form of the verb 차리다 (charida). The ending -자 (-ja) is used in informal speech (반말, banmal) to mean 'Let's...'. It's a suggestion made to a group that includes the speaker, or in this case, to oneself.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 정신(을) 차리다
meaning: An idiomatic expression meaning 'to pull oneself together,' 'to come to one's senses,' or 'to snap out of it.' It is used when someone is dazed, distracted, or not thinking clearly.
example: 피곤해도 정신 차리고 운전해야 돼. (Even if you're tired, you have to pull yourself together and drive.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -자
meaning: The informal propositive ending, used to make suggestions. It translates to 'Let's...'. This is used in 반말 (banmal), or casual speech, between close friends or to oneself.
example: 우리 밥 먹자. (Let's eat.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:10:58] 이제 정신 차려졌어 ===
meaning: I've come to my senses now.
tone: Informal, declarative. Recovering from a moment of drowsiness.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 means 'now' from this point forward, implying a change from the past. It's often contrasted with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this very moment'. Here, it signifies she wasn't clear-headed before, but is now.
--- word ---
term: 정신
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: mind, consciousness, senses
notes: A common word in many expressions. 정신(을) 차리다 (jeongsin(eul) charida) means 'to come to one's senses' or 'to pull oneself together'. 정신이 없다 (jeongsini eopda) means 'to be out of it' or 'to be hectic'.
--- word ---
term: 차려졌어
type: Verb
breakdown: 차려지다 + -었어
meaning: has been gathered/collected
notes: This is the past tense, informal conjugation of 차려지다 (charyeojida), the passive form of 차리다 (charida, 'to prepare, set up'). The full phrase 정신이 차려지다 (jeongsini charyeojida) is a passive way of saying 'one's mind becomes clear'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어지다
meaning: A passive voice construction attached to verb stems. It indicates that an action is done to the subject, rather than the subject performing the action. It can also imply a state that has come about naturally.
example: 문이 저절로 열어졌어요. (The door was opened by itself.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:11:11] 나 살짝 좀 헛소리 했지? ===
meaning: I said some nonsense just now, didn't I?
tone: Informal, self-aware. Asking her fans for confirmation.
--- word ---
term: 나
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: I, me (informal)
notes: The informal first-person pronoun. Used with friends, younger people, or in casual settings like this livestream. The formal equivalent is 저 (jeo).
--- word ---
term: 살짝
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: slightly, a little bit
notes: A very common colloquial adverb used to mean 'a little', 'lightly', or 'secretly'. It softens the action. Here, it downplays the 'nonsense' she might have said.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조금 -> 좀
meaning: a bit
notes: A contraction of 조금 (jogeum). While it means 'a little', it's frequently used in colloquial speech to make statements or requests sound softer and less direct, much like 'just' or 'kinda' in English.
--- word ---
term: 헛소리
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: nonsense, gibberish
notes: A compound word from 헛- (heot-, meaning 'empty' or 'futile') and 소리 (sori, 'sound'). 헛소리 하다 (heotsori hada) means 'to talk nonsense'.
--- word ---
term: 했지?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -었- + -지?
meaning: did, right?
notes: This is the past tense of 하다 (hada, 'to do') combined with the sentence ending -지? (-ji?). This ending is used to seek confirmation or agreement from the listener for something the speaker assumes is true.

=== [1:11:13] 갑자기 기억이 안 난다 ===
meaning: Suddenly, I can't remember.
tone: Informal, spontaneous. Expressing a sudden lapse in memory.
--- word ---
term: 갑자기
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: suddenly
notes: A common adverb used to describe an abrupt or unexpected event.
--- word ---
term: 기억이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 기억 + -이
meaning: memory (as the subject)
notes: 기억 (gieok) means 'memory'. The particle -이 (-i) marks it as the subject of the verb 나다 (nada).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: This is the most common way to negate verbs and adjectives in Korean. It is placed directly before the verb/adjective. Another way is to use the -지 않다 (-ji anta) ending.
--- word ---
term: 난다
type: Verb
breakdown: 나다 + -ㄴ다
meaning: occurs, comes out
notes: The verb 나다 (nada) has many meanings like 'to emerge' or 'to happen'. The phrase 기억이 나다 (gieogi nada) is a set expression meaning 'to remember' (literally, 'a memory comes out'). The -ㄴ다 (-nda) ending is the plain form, often used when talking to oneself, in diaries, or in casual speech to state a fact.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 기억이 나다 (gieogi nada)
meaning: The idiomatic expression for 'to remember'. The literal translation is 'a memory comes out'. The opposite, 'to forget', is 잊어버리다 (ijeobeorida) or 기억이 안 나다 (gieogi an nada).
example: 그 사람 이름이 기억이 안 나요. (I can't remember that person's name.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:11:20] 뭘 줬다 줬어요? ===
meaning: What was that about giving something? What was given?
tone: Informal, confused, questioning. The mix of speech styles shows her grogginess.
--- word ---
term: 뭘
type: Pronoun + Particle (Contraction)
breakdown: 무엇 + -을 -> 뭘
meaning: what (as object)
notes: 뭘 (mwol) is the contracted form of 무엇을 (mueoseul). It's very common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 줬다
type: Verb
breakdown: 주다 + -었- + -다
meaning: gave (plain form)
notes: This is the plain form, past tense of 주다 (juda, 'to give'). This form is typically used in writing or when talking to oneself. Its presence here is unusual.
--- word ---
term: 줬어요?
type: Verb
breakdown: 주다 + -었- + -어요?
meaning: gave? (polite informal form)
notes: This is the standard polite informal (해요체, haeyoche) past tense question form. Using both 줬다 and 줬어요? in one utterance is grammatically strange and highlights the speaker's confusion, as if she's thinking aloud ('gave...') and then forming a question to the audience ('...did I give?').

=== [1:11:21] 무슨 말 했어? ===
meaning: What did I say?
tone: Informal, direct question. Trying to get back on track.
--- word ---
term: 무슨
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: what, what kind of
notes: 무슨 (museun) is used before a noun to ask 'what kind of ~?'. It's different from 뭐 (mwo) which stands alone as 'what'. For example, 무슨 색? (museun saek? - What color?) vs. 이게 뭐예요? (ige mwoyeyo? - What is this?).
--- word ---
term: 말
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: words, speech, language
notes: The phrase 말을 하다 (mareul hada) means 'to speak' or 'to say something'.
--- word ---
term: 했어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -었어?
meaning: did?
notes: The informal, past tense question form of 하다 (hada, 'to do'). In the context of 말(을) 했어?, it means 'did you say?' or 'did I say?'.

=== [1:11:38] 되게 찰나의 순간인데 기억이 안 난다 ===
meaning: It was such a fleeting moment, but I can't remember.
tone: Informal, reflective, slightly frustrated.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really, so
notes: A colloquial adverb used to emphasize adjectives or other adverbs. It's very common in casual conversation, similar to 진짜 (jinjja) or 정말 (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 찰나의
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 찰나 + -의
meaning: of a moment, fleeting
notes: 찰나 (chalna) is a noun of Sino-Korean origin meaning 'an instant' or 'a moment'. The particle -의 (-ui) indicates possession, so 찰나의 순간 (chalnaui sungan) means 'a moment of an instant' or 'a fleeting moment'.
--- word ---
term: 순간인데
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 순간 + -이다 + -는데
meaning: it is a moment, but...
notes: The ending -ㄴ/는데 (-n/neunde) is used to connect two clauses. It provides background context for the following clause and often implies a contrast or unexpected result. Here, 'It was a moment, BUT I can't remember.'
--- word ---
term: 기억이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 기억 + -이
meaning: memory (as the subject)
notes: 기억 (gieok) means 'memory'. The particle -이 (-i) marks it as the subject of the verb 나다 (nada).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: Placed before a verb or adjective to negate it. For example, 안 가요 (an gayo) means 'I'm not going'.
--- word ---
term: 난다
type: Verb
breakdown: 나다 + -ㄴ다
meaning: occurs, comes out
notes: Part of the set phrase 기억이 나다 (gieogi nada), meaning 'to remember'. The -ㄴ다 (-nda) ending is a plain form declarative, often used when talking to oneself or stating a fact in a casual way.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -ㄴ/는데
meaning: A connective ending that links two clauses. It provides background information or sets the scene for the main clause. It can imply a soft contrast ('but'), a reason ('so'), or simply connect two related actions.
example: 비가 오는데 우리 뭐 할까? (It's raining, so what should we do?)
level: Beginner

=== [1:12:04] 어 이제 밖이 보여 ===
meaning: Oh, now I can see outside.
tone: Informal, observational. A sudden change of topic.
--- word ---
term: 어
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Oh, Ah
notes: A common interjection used to express mild surprise, realization, or when starting to speak.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: Indicates a change of state. Before, she couldn't see outside (it was foggy or she wasn't paying attention), but from this point forward, she can.
--- word ---
term: 밖이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 밖 + -이
meaning: the outside (as subject)
notes: 밖 (bak) means 'outside'. The particle -이 (-i) marks it as the subject of the verb 보여 (boyeo).
--- word ---
term: 보여
type: Verb
breakdown: 보이다 + -어
meaning: is seen, can be seen
notes: 보이다 (boida) is the passive form of 보다 (boda, 'to see'). It means 'to be seen' or 'to be visible'. Using 보이다 implies that the ability to see is not an active choice, but that the view has become available.

=== [1:12:05] 밖에는 보여줄게 ===
meaning: I'll show you the outside.
tone: Informal, promising. Directly addressing and engaging with her fans.
--- word ---
term: 밖에는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 밖 + -에는
meaning: as for the outside
notes: The combination of the location particle -에 (-e) and the topic particle -는 (-neun) adds emphasis or contrast. It implies 'As for the outside (which we were just talking about), I'll show you'.
--- word ---
term: 보여줄게
type: Verb
breakdown: 보여주다 + -ㄹ게
meaning: I will show you
notes: This combines two grammar points. 보여주다 (boyeojuda) is 보다 ('to see') + -어 주다 ('to do for someone'), so it means 'to show'. The ending -(으)ㄹ게 (-l/eulge) expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something. It's informal and can only be used with 'I' as the subject.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어 주다
meaning: Attached to a verb stem, this pattern indicates that the action is being performed for the benefit of someone else. It adds a sense of favor or service.
example: 문을 열어 주세요. (Please open the door for me.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: A sentence ending that expresses the speaker's intention or a promise to the listener. It is used in informal speech and only with a first-person subject ('I' or 'we'). It has a softer nuance than -(으)ㄹ 거야 (will do).
example: 내가 먼저 갈게. (I'll go first.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:12:10] 짜잔 ===
meaning: Ta-da!
tone: Playful, onomatopoeic, for a reveal.
--- word ---
term: 짜잔
type: onomatopoeia / interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ta-da!
notes: A common sound effect used in Korean when revealing or presenting something, similar to a drumroll or flourish. It's frequently used in broadcasts and daily life to add a playful effect.

=== [1:12:18] 내가 얻어졌지? ===
meaning: I've appeared, right?
tone: Informal, playful, self-referential.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + ~가
meaning: I
notes: 나 is the informal, plain form for "I," used with friends or those younger than you, as the speaker (Hyejin) does with her fans. ~가 is the subject marking particle, used after words ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 얻어졌지?
type: verb
breakdown: 얻어지다 + ~었~ + ~지?
meaning: was obtained, right?
notes: The literal meaning is "I was obtained, right?" which is grammatically unusual. 얻어지다 is the passive form of 얻다 (to get/obtain). In this context, it's a quirky, playful way of saying "Here I am!" or "I have appeared," as if she has been presented to the viewers. The ~지? ending seeks agreement, making the tone conversational.

=== [1:12:31] 근데 난 비가 오는 건 너무 좋은데 ===
meaning: But, while I do really like it when it rains...
tone: Informal, conversational, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: adverb/conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: But / However
notes: A very common contraction of 그런데 in spoken Korean. It's used to connect contrasting ideas or to change the subject, similar to "but" or "by the way."
--- word ---
term: 난
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나는 (contraction)
meaning: As for me / I
notes: A common contraction of the informal pronoun 나 (I) and the topic particle ~는. Using the topic particle here emphasizes "As for me..." in contrast to other potential opinions.
--- word ---
term: 비가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 비 + ~가
meaning: rain (as the subject)
notes: 비 means "rain." The particle ~가 marks it as the subject of the verb 오는 (coming/falling).
--- word ---
term: 오는
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 오다 + ~는
meaning: that is coming/falling
notes: This is the present tense adjective form of the verb 오다 (to come). In Korean, rain "comes" (비가 오다) rather than "rains." Here, it modifies the noun 건 (thing).
--- word ---
term: 건
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 것은 (contraction)
meaning: the thing that / the fact that
notes: 것 is a dependent noun meaning "thing" or "fact." The phrase 비가 오는 건 means "the fact that it's raining" or more naturally, "when it rains."
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / very / really
notes: While textbooks often teach 너무 as "too" (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean it's very frequently used to mean "very" or "really" in both positive and negative sentences.
--- word ---
term: 좋은데
type: adjective ending
breakdown: 좋다 + ~은데
meaning: I like it, but...
notes: The ~ㄴ/은데 ending is used to provide background information before making a suggestion, asking a question, or stating a contrasting idea. Here, it leaves the sentence hanging, implying she is about to state a problem or a contrasting thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 것
meaning: Turns a verb clause into a noun phrase, meaning "the act of V-ing" or "the fact that V." It allows you to use a whole action as the subject or object of a sentence.
example: 매일 운동하는 것은 건강에 좋아요. (Exercising every day is good for your health.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:12:34] 가끔씩 이런 ===
meaning: Sometimes, this kind of...
tone: Informal, introductory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 가끔씩
type: adverb
breakdown: 가끔 + ~씩
meaning: Sometimes / Occasionally
notes: 가끔 and 가끔씩 are mostly interchangeable. The suffix ~씩 can add a nuance of distribution or repetition, like "from time to time."
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: this kind of / like this
notes: 이런 modifies the following noun (which is unstated here as she pauses). It's formed from the adjective 이렇다 (to be like this). The three related forms are 이런 (like this), 그런 (like that, near the listener), and 저런 (like that, over there).

=== [1:13:34] 이런 높은 데서 ===
meaning: from a high place like this...
tone: Informal, descriptive, continuing a thought.
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: this kind of / like this
notes: Modifies the noun phrase 높은 데 (a high place). It refers to her current situation in a high-floor hotel room.
--- word ---
term: 높은
type: adjective modifier
breakdown: 높다 + ~은
meaning: high
notes: The adjective form of 높다 (to be high), used to modify the following noun 데 (place).
--- word ---
term: 데서
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 데 + 에서 (contraction)
meaning: from a place
notes: 데 is a dependent noun meaning "place" or "spot." ~에서 is a particle indicating location, which can mean "at," "in," or "from." Here, it means "from." 데서 is a common spoken contraction of 데에서.

=== [1:13:36] 떨어질 때 ===
meaning: when falling...
tone: Informal, setting up a scenario.
--- word ---
term: 떨어질
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 떨어지다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: (that will) fall
notes: This is the future-tense modifier form of 떨어지다 (to fall). It modifies the noun 때 (time).
--- word ---
term: 때
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: time / when
notes: A noun that means "time." It is very commonly used with a preceding verb modifier to mean "when [verb] happens."
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: A grammar pattern used to mean "when" or "at the time of" doing a future or potential action.
example: 한국에 갈 때 선물을 살 거예요. (When I go to Korea, I'm going to buy a gift.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:13:40] 이런 높은 데서 떨어지는 상상을 할 때가 있잖아 우리가 ===
meaning: You know, we sometimes have moments where we imagine falling from a high place like this.
tone: Informal, relatable, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 이런
type: determiner
breakdown: 이렇다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: this kind of
notes: Refers to the specific type of high place she is in (a hotel on the 43rd floor).
--- word ---
term: 높은
type: adjective modifier
breakdown: 높다 + ~은
meaning: high
notes: Adjective form of 높다 (to be high) modifying 데 (place).
--- word ---
term: 데서
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 데 + 에서
meaning: from a place
notes: A common spoken contraction of 데에서, indicating the starting point of the action (falling).
--- word ---
term: 떨어지는
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 떨어지다 + ~는
meaning: falling
notes: The present tense modifier form of 떨어지다 (to fall). It modifies the noun 상상 (imagination), creating the phrase "the imagination of falling."
--- word ---
term: 상상을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 상상 + ~을
meaning: imagination (as object)
notes: 상상 means "imagination." The particle ~을 marks it as the direct object of the verb 하다 (to do).
--- word ---
term: 할
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 하다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: to do / to have
notes: Future tense modifier form of 하다 (to do). 상상을 하다 means "to imagine." Here, 할 modifies 때 (time).
--- word ---
term: 때가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 때 + ~가
meaning: a time / moment
notes: The noun 때 (time) is marked by the subject particle ~가, making it the subject of the verb 있다 (to exist).
--- word ---
term: 있잖아
type: verb ending
breakdown: 있다 + ~잖아
meaning: there is, you know
notes: A very common colloquial ending used to state something the speaker assumes the listener already knows or would agree with. It creates a friendly, inclusive tone. It's the informal (반말) version of 있잖아요.
--- word ---
term: 우리가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 우리 + ~가
meaning: we
notes: 우리 means "we" or "us." Placing the subject at the end of the sentence is a common feature of spoken Korean, often for emphasis or as an afterthought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 때가 있다/없다
meaning: Expresses that one "sometimes does V" or "there are times when one does V." The opposite, ~(으)ㄹ 때가 없다, means "one never does V."
example: 가끔씩 너무 피곤해서 저녁을 못 먹을 때가 있어요. (Sometimes I'm so tired there are times I can't eat dinner.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~잖아(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to remind the listener of a shared piece of information or to state an obvious fact. It translates to "...you know," or "as you know..."
example: 오늘 일요일이잖아. (It's Sunday today, you know.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:13:47] 뭐가? ===
meaning: What is?
tone: Informal, questioning, seeking clarification.
--- word ---
term: 뭐가?
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 뭐 + ~가
meaning: What?
notes: 뭐 is the common contraction of 무엇 (what). The subject particle ~가 is attached, so the question specifically asks "What (is the subject)?" It's used to ask for clarification about the subject of a previous statement.

=== [1:13:59] 떨어지는 상상을 할 때 ===
meaning: When I imagine falling...
tone: Informal, descriptive, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 떨어지는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 떨어지다 + -는
meaning: falling
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of 떨어지다 (to fall). The ending -는 turns the verb into a modifier for the following noun, 상상 (imagination). It describes an action that is currently happening or a general state. For example, '가는 사람' means 'the person who is going'.
--- word ---
term: 상상을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 상상 + -을
meaning: imagination (as an object)
notes: 상상 means 'imagination'. The particle -을 marks it as the direct object of the verb. The full phrase is 상상을 하다 (to imagine), which literally means 'to do an imagination'.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 하다 + -ㄹ
meaning: doing
notes: This is the future adjectival form of the verb 하다 (to do). It modifies the noun 때 (time). The -ㄹ/을 ending is used to describe a future action or a potential/intended action related to the noun.
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: when / at the time of
notes: 때 is a noun meaning 'time' or 'moment'. It is very commonly used with a verb modifier to create a clause meaning 'when...'. For example, '공부할 때' means 'when studying'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time 'when' an action takes place. It attaches to a verb stem.
example: 밥을 먹을 때 TV를 봐요. (I watch TV when I eat.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:14:03] 너무 실감나게 상상해서 ===
meaning: Because I imagine it so realistically...
tone: Informal, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / too / very
notes: 너무 is a very common adverb of degree. While it traditionally meant 'too' (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean, it's frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so', similar to 정말 or 아주. Its usage here is very natural for casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 실감나게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 실감나다 + -게
meaning: realistically / vividly
notes: The verb 실감나다 means 'to feel real' or 'to be realistic'. The -게 ending is attached to a verb or adjective stem to turn it into an adverb, describing how an action is performed. So, 실감나게 means 'in a way that feels real'.
--- word ---
term: 상상해서
type: Verb
breakdown: 상상하다 + -아/어서
meaning: because I imagine / I imagine and so...
notes: The -아/어서 ending has two main uses: indicating a cause/reason ('because') or indicating a sequence of events ('and then'). Here, it clearly states the reason for the feeling she is about to describe. The speaker is using 반말 (banmal), the informal speech style.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj + -아/어서
meaning: Connective ending indicating reason or cause. The clause before -아/어서 is the reason for the clause that follows.
example: 비가 와서 집에 있었어요. (Because it rained, I stayed home.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:14:06] 왠지 내가 ===
meaning: For some reason, I...
tone: Informal, hesitant, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 왠지
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: for some reason / somehow
notes: 왠지 is a contraction of 왜인지 (literally 'why it is'). It's used when you have a certain feeling or thought but aren't sure of the exact reason. It's very common in conversational Korean.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + -가
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: 내가 is the informal way to say 'I' when it is the subject of the sentence. It combines the informal pronoun 나 (I) with the subject marker -가. This is different from 나는 (I, as the topic), which would be used to introduce or contrast the topic of 'I'.

=== [1:14:10] 그 ===
meaning: That...
tone: Informal, filler word.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: Determiner / Filler
breakdown: 
meaning: that / um...
notes: While 그 means 'that' (referring to something away from the speaker but known to the listener), it's also frequently used as a filler word, similar to 'um' or 'uh' in English, when the speaker is pausing to think of what to say next.

=== [1:14:11] 아 이거 뭐라고 설명해야 되지? ===
meaning: Ah, how should I explain this?
tone: Informal, self-questioning, trying to find words.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common interjection used to express realization, surprise, or when starting a new thought.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것 (contraction)
meaning: this thing / this
notes: 이거 is the colloquial contraction of 이것 (this thing). It's very common in spoken Korean. The three core demonstrative pronouns are 이것 (this), 그것 (that), and 저것 (that over there).
--- word ---
term: 뭐라고
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 뭐 + -라고
meaning: as what / saying what
notes: 뭐 means 'what' and -(이)라고 is a particle used for quoting nouns. Here, '뭐라고 설명하다' literally means 'to explain saying what?', which translates naturally to 'how should I explain?'.
--- word ---
term: 설명해야
type: Verb
breakdown: 설명하다 + -아/어야 하다
meaning: (I) have to explain
notes: This is part of the V-아/어야 하다/되다 grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity ('must' or 'have to'). Here, the 하다/되다 part is implied in the following 되지?.
--- word ---
term: 되지?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -지?
meaning: should it be? / right?
notes: The -지(요)? ending is a tag question, used to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener, or sometimes asked to oneself rhetorically. Here, '설명해야 되지?' is a common phrase for 'How should I explain this?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to' in English.
example: 지금 가야 돼요. (I have to go now.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:14:13] 뭔 느낌인지 알아? ===
meaning: Do you know what kind of feeling it is?
tone: Informal, directly addressing the audience.
--- word ---
term: 뭔
type: Determiner
breakdown: 무슨 (contraction)
meaning: what / what kind of
notes: 뭔 is the spoken, colloquial contraction of 무슨. It's used before a noun to ask about its nature or identity. Very common in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 느낌인지
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 느낌 + -이다 + -ㄴ지
meaning: what feeling it is
notes: This construction embeds a question into a larger sentence. 느낌 (feeling) + -인지 combines the noun with the verb 'to be' (이다) and the ending -ㄴ/은지, which means 'whether' or 'if', and is used here to embed the question 'what feeling is it?'.
--- word ---
term: 알아?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -아?
meaning: Do you know?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite question form (반말, banmal) of the verb 알다 (to know). The speaker, Hyejin, uses this casual style when talking to her fans, the 'Cheong-i'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)ㄴ지 알다/모르다
meaning: Used to say you know/don't know 'what [Noun] is'. It embeds a 'who/what/where/when/why/how' question about a noun.
example: 그 사람이 누구인지 알아요? (Do you know who that person is?)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:14:15] 뭔 느낌인지 아시는 분? ===
meaning: Anyone know what feeling it is?
tone: Informal, polling the audience.
--- word ---
term: 뭔
type: Determiner
breakdown: 무슨 (contraction)
meaning: what / what kind of
notes: The colloquial contraction of 무슨 (what kind of). Used frequently in spoken Korean to ask about the nature of a noun.
--- word ---
term: 느낌인지
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 느낌 + -이다 + -ㄴ지
meaning: what feeling it is
notes: This pattern embeds a question within a larger clause. It's formed by adding -인지 to a noun, and is often followed by verbs like 알다 (to know) or 모르다 (to not know).
--- word ---
term: 아시는
type: Verb (honorific adjective form)
breakdown: 알다 + -시- + -는
meaning: (a person) who knows
notes: This form includes the honorific marker -시-, which shows respect to the subject of the verb—in this case, her fans. Even while speaking in overall informal 반말 (banmal), it's common to use honorifics like this when addressing an audience.
--- word ---
term: 분?
type: Noun (honorific)
breakdown: 
meaning: person?
notes: 분 is the honorific word for 'person', used instead of 사람 to show respect. The sentence is a fragment, short for '아시는 분 계세요?' (Is there anyone who knows?). This is a common way to poll a group.

=== [1:14:19] 너무 실감나게 ===
meaning: So realistically...
tone: Informal, re-emphasizing, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / too / very
notes: In modern spoken Korean, 너무 is widely used to mean 'very' or 'so', not just its traditional meaning of 'too' (in a negative sense). It adds emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 실감나게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 실감나다 + -게
meaning: realistically / vividly
notes: The -게 ending attaches to adjective and verb stems to create an adverb, describing how an action is done. 실감나다 means 'to feel real', so 실감나게 means 'in a realistic way'.

=== [1:14:25] 상상을 해가지고 ===
meaning: After imagining...
tone: Informal, trailing off, starting a thought.
--- word ---
term: 상상을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 상상 (imagination) + -을 (object particle)
meaning: imagination (as object)
notes: 상상 (sangsang) means 'imagination'. The particle -을 marks it as the direct object of the verb that follows. The final consonant in 상상 requires -을 instead of -를.
--- word ---
term: 해가지고
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + -아/어 가지고
meaning: by doing and then...
notes: This is the verb 하다 (hada, 'to do') conjugated with the -아/어 가지고 grammar pattern. It connects clauses to show sequence ('do X and then Y') or reason ('because of doing X'). Here, the sentence trails off, implying 'After imagining things... (something happened)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -아/어 가지고
meaning: A connective pattern indicating sequence (do A, and then B) or reason (because of A, B happened). It's very common in spoken Korean and is slightly more colloquial than the similar pattern -아/어서.
example: 돈을 모아가지고 차를 샀어요. (I saved money and then bought a car.)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:14:28] 막 ===
meaning: Just...
tone: Informal, filler word, adding emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just / intensely / recklessly
notes: 막 (mak) is a versatile adverb often used in colloquial speech. It can mean 'recklessly' or 'carelessly' (e.g., 막 먹다 - to eat recklessly), but here it's used as a filler word to add emphasis, similar to saying 'just' or 'like' in English before describing something intense or overwhelming.

=== [1:14:29] 막 ===
meaning: Just...

--- word ---
term: 막
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just / intensely / recklessly
notes: The speaker repeats 막 (mak) as a verbal tic or for emphasis, highlighting her unstructured train of thought. This kind of repetition is very common in casual, spontaneous speech.

=== [1:14:36] 모르겠어 ===
meaning: I don't know.
tone: Informal, uncertain, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠어
type: verb
breakdown: 모르다 (to not know) + -겠- + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: I don't know / I'm not sure
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) form. The infix -겠- (get) here expresses the speaker's current thought or feeling, softening the statement. It feels less blunt than just 모르다, conveying a sense of 'I guess I don't know' or 'I just can't figure it out'.

=== [1:14:46] 왜 잘 준비를 하는 걸까? ===
meaning: Why am I getting ready for bed?
tone: Informal, self-questioning, rhetorical.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: why
notes: 왜 (wae) is the standard word for 'why' and is used to form questions.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: verb
breakdown: 자다 (to sleep) + -ㄹ (future modifier)
meaning: to sleep
notes: In the phrase '잘 준비' (jal junbi), 잘 is a modified form of the verb 자다 (jada, 'to sleep'). It acts like an adjective describing 준비 (junbi, 'preparation'), meaning 'preparations for sleeping'.
--- word ---
term: 준비를
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 준비 (preparation) + -를 (object particle)
meaning: preparations
notes: 준비 (junbi) means 'preparation'. The phrase 준비를 하다 (junbireul hada) means 'to prepare' or 'to get ready'.
--- word ---
term: 하는
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + -는 (present tense modifier)
meaning: that is doing
notes: This turns the verb 하다 (hada, 'to do') into a descriptive form that can modify a noun. Here, it modifies the following word, 걸 (geol).
--- word ---
term: 걸까?
type: noun + verb ending
breakdown: 것 (thing) + -일까? (I wonder if...?)
meaning: I wonder why...?
notes: 걸 (geol) is a common contraction of 것 (geot, 'thing') + 을 (eul, object particle). The ending -ㄹ까? (-lkka?) is used to ask a question to oneself or to make a suggestion. The full pattern -는 걸까? expresses wondering or pondering about a present action.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ까?
meaning: A sentence ending used to express supposition, ask a question to oneself ('I wonder...?'), or make a suggestion to others ('Shall we...?').
example: 비가 올까? (I wonder if it will rain?)
level: Beginner

=== [1:14:48] 아 안 되는데 ===
meaning: Oh, this can't be happening...
tone: Informal, mild protest, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common interjection used to express surprise, realization, or frustration.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 (an) is a short-form adverb used to negate verbs and adjectives. It is placed directly before the word it negates. It's more common in spoken Korean than the longer -지 않다 form.
--- word ---
term: 되는데
type: verb
breakdown: 되다 (to be okay/possible) + -는데
meaning: it shouldn't, but...
notes: 안 되다 (an doeda) means 'to not be okay,' 'to be forbidden,' or 'it can't be.' The ending -는데 (-neunde) provides background context or shows a contrast. By ending the sentence here, the speaker implies a trailing thought like, 'This shouldn't be happening, but it is...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -는데/은데/ㄴ데
meaning: A connective ending used to provide background information, show contrast, or express surprise. When used to end a sentence, it creates a trailing-off effect, inviting a response or leaving a thought unfinished.
example: 날씨가 좋은데... (The weather is nice, but... [let's do something? / why are we inside?])
level: Beginner

=== [1:15:29] 이제 저는 ===
meaning: Now, as for me...
tone: Informal, transitional, slightly formal pronoun choice.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now,' but often with a sense of a change from the past. It implies 'from this point forward.' It's often contrasted with 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' at this exact moment.
--- word ---
term: 저는
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 저 (I, humble) + -는 (topic particle)
meaning: I
notes: 저 (jeo) is the humble form of 'I', typically used in formal speech (존댓말). The speaker is using informal speech (반말) with her fans, where 나 (na) is the expected pronoun. Using 저 here is a common habit, sometimes used to sound slightly more declarative or polite when starting a new topic, even if the verb endings remain informal.

=== [1:15:30] 저녁 잘 보내고 ===
meaning: Have a good evening and...
tone: Informal, friendly, addressing fans, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 저녁
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: evening / dinner
notes: 저녁 (jeonyeok) can mean both 'the evening' and the 'evening meal' (dinner).
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: 잘 (jal) is an adverb meaning 'well' or 'good'. The phrase 잘 보내다 (jal bonaeda) is a common expression meaning 'to have a good time' or 'to spend time well'.
--- word ---
term: 보내고
type: verb
breakdown: 보내다 (to spend time) + -고 (and)
meaning: spend time and...
notes: This is a command or suggestion to her fans. The full phrase would be something like 저녁 잘 보내세요 (jeonyeok jal bonaeseyo, 'Have a good evening'). By ending with -고 (-go), she's connecting this well-wish to another thought that she leaves unsaid.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -고
meaning: A connective particle that links verbs or clauses, most commonly meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It lists actions in sequence.
example: 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요. (I ate and then watched a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:16:01] 잘 먹고 ===
meaning: Eat well and...
tone: Informal, instructional, part of a list.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: A very common adverb placed before a verb to indicate that the action is performed well. For example, 잘 자요 (jal jayo) means 'Sleep well'.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: eat and...
notes: The verb stem 먹다 (meokda, 'to eat') is combined with the connective ending -고 (-go). This ending links clauses, often in a sequence (A and then B) or simply lists actions. It's a fundamental pattern for connecting sentences.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -고
meaning: Connects two verbs or clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. It lists actions or shows a sequence.
example: 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요. (Babeul meokgo yeonghwareul bwasseoyo.) - I ate and then watched a movie.
level: Beginner

=== [1:16:02] 잘 듣고 ===
meaning: Listen well and...
tone: Informal, instructional, continuing a list.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: An adverb that modifies the following verb, indicating the action is done well. It's one of the most frequently used adverbs in Korean.
--- word ---
term: 듣고
type: Verb
breakdown: 듣다 + -고
meaning: listen and...
notes: 듣다 (deutda, 'to listen') is a 'ㄷ' irregular verb. The 'ㄷ' changes to 'ㄹ' before a vowel (e.g., 들어요, deureoyo). However, before a consonant like in the -고 (-go) ending, the stem remains unchanged.

=== [1:16:06] 알았지? ===
meaning: Got it?
tone: Informal, seeking confirmation.
--- word ---
term: 알았지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -았- + -지?
meaning: You got it, right?
notes: A very common phrase in casual speech (반말, banmal). It combines 알다 (alda, 'to know'), the past tense infix -았- (-at-), and the question ending -지? (-ji?). It's used to confirm something the speaker assumes the listener already knows or agrees with. It's softer and more conversational than a direct question like 알았어? (arasseo?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -지(요)?
meaning: A question ending used to seek confirmation or agreement about something the speaker believes is true. It translates to '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?'.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (Oneul nalssi jochi?) - The weather is nice today, right?
level: Beginner

=== [1:16:13] 여긴데 ===
meaning: This is the place, but/and...
tone: Informal, introductory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 여긴데
type: Pronoun + Copula
breakdown: 여기 + -이다 + -ㄴ데
meaning: It's here, but/and...
notes: This is a contraction of 여기인데 (yeogiinde). 여기 (yeogi) means 'here'. The -ㄴ데 (-nde) ending is extremely versatile. It can provide background context for a following statement, show a soft contrast ('but'), or be used to trail off a sentence, as seen here. The speaker is likely introducing the view from her hotel room.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + -인데 / V/A + -(으)ㄴ/는데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background information for the next clause. It can mean 'and' or 'but' depending on context, or simply set the scene.
example: 비가 오는데 우리 뭐 할까? (Biga oneunde uri mwo halkka?) - It's raining, so what should we do?
level: Intermediate

=== [1:16:30] 좋은 것도 많이 보고 ===
meaning: See lots of good things, too, and...
tone: Informal, encouraging, part of a list of well-wishes.
--- word ---
term: 좋은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 + -은
meaning: good
notes: The adjective 좋다 (jota, 'to be good') is conjugated with the -은 (-eun) ending to modify the following noun. This is the standard way to turn descriptive verbs (adjectives) into noun modifiers.
--- word ---
term: 것도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + -도
meaning: thing too
notes: 것 (geot) is a dependent noun meaning 'thing' or 'stuff'. The particle -도 (-do) means 'also' or 'too'. It attaches to a noun and often replaces subject (이/가) or object (을/를) particles.
--- word ---
term: 많이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 많다 + -이
meaning: a lot, many
notes: This adverb is formed from the adjective 많다 (manta, 'to be many/much'). It's used to quantify verbs, meaning 'to do (verb) a lot'.
--- word ---
term: 보고
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 + -고
meaning: see and...
notes: The verb 보다 (boda, 'to see/watch') is connected to a following clause with the -고 (-go) ending, continuing the list of actions she is suggesting to her fans.

=== [1:16:52] 조식을 한번 먹겠지만 ===
meaning: I'll probably eat the hotel breakfast once, but...
tone: Informal, speculative, providing context.
--- word ---
term: 조식을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 조식 + -을
meaning: breakfast (as an object)
notes: 조식 (josik) is a Sino-Korean word for 'breakfast', often used in more formal settings like hotels. The common everyday word is 아침 (achim). The particle -을 (-eul) marks it as the object of the verb.
--- word ---
term: 한번
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: once, one time
notes: Literally 'one time'. It can also be used with the pattern '-아/어 보다' to mean 'to try doing something'. Here, it simply means she will eat it one time.
--- word ---
term: 먹겠지만
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -겠- + -지만
meaning: I will probably eat, but...
notes: This combines two grammar points. -겠- (-get-) expresses the speaker's intention or supposition ('I will', 'I guess'). -지만 (-jiman) is a connective ending meaning 'but' or 'although', showing contrast with the next clause.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -겠지만
meaning: Expresses an assumption or intention about a fact, but introduces a contrasting idea. 'It may be..., but...'; 'I will..., but...'.
example: 어렵겠지만 포기하지 마세요. (Eoryeopgetjiman pogihaji maseyo.) - It will probably be difficult, but don't give up.
level: Intermediate

=== [1:17:03] 가볼게 ===
meaning: I'm off now.
tone: Informal, signaling departure.
--- word ---
term: 가볼게
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 + -아 보다 + -ㄹ게
meaning: I'll go and see / I'll be going
notes: This combines two patterns. First, V + -아/어 보다 (-a/eo boda) means 'to try doing V'. So 가보다 (gaboda) is 'to try going'. Second, the ending -ㄹ게 (-lge) is used in informal speech (반말, banmal) to state the speaker's intention or promise, often when it's relevant to the listener. It's a very common and natural way to say 'I'm leaving now'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아/어 보다
meaning: Indicates trying or experiencing something. It adds a nuance of 'giving it a shot'.
example: 이 옷을 입어 보세요. (I oseul ibeo boseyo.) - Please try on these clothes.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: An informal ending used to express the speaker's intention or promise to do something. It implies the action is being taken with the listener in mind.
example: 내가 먼저 갈게. (Naega meonjeo galge.) - I'll go first.
level: Intermediate

=== [1:17:06] 안녕 ===

--- word ---
term: 안녕

breakdown: 
meaning: Bye
notes: A versatile informal greeting (반말, banmal) used between close friends. It can mean both 'Hello' and 'Goodbye'. In this context, following '가볼게' (I'm leaving), it clearly means 'Bye'.

=== [1:17:07] 너희들 모두 ===
meaning: All of you.
tone: Informal, addressing the audience directly.
--- word ---
term: 너희들
type: pronoun
breakdown: 너희 + 들
meaning: you (plural)
notes: 너희 is the informal, non-polite way to say 'you' (plural), used with people of the same age or younger. Adding the plural marker 들 is common in spoken Korean for emphasis, even though 너희 is already plural. The polite equivalent is 여러분.
--- word ---
term: 모두
type: adverb/pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: all, everyone

=== [1:17:10] 보통 호텔 조식은 6시 반부터야? ===
meaning: Is hotel breakfast usually from 6:30?
tone: Informal, curious, asking a general question.
--- word ---
term: 보통
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: usually, normally
notes: A common adverb used to describe what is typical or average. It's often placed at the beginning of a sentence. For example, '보통 주말에 뭐 해요?' (What do you usually do on weekends?).
--- word ---
term: 호텔
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: hotel
notes: A loanword from English, pronounced 'ho-tel'.
--- word ---
term: 조식은
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 조식 + 은
meaning: as for breakfast
notes: 조식 (朝食) is a Sino-Korean word for 'breakfast', often used in more formal settings like hotels or restaurants. The common everyday word is 아침 or 아침밥. The particle 은 marks 'breakfast' as the topic of the question.
--- word ---
term: 6시
type: noun
breakdown: 여섯 + 시
meaning: 6 o'clock
notes: Korean uses two number systems. For telling time, native Korean numbers (하나, 둘, 여섯) are used for the hour (시), while Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼) are used for minutes (분) and seconds (초).
--- word ---
term: 반부터야?
type: noun + particle + verb ending
breakdown: 반 + 부터 + 이야?
meaning: is it from half past?
notes: 반 means 'half' and is used after the hour to mean 'half past'. 부터 is a particle meaning 'from'. The ending -야? is the informal, non-polite question form of the verb 이다 (to be). The full form is 부터 + 이야?.

=== [1:17:24] 난 안 잘 거야 ===

tone: Informal, declarative, slightly defiant.

term: 난

type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A negation adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective to make it negative. This is the 'short form' of negation. The 'long form' would be 자지 않을 거야.

term: 잘
type: verb stem
breakdown: 자다
meaning: sleep
notes: The stem of the verb 자다 (to sleep).

breakdown: -(으)ㄹ 것 + 이야
meaning: am going to
notes: This is the informal, non-polite future tense ending -(으)ㄹ 거야, expressing intention or a future plan. It's a contraction of -(으)ㄹ 것이야. The polite equivalent is -(으)ㄹ 거예요.

meaning: Short-form negation. A simple way to make a sentence negative by placing '안' before the verb or adjective.

--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)ㄹ 거야

example: 내일 영화 볼 거야. (I'm going to watch a movie tomorrow.)

=== [1:18:00] 그 밤 칩은 꼭 사 ===
meaning: You have to buy those chestnut chips.
tone: Informal, emphatic, strong recommendation.
--- word ---
term: 그
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: 그 (geu) refers to something that is far from the speaker but close to or known by the listener. It's also used for something previously mentioned in conversation. It contrasts with 이 (i), 'this' (close to speaker), and 저 (jeo), 'that over there' (far from both).
--- word ---
term: 밤
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: chestnut
notes: 밤 (bam) means 'chestnut'. Be careful not to confuse it with the homonym 밤 (bam) which means 'night'.
--- word ---
term: 칩은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 칩 + 은
meaning: the chips (as for)
notes: The topic particle 은/는 (eun/neun) is used here to emphasize 'the chestnut chips' as the main topic, setting them apart from other snacks. It implies a contrast: 'As for *these* chips (unlike others), you must buy them.'
--- word ---
term: 꼭
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: definitely, surely
notes: 꼭 (kkok) is an adverb that adds strong emphasis, meaning 'for sure' or 'must'. It's often used with commands or statements of intention, like '꼭 오세요' (Please make sure to come).
--- word ---
term: 사
type: Verb
breakdown: 사다
meaning: buy
notes: This is the informal, non-polite imperative form of 사다 (sada), 'to buy'. It's formed by dropping the -다 and adding -아/어. This is a form of 반말 (banmal), casual speech used with close friends, which Hyejin uses with her fans.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 꼭 + Verb
meaning: Used to strongly emphasize that an action must be done. It translates to 'make sure to...', 'definitely...', or 'you must...'.
example: 내일 꼭 전화해. (Make sure to call me tomorrow.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:18:02] 알았지? ===

=== [1:18:16] 우주 1등 ===

term: 우주
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: universe / space
notes: 우주 (uju) means 'universe'. It's often used hyperbolically in slang to mean 'the absolute best' or 'on a cosmic level', as in 우주 대스타 (uju daeseuta), 'universe-level superstar'.

term: 1등
type: Noun

meaning: first place / number one
notes: 1등 (ildeung) combines the Sino-Korean number 일 (il, one) with 등 (deung), a counter for rank or grade. It literally means '1st rank'. It's commonly used to say something is 'the best'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: (Noun) + 1등
meaning: A common hyperbolic expression used to declare something as the absolute best or 'number one' in its category.
example: 맛은 여기가 1등이야. (When it comes to taste, this place is number one.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:18:32] 벨소리 켜두고 잘게 ===
meaning: I'll go to sleep with my ringer on.
tone: Informal, declarative. Stating her plan to her fans.
--- word ---
term: 벨소리
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: ringer sound
notes: A compound noun made from 'bell' (벨) and 'sori' (소리), meaning 'sound'. It specifically refers to the sound a phone makes for calls or notifications.
--- word ---
term: 켜두고
type: Verb
breakdown: 켜다 + -어/아 두다 + -고
meaning: turn on and leave it that way, and...
notes: The verb 켜다 means 'to turn on'. The grammar pattern -어/아 두다 indicates doing an action and maintaining that state for future convenience. The -고 ending connects this action to the next one.
--- word ---
term: 잘게
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I'll sleep
notes: The -(으)ㄹ게 ending is used in informal speech (반말) to express the speaker's intention or promise to do something. It conveys a sense of personal commitment to the listener. For example, '내가 할게' (naega halge) means 'I'll do it'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -어/아 두다
meaning: Indicates performing an action in advance or for future use, and maintaining that state. It implies preparation.
example: 창문을 열어 두세요. (Changmun-eul yeoreo duseyo.) - Please open the window (and leave it open).
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or a promise to the listener. It's used in the first person. The informal form is -(으)ㄹ게 and the polite form is -(으)ㄹ게요.
example: 먼저 갈게. (Meonjeo galge.) - I'll go first.
level: Beginner

=== [1:18:35] 안녕 ===
meaning: Bye.
tone: Informal, closing.
--- word ---
term: 안녕
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Bye
notes: A versatile informal greeting that can mean both 'hello' and 'goodbye'. The context determines the meaning. Since she is ending the livestream, it means 'bye' here. The formal equivalent for leaving is '안녕히 계세요' (annyeonghi gyeseyo - stay well) or '안녕히 가세요' (annyeonghi gaseyo - go well).

=== [1:18:36] 또 잘 거야 너희들도? ===
meaning: Are you guys going to sleep again too?
tone: Informal, inquisitive. Directly addressing her fans.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: An adverb meaning 'again' or 'also'. It's placed before the verb it modifies. For example, '또 봐요' (tto bwayo) means 'See you again'.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Verb Stem
breakdown: 자다
meaning: sleep
notes: This is the stem of the verb 자다 (jada), meaning 'to sleep', before the future tense ending is added.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: -(으)ㄹ 것이다
meaning: are going to
notes: This is the informal question form of -(으)ㄹ 것이다, which expresses future tense or intention. 거야 is a contraction of '것이야'. The rising intonation in speech makes it a question.
--- word ---
term: 너희들도?
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 너희들 + -도
meaning: you all too?
notes: 너희들 (neohuideul) is the informal plural 'you'. The particle -도 (do) means 'too' or 'also'. This is a very casual way to address a group of close friends or younger people, fitting for an idol speaking to her fans.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄹ 거야?
meaning: An informal (반말) way to ask about a future action or someone's intention. It's the question form of the future tense statement -(으)ㄹ 거야.
example: 점심 먹을 거야? (Jeomsim meogeul geoya?) - Are you going to eat lunch?
level: Beginner

=== [1:18:38] 또 바로 잘 거야? ===
meaning: Are you going to sleep right away again?
tone: Informal, inquisitive.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: An adverb meaning 'again' or 'also'. It's placed before the verb it modifies.
--- word ---
term: 바로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: right away
notes: An adverb meaning 'immediately' or 'right away'. It can also mean 'directly' or 'straight'. For example, '바로 여기' (baro yeogi) means 'right here'.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Verb Stem
breakdown: 자다
meaning: sleep
notes: The stem of the verb 자다 (jada), meaning 'to sleep'.
--- word ---
term: 거야?
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: -(으)ㄹ 것이다
meaning: are you going to...?
notes: The informal question form of the future tense ending -(으)ㄹ 것이다. The rising intonation makes it a question. It's a contraction of '것이야?'.

=== [1:18:49] 러브 유 ===

term: 러브
type: Loanword (Noun)
breakdown: 
meaning: Love
notes: This is the English word 'love' written in Hangeul (Korean alphabet). It's an example of Konglish (Korean-style English).

breakdown: 
meaning: You
notes: This is the English word 'you' written in Hangeul. Combining '러브' and '유' is a common, cute way to say 'I love you' in a casual setting.

=== [1:18:50] 같이 다시 자자 ===
meaning: Let's go back to sleep together.
tone: Informal, suggestive, friendly.
--- word ---
term: 같이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: together
notes: This adverb means 'together'. Note that its pronunciation is [가치, gachi], not [가티, gati]. It is often used with the particle -(와/과) or -(이)랑, as in '친구랑 같이' (chingurang gachi) - 'together with a friend'.
--- word ---
term: 다시
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: An adverb meaning 'again' or 'once more'. It's similar to 또 (tto), but 다시 often implies repeating the same action, while 또 can mean 'again' or 'also/in addition'.
--- word ---
term: 자자
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 + -자
meaning: let's sleep
notes: The -자 ending is attached to a verb stem to make an informal proposal, like 'Let's...'. It's used with friends and people younger than you. The polite equivalent is -(으)ㅂ시다.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -자
meaning: The informal propositive ending, meaning 'Let's...'. It's used to make suggestions to friends or people of equal or lower status.
example: 영화 보자. (Yeonghwa boja.) - Let's watch a movie.
level: Beginner

=== [1:18:58] 이상한 라이브였다 오늘 정말 ===
meaning: It was a strange live (broadcast) today, really.
tone: Informal, reflective, humorous.
--- word ---
term: 이상한
type: Adjective
breakdown: 이상하다 + -ㄴ
meaning: strange
notes: This is the adjective 이상하다 (isanghada - to be strange) in a form that can modify a noun. The -ㄴ ending is attached to adjective stems ending in a vowel to turn them into noun modifiers.
--- word ---
term: 라이브였다
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 라이브 + -였다
meaning: was a live (broadcast)
notes: 라이브 (raibeu) is a loanword from 'live'. -였다 is the informal past tense of the copula 이다 (ida - to be). It's used after nouns ending in a vowel. For nouns ending in a consonant, -이었다 is used.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: This word can function as both a noun ('Today is my birthday') and an adverb ('I'm busy today'). Here, it functions as an adverbial noun specifying the time.
--- word ---
term: 정말
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really
notes: An adverb meaning 'really' or 'truly'. It's very common in spoken Korean and is often used interchangeably with 진짜 (jinjja). 정말 can feel slightly more formal than 진짜, but both are used in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective Stem + -(으)ㄴ + Noun
meaning: This pattern is used to make an adjective describe a noun that follows it. -ㄴ is used for stems ending in a vowel (like 예쁘다 -> 예쁜), and -은 is used for stems ending in a consonant (like 작다 -> 작은).
example: 재미있는 영화 (jaemiinneun yeonghwa) - an interesting movie. (Note: 있다/없다 use -는).
level: Beginner

term: 안녕

breakdown: 
meaning: Bye
